### A.21. Executive summary

# Context and Rationale:
Addressing the accelerating degradation of rangeland ecosystems is the most urgent climate change priority for the people of Botswana. More than 70% of household income in the country's rural areas comes from farming, which in Botswana relies heavily on rain-fed agriculture; consequently, productivity has been declining over time and has been called into question by scientists and policymakers for more than a thousand years, and this culturally and economically critical activity is under existential threat from the impacts of climate change. As climate stressors increase, pressures on groundwater resources are also increasing, with borehole yields falling by 12% in recent decades, reducing economic income, creating a cascade of negative economic impact and vulnerability for the country, particularly in rural geographies.

The Government of Botswana recognizes the reliance of its population on rangelands, and through the National Development Plan, has identified international change in the way its rangelands are managed to sustainably support the socioeconomic well-being and climate resilience of its people, as well as the health of its ecosystems, and to reduce national GHG emissions.

Botswana's rangelands and the people dependent on these ecosystems have historically been extremely vulnerable to current and projected changes in climate. Climate change and poor management practices are already leading to a decline in livestock assets, and further climate impacts may trigger a collapse into chronic poverty for rural populations. While there has always been natural variation in the occurrence of drought and severe storms, anthropogenic (climate) change is now being recognized as a major driver of these events. The frequency of extreme events by 18% since 1980; 2019 was the hottest year on record for Botswana with the worst drought in decades. Lake Ngami dried completely for the first time in nearly 40 years, and thousands of livestock died as a result.

Climate model projections show that higher temperatures, longer dry seasons, and increased frequency of drought and fire are all expected in the future and will increase rangeland degradation and associated sequestration potential, exacerbate populations' vulnerability, and increase exposure to extreme events. There is a critical need for new models of livestock husbandry and rangelands management systems that work at broad spatial scales to restore Botswana's

# Proposed Approach:

The Project interventions are designed to significantly increase the adaptive capacity of the people of Botswana to respond to the negative impacts of climate change in the country's communal lands.

The Project will exclusively service "last-mile communities" who farm on non-tenured Vlei Grazing Areas and achieve its objectives through activities holistically designed to:

1. Strengthen institutions and support systems for climate-responsive planning and management in communal rangelands;
2. Reduce vulnerability to negative livelihood impacts through rangeland rehabilitation and improved livestock management; and
3. Sustain enhanced adaptive capacity and low-emissions development through value-chain and policy transformation.

The Project will be implemented in three target areas of Botswana: Ngamiland (northwest), Kgalagadi (southwest), and Bobirwa (east central). The three areas were prioritized for GCF funding due to their high vulnerability. Collectively, the areas cover 41.3% (~240,000 km²) of the country and have an average population density of less than one person per square kilometer. In addition, the poverty rate exceeds 50%, with socio-economic conditions largely attributed to the effects of drought on traditional livelihoods and limited economic opportunities for rural households compared to formal markets. Although similar socio-economic characteristics of the three areas allow for similar policy and institutional development approach, the carbon sequestration benefits will differ across the three areas due to differences in vegetation cover, soils present, and regional herd size.

In Bobirwa, private and group ranches that supply the commercial beef sector occupy both class I road and market infrastructure. In other areas, livestock are often confined to communal grazing areas due to conflict and trade bans. Commercial rangelands that are the target of the project are in more remote areas, offer adjacent to wildlife areas but excluded from high-value lands, such as areas closer to the Okavango Delta where cattle ranching is a major economic activity.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 7

## Figure 2. Project Target Areas (red borders)

CI, with the extensive support of farming communities, the Government of Botswana, civil society organizations, and NGOs will replicate and scale the demonstrated success of the Herding For Health project in the Chobe District of Botswana. This is a joint initiative between Conservation International and its partners. All Project activities are designed to respect established Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) in compliance with CI's Environmental and Social Policy.

There are three key elements to this model:

- Utilise donor funding and government job creation investments to scale capacity building and career development in professional herding and rangelands rehabilitation that draws on indigenous knowledge.
- Work with communities, NGOs, and government agencies to develop communal grazing plans and improved veterinary services for traditional farmers' livestock. With planned, collective grazing and herd health overseen by professional herders, rangelands are allowed time to recover from overgrazing while increasing the productivity of livestock through better access to fodder and soil organic carbon (SOC) sequestration, and reduce livestock-based GHG emissions.
- Work with government and private sector actors to create flexible but consistent access to markets that sustainably incentivise collective management, reward environmental


---
**Page 12**
---

improvements, and increase adaptive capacity by enabling sale/offtake during times of climate stress.

The Project activities will also include the development and deployment of a Rangeland Stewardship Portal, through which data will be collected on ecosystem health, climatic conditions, cattle herd size and locations, fires, disease outbreaks, and economic returns from livestock. The future Rangeland Portal will provide real-time information to stakeholders in government, and organizations to inform their decision making and planning. The Portal will also connect with the existing M&E system of the Project, which is currently a paper-based system, to provide information flows both to and from Project beneficiaries. As part of the Project & M&E system, an innovative Impact Evaluation will be undertaken to better determine project-attributeable change for key indicators.

The Project approaches emphasize the importance of collective planning and action that respects the indigenous knowledge and self-determination of its beneficiaries. By working directly with communities to build governance and technical capacity to coordinate their grazing and land use practices, the Project will help them to improve their livelihoods by increasing their existing stock; reduce risks from disease, theft, wildlife conflict, and predation; and conserve the natural resources on which they depend.

These engagements will ensure alignment of support and embedding of new approaches to climate-resilient land and livestock management. The Project will also create opportunities for Botswana’s rural communities to better understand their role in addressing global threats from climate change and how their communities can contribute to the mitigation of GHG emissions through improved rangelands management.

The sustainability of interventions is considered critical by the Project at all levels. Addressing the demand side of livestock production is critical as the CGI and Project partners will work with the private sector and government to increase the value of livestock produced and bring market access for their commodities. This will be achieved by providing incentives to farmers forced to travel long distances to sell their livestock assets, an intervention that is particularly relevant during times of climate stress when livestock are unable to travel long distances and often die en route.

In addition to these interventions, improving the financial sustainability of grazing arrangements, and creating financial assistance and operational protocols for private sector and community-based organizations, climate-smart meat products will also contribute to the long-term success of the Project.

The Government of Botswana (GoB) and stakeholders at all levels have been engaged throughout Project development and are very supportive of the Project approach. The government has committed $54 million in co-finance to the Project to fund the training and deployment of 100 extension officers, as well as the establishment of a wage-for-public works program. This contribution represents nearly 60% of the total Project cost. In addition, the GoB has committed to providing the technical and human resource capacity required for the successful delivery of the Project’s adaptation and mitigation impacts. Botswana’s Ministry of Agricultural Development and Food Security (MADF) will lead the implementation of the Project in partnership with CGI. The MADF team that includes representatives of the Ministries of Local Government, Environment, and Trade and Industry will be responsible for ensuring that the Project is implemented effectively and support is critical to the success of the Project and will support the ability of the government to align climate response strategies in communal rangelands and replicate successful Project

## Project Impacts:

The Project approaches will simultaneously reduce degradation of Botswana's rangelands while improving the condition of livestock to increase the resilience of Botswana's most vulnerable populations and the local economies where they live. The Project activities will also result in significant reductions in greenhouse gas emissions from livestock production, including methane and by reducing enteric emissions from livestock through improved management and fodder. Finally, the Project will generate significant co-benefits for biodiversity, ecosystem health, water conservation, and other barriers to move towards an inclusive and diversified climate-resilient economy for Botswana.

Over the 8-5 year implementation period, the Project is expected to directly increase the climate change resilience of 247,000 people (80% of the Project area population, see Table 1) and improve the management of 46,000 km² of natural ecosystems at a cost efficiency of ~$3.00 per hectare.

The Project will also contribute to the achievement of national targets for improvements to livestock management and restoring ecosystems in the target landscapes which will contribute more than 45% of Botswana's National Determined Contribution (NDC) to mitigating global climate change by reducing greenhouse gas emissions from livestock production relative to the country’s 2019 baseline. Over the course of its 20-year lifespan, emissions reductions resulting from the Project are projected to be ~-21.5 Mt CO₂e. The Project has the long-term potential to indirectly increase the climate change resilience of the entire national population of Botswana (~2.3M people) and improve the condition of 175,038 km² of natural ecosystems and associated mitigation impacts. The Project will do this through the development of climate-smart tools and methods available for use by farmers in Botswana, support for national policy development including Commodity Based Trade (CST) reforms, and the enhancement of existing government programmes and value-chains that support sectors vulnerable to climate change.

GCF investment in this Project is critical to catalyze the coordination and capacity building of the people and Government of Botswana to address the impacts of climate change. The models and data developed through this project will influence government policy, create innovation in the way that (p)leigging funds are deployed, and farmers will see first-hand the value in creating well-managed landscapes for generations to come.

# PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1 Climate Change and Development

### Baseline and Current Impacts:

#### 1. The United Nations Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) observed that increased temperature, increased temporal and spatial variation in precipitation, and more frequent climatic disasters are taking place across Africa, particularly where oceanic moderation is absent. Botswana, which shares a border with South Africa, Namibia, and Botswana as an inland, dryland country within Southern Africa is especially exposed to climate risks[1]. According to the ND-GAIN index, Botswana is the 67th most vulnerable country in the world. The impacts of climate change on Botswana’s economy and its capacity to adapt, national adaptation needs are profound and urgent action is required[2].

#### 2. Botswana is currently experiencing significant impacts from anthropogenic climate change[3]. Average temperatures have risen by more than 1 degree Celsius above pre-industrial levels[4], drought frequency has increased significantly (see Figure 4), severity has intensified, and the number of wet years has decreased. The country experiences drier dry seasons and heavier rainfall – resulting in an 18% increase in the severity of extreme precipitation events since 1960[5]. Botswana’s greatest exposure to climate risks comes from the impacts of intensified and more frequent heatwaves, droughts, and increased risk of flooding.

#### 3. Over the last 60 years in Botswana, 21 years (or 32%) were classified as abnormally, moderately, severely, extremely, or exceptionally dry. Seventeen years (25% over this period) were classified as wetter than normal. From 1980 to present, 43.4% of years have been classified as drier compared to 14% being wetter and 43% being near-normal. Since the 1950s to present, Botswana has experienced 27 dry years (11.3%) since 1960[6]. The year 2018 is a wet year for every 3.06 dry years. Furthermore, in the full record (66 years) there have been 43 months classified as Severely, Extremely or Exceptionally Dry, with 31 of those (72%) occurring between 2015 to 2019[7].

### 4. An examination of the effects of El Niño–Southern Oscillation (ENSO) trends shows strong correlation between ENSO and the climate of Botswana. Rainfall decreases and temperature increases in El Niño years and vice versa in La Niña years. Over the 116 years of data, 58% more years show a decrease in precipitation than an increase [37:23]. The year showing the largest decrease was 1940 (−55%), while the year with the largest increase was 1983 (+55%). La Niña years tend to show an increase in precipitation. The La Niña years saw ~7% more wet years than dry years (29:27). However, these wetter years are ∼33mm wetter than normal, and >76% wetter than the La Niño that was the driest of all La Niñas (1980–81). The El Niño phase shows a strong correlation between temperature* and ENSO phase. El Niño phase sees 47 out of 60 years showing an increase in temperature, while only 23 out of 56 years show a decrease by ≥0.3°C. Ngamiland (Average of 703 mm/year) has the highest sensitivity to the ENSO phases with the largest inverse correlations (-100mm per year) to El Niño in 1950, 1982 and La Niña in 1964–65. Ngamiland also shows a strong correlation between El Niño (average 1970, 1980 and 2010), however, the low precipitation climatology (Average of 474 mm/year) this is likely more significant on the ground.

| Figure 3.5: Average temperature (°C) and precipitation (P) compared to the Bivariate ENSO Timeseries average for each of the Project sites |

#### 5. Seventy-three percent (73%) of Botswana's total land area of 572,000 km² is covered by arid and semi-arid rangelands grazed by both livestock and wild animals. Communal livestock production in Botswana plays a significant role there in the livelihoods of rural households socio-economically to ~70% of rural livelihoods[14–21] and the importance of communal livestock production in provisioning food, income and risk assurance to Botswana's rural poor is well documented[2]. The current degradation of Botswana's communal rangelands


---
**Page 17**
---

reduce their population’s capacity to maintain their livelihoods in the face of climate change[1]. In addition to supporting livestock, livelihoods, livestock plays a central role in the cultural practices of the Batswana people[2]. Approximately 85% of Botswana's agricultural output is derived from livestock production. Livestock are the only net positive contributor to GDP for the country. Animals in the traditional livestock system contribute up to 90% of all total national herd. As a result, Botswana’s beef production depends strongly on the success of communal livestock producers[3]. This dual role of livestock as a social safety net and economic drive is reflected in Botswana's current policies and programmes that aim to expand the economic productivity of the nation.

6. The impacts of recent drought intensity and frequency are threatening livelihood activities and increasing pressure on livestock and wildlife populations. Recent drought events in Botswana have affected ~1.3 million people[4] and caused <US$47 million in damages[5]. Among the most significant effects of these droughts is the increased competition for water, with increasing concentrations of domestic and wild animals around water sources, resulting in increased human-wildlife conflict and competition for water. Droughts and fires also lead to a decrease in vegetation cover and quality, which can have negative impacts on livestock[6]. With evidence from satellite imagery confirming this has occurred and has been accelerating since at least the mid-1980s[7], The mobility of animals driven by their search for water has decreased[8] and wildlife conflicts[9] and increased disease outbreaks that, in turn, lead to market exclusions (Foot-and Mouth Disease (FMD) in particular) and resultant loss of income for livestock producers.[10]

## Figure 4: All Official Drought Declarations in Botswana 1911 - 2017

An estimated 710,000 livestock units (approximately 36%) are affected annually by droughts in Botswana[5]. In the rural poor, the effects can be seen as food and seasonal droughts in three of every four households. The loss of livestock occurs in marginalized communities (often in communal lands), including loss of livestock, water scarcity, and marketability. For example, 10% of cattle were lost to drought in 2019, representing approximately 20% of the total herd, devastating the livelihoods of thousands[5]. Loss of livestock to predation also increases in times of drought as livestock are forced into areas where predators are more active[6]. The effects of drought although research is still ongoing, there are likely knock-on effects of drought including increased human wildlife competition and interactions[7] including elephant and rhino poaching, bush-meat hunting, and over-harvesting of Mopane worms[8].

The recorded increased intensity of rainfall events results in floods that negatively impact lives and livelihoods. In the last 50 years, the number of days with heavy rain has not changed significantly. However, the frequency and severity of rainfall events are changing. Of the 11 major flood events recorded since 1972, seven have occurred within the past decade. According to local stakeholders, the observed larger rainfall events, concentrated in fewer months, cause flooding and damage infrastructure and livestock-based livelihoods (Figure 5). These impacts disproportionately affect women who are responsible for finding clean water and food after wells and gardens are destroyed.

9. The draft GCF Country Programme for Botswana recognizes that a critical lack of baseline climate data is one of the primary challenges that the country faces. Local and regional literature suggests that there are significant gaps in understanding rainfall variability and water availability across Botswana. Mean annual rainfall ranges from less than 250 mm in the southwest of the country to more than 600 mm in the northeast. The mean annual surface runoff is estimated at approximately 17 billion m³, with a maximum surface runoff of >700 million m³/year. Official estimates of the rate of aquifer recharge differ between 25 and 40% of rainfall, depending on the source. These figures are derived from data provided by the Department of Water Affairs being on the lower end of this wide range. Most groundwater resources are, however, located in confined aquifer systems that were recharged during the last glacial period (10-25 kyr BP) and are not replenished through modern recharge[4]. The majority of surface run-off is unable to be captured in part because of insufficient storage capacity and in part due to the high evaporation rates associated with the temporal variability of run-off[6]. The surface water storage capacity of the country is recognized as one of its most significant natural resources. However, it should also be noted that Botswana is heavily dependent on transboundary water sources, including groundwater aquifers and rivers[7]. Surface water sources in the country, except for the Okavango Delta, experience a high degree of seasonality with the majority of seasonal surface runoff which is being exacerbated by climate change impacts[8]. Despite ~90% of water supply in the

## Areas coming from surface water, the majority (~65%) of Botswana’s total water supply is sourced from groundwater[20]. In addition to national water resources, Botswana receives an annual water quota of up to 7.3 million m³/year from the Molopo Dam in South Africa[14]. The water demand for agriculture and reliance on the communal livestock sector are the Okavango Delta and groundwater aquifers.

### 10. The Okavango Delta, a global RAMSAR and Natural World Heritage Site, and a critical source of water supply to key rangelands in private and community tourism concessions being threatened by climate change[23]. Water levels in the Okavango River have been declining since 2018-2019 season was the lowest in the last 38 years, lower than the 1996-98 drought that resulted from a La Niña event. The amount of water entering the Delta has decreased as well as the magnitude of the wet season pulse[24]. This has implications for the surrounding vegetation and ecology, which varies in its structure and function depending on the timing and volume of water entering the Delta.

Reduced flow into this Okavango delta is shown to increase wildlife dispersal into adjacent communal rangelands. The increased movement of wildlife into these areas increases the number of conflicts between wildlife, humans, and livestock as these populations complete over winter grazing[25]. Wildlife numbers in the Okavango Delta have been increasing while brightness has also consistently increased in the Delta over the last decade. There is limited academic consensus on whether all observed changes can be fully attributed to climate change or other factors such as human activities. The increase in wildlife numbers stimulates regeneration of nutritious “green flush”. In either case, direct or indirectly, climate change and its associated impacts are increasing pressure on water-rich Delta systems are increasing Botswana’s emissions profile and warrant changes towards management approaches that are integrated across communal-grazing-ecotourism landscapes[26].

### 11. The direct impacts of anthropogenic increases in GHG emissions (including deforestation) are projected to increase the frequency, intensity, duration, and affecting this ecosystem mitigation potential[27]. Botswana’s rangeland degradation is driven by expansion of bush encroachment, alien plant invasions, and bare ground. A reduction in the number of frost days due to climate change[28] and increased atmospheric CO₂ is contributing to the

## Green Climate Fund

### Projected Climate Change Impacts in Botswana

**13.** Despite the intent of the Paris Agreement to reduce emissions to a level resulting in less than 2 degrees Celsius, temperatures are on pace to increase by the 1.5 degree threshold at the end of the next decade and 3.2 degrees by the end of the century, with higher temperature increases expected in Botswana [7]. There is therefore an urgent need for Botswana to prepare and adapt while. With current impacts of climate change in Botswana already being felt, the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) show that additional, potentially catastrophic climatic impacts can be expected by 2050 [1] (see Figure 6).

**14.** Temperatures across Botswana are likely to increase by between 1.5 and 3.5°C by 2050. Daytime maximum temperatures are likely to increase by ~2.0°C in the southwest and

* * *

**Page 23**

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 19

#### -3 to +4°C in the northern (Ngamiland) and eastern regions (Bobirwa) by 2050 under RCP 4.5 (Figure 7). Increased day time temperatures are closely matched by increased night-time temperatures with the average diurnal/nocturnal range rising by 0.1–0.2°C across most of Botswana. Compact and continuous rainfall in the region is becoming more common (90th percentile) and historically are becoming increasingly common over persistent periods (six days). The number of days contributing to such events used to range from 0-40 days/year (1950–2000) and are expected to increase to 40-100 days/year under RCP 4.5 and 68-150 days/year under RCP 8.5 by 2050.

Annual average precipitation will decrease 5-18 mm annually (3–15% as presented in the Botswana Third National Communication [TNC] to the UNFCCC), but a +4–13 mm increase across three peak rainfall months associated with summertime convective precipitation. This decrease in projected precipitation is greater in northern areas where a decrease of ~32 mm is expected. However, this will be offset by an increase in annual average temperature and most of Botswana by 4-13 mm during these high-rainfall months. The coefficient of variation, already high in the central to northern areas of Botswana, will increase further (in some areas up to 10%) which will further worsen the year-on-year precipitation variability and longer dry spells.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 20

## Figure 17: Projected climate variables (top) and projected climate anomalies (bottom) for Botswana.

### 16. Extreme heat waves are expected to significantly increase under RCP 4.5 and RCP 8.5 scenarios from -2 heatwaves/year historically to 5-10 heatwaves/year by 2050[1]. Future probability of drought occurrence, including severity and duration of drought events, increase under most RCP scenarios. Even with a projected increase in annual precipitation in a model, increasing temperatures will lead to increased evaporation rates and reduced soil moisture content (see Figure 7). The maximum heatwave durations used to be <20 days before the year 2000, but are expected to significantly increase in future. The rate of increase under RCP 8.5 scenario is approximately +1 day per decade. Under RCP 4.5, the rate will be slightly lower and the rate will increase over time with the maximum average anomaly being -3.7°C by 2050 under RCP 4.5 every month, with the largest increases being in October (see Figure 8). This increase will be expressed as a reduction of the number of days per year with temperatures exceeding 36°C (extreme heat stress) from -3 to <20 days (RCP 4.5) by 2050. In addition, 3-day heat waves are projected to increase by ~143% by the mid-2050s[9]. Increased temperatures and heat waves will result in an increase in evaporation of surface water, reducing water availability for agriculture and other uses, with a high probability of water availability which is critical to the broader regional population and economy.

**17.** Extreme water scarcity is expected to become increasingly common and severe across the Project Area, with significant negative direct consequences. A compact analysis of the meteorological water balance between precipitation and evapotranspiration, using SPEI, reveals that the intensity, duration and frequency of drought conditions are projected to worsen in the future. These changes will have a range of impacts on the resilience of rangeland ecosystems to the impacts of grazing and fire, likely resulting in accelerated degradation of these systems. The Project Area is particularly vulnerable as many areas targeted by the Project are likely to be disproportionately impacted by these changes due to their limited baseline adaptive capacity, access to drought relief programmes, and alternative livelihood options.

#### Vegetation composition favouring plants that possess the C<sub>4</sub> photosynthetic pathway (e.g., shrubs) over plants that possess the C<sub>3</sub> photosynthetic pathway (many grasses). This will promote proliferation of shrubs, invasive plants, and the loss of biodiversity. These negative impacts are expected to be more pronounced in the three Project Areas where they have an advantage due to reduced competition for water and nutrients. The increased drought stress is expected to result in a reduction in livestock productivity across all three Project areas and throughout the country. Projected increased temperatures, increased evaporation and low rainfall will also increase soil temperature and reduce forage quality. These combined effects are expected to lead to reduced feed intake by livestock, thus increase erosion and potentially create conditions for increased outbreak of devastating pests (e.g., tsetse fly) and diseases such as anthrax and foot-and-mouth disease that are carriers of diseases such as heartwater and rain scald (bovine dermatophilosis).

#### Livestock sector productivity will decrease and GHG emissions will increase due to climate stress. The direct greenhouse gas contribution of the livestock sector, in the case of rumen methane production, is estimated at 14% of total anthropogenic GHG emissions worldwide[5], which accounts for approximately half (39% and 10%) respectively of total livestock emissions in Botswana. In addition to this, enteric fermentation through ruminant digestion accounts for approximately 50% of the remaining emissions, none of which is applied in extensive communal grazing systems in Botswana. Similarly, manure management is not a consequence of climate change and thus does not contribute significantly to GHG emissions from livestock intensive systems[6]. The impact of climate change on Botswana's livestock sector is felt in terms of reduced feed intake, increased heat stress, reduced growth rates, increased methane emissions where poor grazing quality and energy expenditure required to obtain water and nutrition increases the amount of enteric fermentation per unit intake, reducing production efficiency. This will result in reduced meat and milk yields.

#### Increased temperatures above a threshold of 32°C[7] results in heat stress for cattle, especially for the exotic breeds that are less tolerant to heat than local breeds (Tswana). The projected number of high temperature days and consecutive dry days (CDD) [Climate impact analysis shows a significant increase in CDDs across all three Project areas (Figure 10). This will result in reduced feed intake, decreased growth/mass, reduced milk yields and increased mortality. These impacts are expected to dramatically impact poor farming households and reduce the profitability of the livestock sector overall. Increased temperatures also increase livestock exposure to parasites and diseases.

droughts become more frequent and severe, and as rainfall becomes less regular with larger precipitation events, rangelands experience higher rates of erosion and less vegetation is able to grow. Sandy soils, such as those that characterize the majority of Botswana, are often more susceptible to wind erosion than loamy or clayey soils. In addition, the presence of shallow topsoil exposes a subsoil horizon that is often unsuitable to the production of palatable forage species. This combination of factors results in increased soil erosion and the reduction or loss of biological or economic productivity following a reduction of vegetative cover and subsequent processes[1]. This degradation results in a loss of carbon stored in the ecosystem, as well as reduced capacity to sequester additional carbon through reduced erosion and reduced rates of soil carbon accumulation due to a reduction in vegetative production. Through implementation of appropriate land use practices and improved management practices, the Project will achieve direct mitigation benefits including carbon sequestration from greater solid carbon storage and emissions reduction from avoided soil carbon loss.

22. Poverty levels and vulnerability are likely to increase as climate change contributes to increased degradation of Botswana's communal rangelands. The combined impact of increasing temperatures and reduced rainfall is expected to have a significant negative effect, strongly felt by smallholders who are dependent on communally shared rangelands[2]. The vast majority of the country's low-income population is dependent on the country's communal land resources for their livelihoods (see also Gender Assessment and GAP). More than 50% of all community owned livestock is owned by relatively poor households. This dependence on communal lands for grazing and degradation results in poor animal condition and reduces cattle off-take from communal lands because farmers are less able to sell low weight cattle. Losses are projected to increase as the rangelands degrade, leading to a reduction in milk production and greater exposure to disease and climate stress. The opportunity costs to bring cattle to large towns for sale is also likely to increase due to increased transport costs[3]. This has resulted in an observed mortality rate increasing from 11.8% in 2015 to 15.1% in 2017 for communal herds. Mortality also increases in herds of less than 100 cattle typical of poorer households.

In the context of extensive livestock production, and to the purpose of this project, degradation is defined both on the production of palatable forage species and as a reduction in soil carbon storage. The latter is determined by the presence or absence of vegetation cover and the degree of bare ground and phytomass potentials relative to an arbitrary reference date. Some approaches to estimating degradation are commonly described using the following methods: (i) Vegetation Cover Index (VCI); (ii) Soil Carbon Index (SCI); (iii) Weighted Potability Composition Method which utilizes the fractionage potential ratings of common grass species to determine the degree of degradation; and (iv) The Ecological Index Method. The VCI is a measure of vegetation cover, with values ranging from 0% to 100%. The SCI is an index that measures the extent of soil carbon loss relative to a reference date. The VCI and SCI methods are described in Annex 11. The Ecological Index Method (EIM) is a method for assessing degradation based on the degree of bare ground and phytomass potentials, with values ranging from 0% to 100%. The EIM method is described in Annex 11.

The SCI and VCI methods are not appropriate for use within this project as they do not provide an accurate measure of the grass species ecological group classification relative to a reference date. As described on Annex 11, Appendix A:11.1. The Ecological Index Method (EIM) is used in this project because it provides a more accurate assessment of degradation based on the degree of bare ground and phytomass potentials relative to an arbitrary reference date.

The EIM method is a simplified approach to the Ecological Index Method which was developed by the Ministry of Environment, Waterways and Tourism (MEWT) of Botswana. The EIM method provides a more accurate assessment of degradation based on the degree of bare ground and phytomass potentials relative to an arbitrary reference date.

The degradation risk can be determined based on repeat monitoring over multiple growing seasons to identify trends in the key indicators of vegetation cover, soil carbon storage and phytomass potential. The EIM method is described in Annex 11.

## Increases in area of land degraded will also exacerbate conflicts between agriculture and livestock. Climate change's direct impacts on fodder and water resources will augment competition for available resources and negatively impact the viability of both livestock and wildlife. For example, African buffalo are a host population for FMD in Botswana. If they are forced to move out of their natural habitat into areas where there is no vegetation, the probability that the remaining sources, artificial or otherwise, will be visited by both populations will increase. This could lead to an outbreak of FMD and other diseases that can lead to a complete trade ban for a six-month period and require a significant investment into a vaccination regime. When communal farmers move animals into wildlife movement corridors in search of water and fodder, they may encounter wildlife. The result is that humans experience "wilderness experiences" diminishes the tourism experience or causes wildlife to move out of the area. Stephanie podgorny: Building consumption, and human-wildlife conflict are projected to increase in the future as humans, livestock, and wildlife compete for survival.

### Project Context and Climate Vulnerability in Target Areas

#### 24. As a dryland country in Southern Africa, 76% of Botswana's total land area of 572,000 km² is covered by rangelands grazed by both livestock and wild animals. Nearly 70% of Botswana's wildlife population (approximately 130,000 elephants and 400,000 antelopes) are found in conservation areas, and the remaining 5% is under private ownership, primarily by commercial farmers. While conservation and private lands tend to be managed with ecological principles in mind, the majority of Botswana's land area (approximately 25%) in conservation areas, and the remaining 5% is under private ownership, primarily by commercial farmers. While conservation and private lands tend to be managed with ecological principles in mind, the majority of Botswana's land area (approximately 25%) in conservation areas, and the remaining 5% is under private ownership, primarily by commercial farmers.

#### 25. The Botswana rural livestock herd is approximately 10 million animals. Botswana legislation defines a herd of more than 400 animals as commercial, though this is loosely applicable with some private farmers having fewer than this number of livestock, but still granted private land. Using the ratios between communal and commercial operations, CI estimates that over half of the country's animals are in herds of more than 400 animals that have a structure for breeding or commercial production; 75% are estimated to be in

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 27

## COMMUNAL LIVESTOCK PRODUCTION - COMMUNAL LAND USE ACROSS THE COUNTRY

### EFFECTS ON BEEF PRODUCTION AND ECONOMIC CONTRIBUTION

#### IMPACT ON COMMUNITY-OWNED RANGELAND RESOURCES
In communal livestock production systems, where landowners have access to a shared rangeland resource with equal rights by all, the communal land use across the country represents 97% of the total area. This system is crucial for Botswana's beef production and contributes significantly to its GDP.

#### ROLE OF COMMUNAL LAND IN BEEF PRODUCTION
Botswana's beef production heavily depends on successful communal livestock producers. The national livestock sector accounts for 65% of agricultural GDP, with cattle contributing about 10% of the country’s Gross Domestic Product (GDP). Thus, the livestock sector constitutes approximately 70% of rural livelihoods.

#### ECONOMIC AND SOCIAL IMPORTANCE OF COMMUNAL LAND
In addition to supporting livelihoods, livestock play a central role in cultural practices and tourism. Botswana's rangelands are also economically critical as they provide insurance or "bank accounts" for people during times of need due to their resilience against drought. These lands make a large economic contribution to Botswana’s sustainable development.

#### DECLINE OF COMMUNAL LAND USE
Land for communal production systems is decreasing in size, particularly prime land near infrastructure and water resources, which are privatized or fenced off for commercial livestock and tourism concessions. Current fence-based management practices limit the mobility of livestock, reducing their resilience against increased climate variability and stress. This has led to negative impacts on wildlife and vegetation degradation.

#### IMPLEMENTATION OF PROJECTS
The project will be implemented in communal lands across three districts: Ngamiland, Kgalagadi District, and Northern District. These areas are recognized as home to the most climate-vulnerable populations due to their high proportion of rural poor and severe climatic impacts, particularly drought. The three target Project Areas include:

1. **Ngamiland**
2. **Kgalagadi District**
3. **Northern District**

The areas have an estimated combined population of ~300,000 people (Table 2). The areas are characterized by a low average population density, remoteness, and lack of access to services – which are specific factors that reduce the adaptive capacity of many households.

Poverty in the rural areas is also higher than in the urban centres. In the rural areas, poverty levels exceed 50%. A larger proportion of female-headed households are found in the rural areas than in the urban centres, and these households are observed to have lower adaptive capacity**.

The geographies of the rangelands in these areas include distinct features. The specific habitats of the communal grazing lands targeted by the project are remarkably similar. They fall along a gradient from low-lying floodplains to high-altitude sandvelds with different soils and soil types. Botswana has more clay-rich soils that support more woody species and surface water sources; the Kgalagadi and Ngamiland communal grazing areas have sandier soils and are characterized by ephemeral water sources.

The biodiversity of the rangelands, particularly in palatable grass species across the three communal grazing areas, is very similar. The same dominant grasses and other palatable species are common across the three Project areas, with population densities decreasing in the Bobirwa and Kgalagadi. As a result, the three areas respond ecologically to climate change similarly, requiring similar herbivore management for regeneration and emissions reduction impacts (Table 3).

### 30. Current livestock management practices across the three districts are similar with some regional variation that stem from climate and habitat-driven fodder, water availability and wildlife presence.
Lead by FAO (2006), all livestock farming practices must comply with the Disease and Animal Health Act (2015) which regulates the breeding of animals, their feeding and control of diseases of livestock, regulates the import, export and movement of animals; and provides regulatory instructions for these issues such as quarantine and mandatory vaccination. The Ministry of Livestock is responsible for implementing this legislation, usually as veterinary support and oversight focuses on commercial production and is limited in these areas.
A summary of current practices derived from stakeholder consultations is presented in Table 4 below.

|Livestock Management Practice| Description|
|---|---|
|Herd Movement| Current communal grazing system in the Project Area lack analysis of and planning for herd movement. The livestock management practice of herd movement is typically made at the individual household level on. Fencing should not be practiced and where it exists, it is poorly maintained. In some areas, there are no fences at all.|
|Village Grazing Areas targeted by the Project, multiple farming households (15-20) make uncontrolled use of these grazing areas which results in overgrazing and soil degradation followed by poor animal performance. In Nigeraid, where market access options are not available, livestock owners tend to graze their animals on roadsides, in residential areas and water infrastructure, and cause numerous road accidents. # Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal V2.0 | Page 30

## Herd Health

Current herd health levels across the three Project areas are extremely poor and in most cases, animals are not vaccinated against any disease (the reasons for this are unknown). Although the reasons for this are unknown, herd health should be maintained through systematic vaccination programmes. The Project will support a baseline herd health assessment to determine current coverage of key diseases, including anthrax, and provide recommendations on how to improve herd health in the lean, dry season.

## Composition

Many herds contain unproductive animals of advanced age. In addition, sub-optimal sex and breed composition is common within many herds. This is a major concern as it represents a way to ensure optimal productivity and animal performance. Unproductive animals compete with productive animals for feed resources and reduce herd performance. Breeds are also an important component of herd composition. In Nguniland, CBPP has been identified as the most significant disease in cattle herds. The Project will support a baseline herd health assessment to determine current coverage of key diseases, including anthrax, and provide recommendations on how to improve herd health in the lean, dry season.

## Record-keeping

Currently there is little if any written records of herd ownership, animal ID, annual performance and breeding data. This makes it difficult for farmers to access credit or insurance products. It also makes it difficult for livestock farmers to access formal markets across Botswana. Best practice is to maintain a deep animal record across all animals in the herd. However, this is not possible under current registration system requirements and has become an additional barrier to market entry for commercial farmers due to poor connecting and limited expertise with technology.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL 2.0 | PAGE 31

## Off-take

Currently, off-take across the project areas is low and strongly influenced by annual and rangeland performance that fluctuates between good and poor rainfall years. Off-take is forced to be seasonally constrained (e.g., during the dry season when livestock require water requirements). Ideally, off-take should be systematically planned to maintain optimal levels of herd size and productivity in line with the project’s objectives (see Table 5 in Annex B).

### 31. The Tribal Grazing Lands Policy (TGLP) 1975

Amended in 1993 separates land into commercial grazing areas, communal grazing areas, and reserve areas, the latter of which was intended for use under climate stress. In none of the stakeholder consultations across the three project areas were there any concerns raised about access to water or grazing lands.

### 32. According to the Botswana Census 2015

There are 104 gazetted village areas that include many communal rangelands (e.g., Nangamundu, Kgalagadi, and Bokwele) and private commercial ranches (e.g., Mababe). Climate vulnerability across the target communities within these areas was determined using a combination of three indicators: climate change impacts on water availability, capacity, and rainfall (a measure of access to key services).

### 33. The Project will target populations living within

The 104 Village Grazing Areas (55 in Nangamundu, 34 in Kgalagadi, and 15 in Bokwele). These areas are predominantly not part of either the formal economic sectors (commercial beef production or tourism) and for the

## Project areas are remote, "last-mile" communities. Their subsistence comes largely from Botswana's social grant system; of which the widest reaching programme benefiting these households is the pellegrin "cash for public works" program. Formal reviews of (pellegrin) cash-for-work programmes have been conducted and recommendations implemented since 2008 when the Programme has not achieved its desired economic development and self-reliance goals. The majority of the Project target project households remain dependent on their livestock assets for their livelihoods.

### 34. The observed poverty levels in the target areas are attributed to (1) effects of drought on traditional farming systems, (2) lack of access to markets, (3) limited access to credit and (4) to formal markets. Cattle sales and births in the Project area are lower relative to other regions in the country; whereas deaths, losses and eradication due to veterinary control measures are higher.

### 35. Current adaptation strategies to additional climate stresses on livestock production in the target communities are limited and the impact and ability to respond to changing climate in these communities is low. Women are more likely than men to be involved in decision-making over their own and participate in livestock farming activities. Wealthy households typically have higher numbers of cattle, and female headed households are more likely to have small stock (goats and sheep) for income generation.

### Project Context, Current Value-chain Climate Vulnerability, and GHG Emissions

37. Globally, livestock production accounts for 5-10% of all agricultural emissions contributing to climate change. The growing middle-class of Africa, mostly living in urban areas, is driving demand that is expanding the number of cattle on the continent at an increasing rate, often leading to overgrazing and deforestation (Figure 12). As a result, meat-eating populations are shifting towards vegetarian and vegan diets gain popularity, for poor and marginalized populations, animal-based protein remains essential. The challenge lies in how to feed these vulnerable populations under increased climate stress is a crucial challenge for nearly every country on the continent. As such, it is critical to expand available food sources and reduce livestock emissions. To achieve this, we need to see significant transformational changes in production, by 2030 and 2050, 12% and 15% of Africa's meat will be supplied from less sustainable sources overall.

38. Botswana's current value chains, with low local population densities and highly developed commercial livestock production systems, make it a major exporter of beef within the continent and overseas. The export value of Botswana's beef in 2014 was US$15m, making it Africa's largest beef exporter. In the same year, 57% of the cattle was absorbed by the country's primary processors, which are mainly owned by South African companies. These processors promote and operationalize Botswana beef exports. The EU market is the most important export destination for Botswana's beef. However, the EU has strict regulations that impose considerable costs across the value chain. Botswana's export competition with other countries in the region is also increasing. In addition to the cost of preferential access granted under the interim Economic Partnership Agreement with the EU and the high tariffs imposed by South Africa on imports from Botswana, the EU has entered into with other beef-producing countries. Nevertheless, maintaining its status as EU-approved offers Botswana important access for exporting to other countries, especially in Europe. The EU's decision to grant preferential access to Botswana is based on studies have indicated that EU certification is offset by the significant costs added to the beef value chain. These costs are not only related to the cost of certification but also respond to its consumers' concerns. These input costs, combined with the monopoly of BMC in this export arrangement to the EU, are driving new private sector entities, including Meat for Africa (MfA), into the market.

Within the Ngamiland and Bobirwa Project areas, FMD outbreaks have led to intermittent but continuous livestock losses. The lack of a formalized disease control system has also existed, such as BMC offering higher prices for older, heavier animals, leading to maladaptive responses by farmers. These include selling off their breeding stock at a time when it is most vulnerable during climate stress. Within the Project target areas, there are seven licensed export abattoirs, two municipally run abattoirs, and numerous butcheries. However, these facilities have not been able to meet demand for beef in Botswana due to domestic consumption has arisen in all three areas where the limited local livestock production cannot meet the demand. This is particularly true in times of climate stress. Resilience in this sector and balancing a balance between supply for domestic consumption and export is therefore important for local food security.

This affected the supply of meat to local retailers and the tourism sector importing meat from South Africa for their business survival. Closures of these facilities also led to a significant increase in the number of animals being culled due to FMD outbreaks and more recent operational closures in times of drought-induced water scarcity affect economic viability and reflect the interconnectedness of rangeland health, economic health, and regional climate security.

## Contribution of Project to GHG Emissions Reductions

### 41. Across the Project regions, households own over 320,000 cattle that produce more than 600,000 metric tonnes per year of methane (CH₄) from ruminant and herd stock, a baseline level of CH₄ per year was calculated using IPCC Tier 2 methodologies and is shown in Table 7 below. It is important to note that Botswana's GHG Inventories for Biennial Update Report, First Biennial Update Report, and Second Biennial Update Report are based on the livestock sector's accounting of the emissions from the livestock sector on the IPCC Tier 1 approach. However, this Project will use a Tier 2 methodology as recommended by the IPCC Tier 2 approach as recommended in each of these reports. This Project represents an opportunity to build an evidence foundation for the updated national livestock emission inventory. The default value used is based on the average of the Tier 1 and Tier 2 values.

### 42. Botswana's Tier 1 inventory (32–38 kgCH₄/ head/year) represents the commercialized sector for cattle, sheep, goats, and buffalo. This Tier 1 methodology is considered a baseline method for countries with large livestock populations, such as Botswana. It is expected that the poor fodder quality available to livestock on communal land and significant energy requirements associated with feed production and transport will increase emissions from this sector considerably increases the livestock emissions intensity. This is reflected in the greater range of Tier 2 values (38–50 kgCH₄/ head/year) compared to the Tier 1 inventory (80–83 kgCH₄/ head/year). The opportunity exists to halve the entire fermentation emission rates through the Project's improved management practices.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 39

## Proposed Project is designed to transform current rangeland management in Botswana to collectively build the resilience of rangeland ecosystems and the communities whose livelihoods are threatened by climate change.

### Rangeland practices that regenerate healthy ecosystems that sequester carbon (**) and build resilience to extreme climatic events have been successfully tested in other parts of southern Africa (**), but have not yet been adopted at scale in Botswana.

## The Project will apply proven restoration techniques aimed at reversing degradation that leads to reduced adaptive capacity for ecosystems, negative livelihood impacts, and increased GHG emissions (Table 1.1). An important consequence of managed herd movements is the reduction of bare ground sheet erosion which increases water infiltration into the soil, promoting root growth. A deeper and more extensive root system will generate greater resilience to drought and higher infiltration rates reduce runoff and sediment loss.

### Table 2.1: Summary of restoration techniques and potential change in targeted rangelands (for more information and shapes of restoration techniques please see Feasibility Study Section 4)

| Technique | Degradation | Description |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Panning | Bare ground sheet erosion | This method involves making furrows for water collection across the landscape. The soil is then packed into these furrows to form a dam. The method is suitable for compacted areas but not for extensive in-situ bare ground sheet erosion. It is also not suitable where there are no perennial water sources or where there has been heavy exposure to wind erosion and salt crust formation. | X | X |
| Restoration Seams | Bare ground sheet erosion | These are second into areas of bare compaction and sheet erosion. The soil is packed into these furrows to form a dam. Water collection occurs in the furrow, which can be further enhanced by planting trees or shrubs facilitated through broadcast seeding of natural species or left as native vegetation. This method is suitable for compacted areas but not extensive in-situ bare ground sheet erosion. It is also not suitable where there has been heavy exposure to wind erosion and salt crust formation. | X |
| Restoration Seams | Bare ground sheet erosion - Ecological deviant areas | Designed for sites where water flow is blocked by dynamic vegetation such as shrubs or trees, which can be removed in the new plantings that enable natural regeneration. Also critical for areas with high soil moisture content and low wind. It will be created with enterprise development beneficiaries. | X |
| Livestock Interseeding | Bare ground sheet erosion | This method involves planting grasses and legumes into bare ground sheet erosion areas using a cattle or tractor mounted seeder. The concentrate nutrients from livestock waste and may also enable seed and plant material to be distributed in the soil. It is suitable for restoration and maintenance in areas and times when moisture is present. | X |

| Unpalatable species dominance/increase | The use of livestock herding corridors is useful for the control and management of vegetation and to enable propagation of palatable grass seeds and to allow grazing animals to graze on areas that are not grazed. ## Bush-clearing and Polling

### Bush Encroachment (removal of native species)

| Bush-clearing involves removal of lower branches of encroaching shrubs and trees to allow the growth of grasses, legumes, and other plants that are more palatable for grazing animals. This technique is strategically placed to facilitate vegetation use or in the case of livestock corridors, to provide access points for grazing animals. | X | X |

True pepper trees remove roots, shrubs and other vegetation that are not palatable for grazing animals. This technique is useful in maintaining shade cover and reducing grazing pressure on the soil. | X | X |

50. Research conducted in the Kgalagadi District revealed that communal grazing provides the widest range of ecosystem services as compared with other land uses [1]. Improvement in rangeland condition is likely to result in improved water provision and regulation in the Project area, which will benefit both livestock farmers and wildlife.

Other potential positive outcomes for flora and fauna diversity and carbon sequestration may emerge from the project. The project team and its partners will work with local stakeholders and partners to measure the impact on these environmental indicators to understand the contribution to such co-benefits and to inform policy and practice recommendations for integrated land use management in the region.

51. In addition to building economic resilience of the Project area populations, the Project will protect the environmental assets upon which the broader economy is based: Tourism and wildlife. The Project will also provide benefits by protecting rangelands targeted interface areas where communal farming takes place in areas where either tourism or commercial beef farming exist. For additional context, tourism concession areas are purely fenced off from communal grazing lands and are not contiguous with them geographically or separated by government-maintained fences. Communal grazing lands are primarily used for livestock production and are located adjacent to the interface areas impacted by wildlife coming from tourism areas and eating communal farmers' animals or fodder resources, and commercial farmers supplement their fodder from communal grazing lands.

The Project will also provide benefits by protecting rangelands targeted interface vulnerable populations in the Project-targeted areas that are dependent on farming but have no access to water. These communities are located adjacent to the interface between tourism concession areas. It is also anticipated that improved rangeland health outside of current ecotourism areas will protect Botswana's national reputation as a premier nature tourism destination and provide additional economic benefits.

Similarly, the Project has the potential to bring commercial farmers and their grazing lands into larger markets and supply chains, thereby contributing towards building the climate resilience of such large-scale land users while also improving the climate resilience of their contribution to national value chains and export economy.

52. The current inability of communal farmers to comply with formal trade regulations results in more than 250,000 animals in the Project areas grazing, causing degradation, increasing disease transmission, and contributing to a loss of biodiversity. This has a negative impact on the economy, with only illegal and high-risk slaughters supporting rural food-security.

The Project interventions aimed at landslide/waste management practices will convert this national liability into assets that support the broader socio-economic status of the nation.

# B.2. Theory of change

## Problem Statement

### Climate Change and Degradation of Rangelands in Botswana

#### Current Paradigm

The Project aims to shift the current paradigm where:

- **Climate change is exacerbating degradation of rangeland ecosystems** - reducing these regions' carbon storage capacity and increasing livestock-based emissions. Future climate impacts including extreme droughts will be more severe in the future, leading to further degradation of rangelands.
  
- **Botswana's traditional livestock system**, where herder-controlled seasonal livestock movement according to climate allowed for rangeland rest and recovery and wildlife co-existence, has been widely abandoned. Combined with climate change degraded rangelands are now more vulnerable to extreme hazards through loss of livestock to starvation, lack of water, disease, and predation.

- **There is low economic resilience in marginal wildlife areas where ecotourism is not viable** and there is no opportunity to offtake livestock into formal markets due to FMD legislation and other constraints.


---
**Page 50**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 46

Rural populations most vulnerable to the impacts of climate change adopt coping strategies that are detrimental to human well-being (e.g., sex-work and sacrificing family nutrition, loss of biodiversity, forest degradation, and ecosystems), e.g., over-degradation of rangelands and reduced sequestration capacity, movement into wildlife management areas, illegal poaching).

Government and investment requirements for livestock production exacerbate vulnerability for rural households and communities by limiting access to markets and low-emission opportunities and encouraging value-chains based on geographically fenced zones and ranches that prevent large scale movements required for wildlife and livestock to adapt to new climate situations.

The Project's theory of change expressly addresses the current paradigm through the proposed Project components:

1) Strengthening institutions and support systems for climate-responsive planning and management (Component 1).

2) Reducing GHG emissions and negative livelihood impacts through new job deployment in range land restoration, improved livestock management, and climate impact monitoring. (Component 2)

3) Promoting climate-sensitive enterprise development and value-chain investments to sustain transformational change (Component 3).

Desired Paradigm:

63. The project's primary goals are to improve the adaptive capacity of vulnerable households and maximize land and livestock mitigation contributions to emissions reductions by:
- Improving governance and information availability for decision making on climate-responsive planning and management.
- Improving fodder and water resources in designated village grazing areas by deploying (pledging) investments to fund the salaries of "Ecorangers" and "restoration workers" that are trained in range land restoration, improved livestock management, and climate impact monitoring system of part-time employment for a few months with no skill or outcome targets).
- Institutionalizing the development of a sustainable livelihoods model based on a climate resilient production system (herd movement, herd health, herd composition, record-keeping, and off-take); and
- Reweaving social capital that leads to improved ecosystem health by unlocking market access and enterprise development opportunities; and by building industry awareness and capacity for the private sector to invest in rural enterprises that measurably increases income security for vulnerable farming households and sustains low-emissions production systems.


---
**Page 51**
---

**GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 47**

### 64. The Project will move Botswana to a climate resilient, low-emission sustainable development paradigm where:

- The government of Botswana's commitments to the SDGs, UNFCCC, and GDSA translate into aligned programmes and policies that empower community-level governance structures to develop and enforce climate-resilient communal grazing and water management strategies.
  
- Marginalised rural people are trained and employed as professional restoration workers or Ecorangers (professional herdsmen) to draw on indigenous knowledge systems and utilize new technologies that restore and maintain rangeland ecosystem and livestock health under climate change.

- Farmers and their communities experience fewer losses of their economic assets to climate stresses and benefit from new land and livestock management practices.

- New market access channels and climate-resilience protocols build value-chain partnerships and local-level enterprise development (which may include natural fodder development, restoration enterprises, veterinary enterprises, hides, skins, and wool, as well as beef) that fund mechanisms that sustain healthy rangelands under climate change.

### 65. The theory of change diagram (Figure 16) illustrates how the Project will overcome key barriers to achieve its goals to reduce climate vulnerability and reduce emissions from rangeland livestock production in Botswana's communal rangelands through enabling and monitoring gender equitable governance, supporting climate-resilient livestock farming, and developing sustainable value chains.

### Overcoming Barriers to Achieve Impact

#### 66. The barriers to this paradigm shift are complex and stem from a range of obstacles that perpetuate climate vulnerability and high-emissions production systems including:

- Weak community awareness and governance mechanisms for designing and implementing climate responsive rangeland restoration and livestock production strategies.

Although farmer and community structures exist, and in some cases have overseen village grazing plans, currently livestock management practices on the targeted communal lands are mostly driven by individual farmers. The underlying causes of lack of income for time for rangelands, overstocking, and poor animal health conditions. Private farmers also bring a range of challenges including increased vulnerability to climate change and vulnerability for those that do not own private dry season farm reserves (16). Lack of coordination with livestock owners who own private lands but still utilize communal lands, as well as the lack of enforcement mechanisms for grazing plans.

---

**References:**
(17) Galla, A. C. & Wilson, T. (2005), "Winners and Losers: Privatizing the Commons of Botswana". IED publications, available at https://ied.org.np


---
**Page 52**
---

### Insufficient government resource allocation and coordination for governing communal rangelands and community adoption of climate-resilient livestock management strategies.

#### Government investments in job creation have not been deployed in communal rangeland restoration or climate change resilience, and the effectiveness of the Government’s current approach to managing communal rangelands is questionable. Agricultural investments in communal lands have sought to expand privatization and reduce disease risks through fence-building to reduce degradation and support improved management practices. However, this has led to a reduction in biodiversity due to the presence of migratory wildlife and the seasonal nature of nutritious rangeland conditions. This has also resulted in increased human-wildlife conflict. For example, in Ngamiland, 10,000 km² of fences require regular maintenance due to damage by livestock and wildlife, particularly elephants, as they move across areas following rainfall patterns.

#### Herding is not an attractive career and traditional knowledge of managing livestock, particularly in times of climate stress, is being lost. Currently, negligence and poverty are responsible for a significant number of livestock losses. Despite the value (economic and cultural) of the assets entrusted to them, the herders themselves have no incentive or capacity to manage their resources sustainably as they historically had come to climate-resilience. Most animals are not herded (Ngamiland) or herded by elders (Pikagadag and Bobone) and the rich traditional knowledge of livestock-wildlife interactions is being lost. This has led to a loss in the ability to manage and maintain healthy livestock herds in times of heavy rains or rapid temperature changes being lost.

#### As a result, there are no qualified personnel to manage communal rangelands. The job isn’t “sexy” due to lack of inclusion of technology and/or professionally recognised qualifications for the job (addressed in Output 1.3 and Output 2.1).

### Lack of spatially explicit information, analytical tools, and monitoring effort on climate, rangeland health, livestock management, and emissions reduction strategies.

#### Data that would be useful for developing Eco-System-Based Adaptation (ESBA) activities and adaptive land-use strategies is not accessible to users and decision-makers. Long-term trend monitoring and analysis that correlates climate, rangeland and livestock management with livestock productivity and disease prevalence is lacking. This lack of data prevents evidence-based decision making and accurate GHG inventory and targets related to AP-DLU at the macro and local levels (addressed in Output 1.4).


---
**Page 53**
---

71. Lack of policy-enabling platforms on climate-resilient rangeland and livestock management.

- Community-based Natural Resource Management (NRM) policies have focused on tourism and hunting activities and excluded village livestock grazing areas, leaving these communal areas in an unclear regulatory environment and subject to the impacts of maladaptive land use practices.
- National policies for climate-smart agriculture (CCARDESA) and forest restoration (AFR100), national policy actors in Botswana have no peer networks for climate smart restoration and livestock management. This is a gap that needs to be filled with lessons learned from other countries to improve policy and practice (addressed in Output 1.5).

72. Investments aimed at reducing vulnerability of communal farmers are reactive rather than proactive.

- Drought relief and price incentives to encourage sales (Beeftok offtake) from communal lands are only implemented after the official declaration of a drought.
- The level of funding spent on drought relief by the government of Botswana over the last decades has increased significantly (see Figure 4 - drought declarations). Between July 2019 and June 2020, the government allocated $3.5 million to drought relief. This is an important investment to address impacts from the 2019 drought[17]. Government concentrating its efforts and resources on reactive measures such as drought relief has not been effective in addressing rangeland restoration labour that could prevent or mitigate the effects of droughts and floods (addressed in Output 1.5 and Output 2.1).

73. Lack of scientific understanding of linkages between rangeland restoration, climate smart livelihood practices, and their ecological impacts, social and co-benefits.

- Productivity on rangelands is driven by complex factors and rangeland ecosystems are often driven by natural variability.
- The interaction between climate, ecology, economics, and cultures on rangelands is intricate[18], making it challenging for land users to predict the effects of management practices. This lack of understanding can lead to overgrazing, reduce biodiversity development of management systems that affect rangeland condition, health, and productivity.
- Understanding these linkages is crucial for expanding carbon mitigation and adaptation potential from rangelands in Botswana and throughout Africa (Addressed in Outputs 1 and 2.2).

74. Trade barriers for communal farmers exist and have detrimental impacts on livelihoods.

- Geographic-zoning disease management approaches discriminate against good livestock management practices and exacerbate human-wildlife conflicts, and all farmers in a zone are penalised equally during disease outbreaks[19]. A new national traceability system has become operational but is not yet fully implemented. This is an area where the private sector can play a role through market-based solutions.
- Implementation of the new OIE Commodity-based Trade Standard could be rolled out in a way that further favours the existing commercial beef herd and deepens the climate vulnerability of communal farmers (Addressed in Output 1.2, 1.4, and 3.2).


---
**Page 54**
---

76. The Project aims to overcome these barriers and realise its Theory of Change through eight pathways that are aimed at enabling beneficiaries to implement actions that permanently reverse rangeland degradation trends and improve livestock management on Botswana’s communal grazing lands. These include: (i) the implementation of improved land use planning; (ii) EBA actions with income generated climate-resilient rangeland product value chains as presented in Table 12.

Table 12: Actions to combat impacts of climate change in targeted rangelands that will be the focus of the Project enabling pathways

| Actions that improve climate-resilience and reduce GHG emissions on communal grazing lands | Actions that improve climate-resilience and reduce GHG emissions on "Botswana Product™" value chains |
|---|---|
| * active ecological restoration management (erosion control, bush cleaning, ponding, etc.) | * enterprise development related to new livestock enterprises such as sheep and Ecoranger base-station production, cattle production, buffalo production, bush-fodder production, fodder production |
| * grazing and movement management that controls overgrazing and reduces concentrating manure in degraded areas as a mechanism for soil regeneration | * strategic manure management from livestock enterprises (e.g. composting/restoration/composting) for emissions reduction |
| * implementation of planned seasonal rest from grazing to allow land regeneration | * adoption of water saving cleaning products and technologies |
| * improved animal health and nutrition that leads to better leadership and reduced mortality rates | * transition to renewable energy sources for livestock enterprises |
| * removal of unproductive animals | * improved efficiencies in animal and product production |
| * transition to more resilient livestock breeds | * diversification into low-emissions livestock enterprises (e.g. sheep, goats, api-rangers) where desired by stakeholders and economically feasible |

* Rangeland product value chains targeted by the project may include natural fodder development, restoration enterprises, veterinary services, etc., depending on the specific needs of each community. The goal is to ensure that these enterprises are financially viable and sustainable as a result of the presence of wildlife; there is currently no opportunity to sell or manage commercial livestock due to FMD legislation.
