77. In combination, these Pathways create and sustain the conditions for the adaptation and mitigation impacts of rangeland restoration. Gender responsiveness and social equity are embedded in all three pathways, and open communication and science-based adaptive management underpin their delivery.

1. Building the capacity of communities to understand and formally agree to collective land and livestock management (training, mentorship, and legal support);
2. Expanding the capacity of government to provide efficient and effective support to EBA in communal rangelands (human resources, technical and operational support);
3. Providing marginalized individuals with new employment and self-development opportunities through training, mentoring, upgrading and job trade opportunities (new accredited training programme for herders, restoration workers, and graduate monitors);
4. Enhancing access to information and analysis for adaptive management of rangeland systems (new IT system and user training);
5. Contributing to an enabling policy environment (policy briefs and facilitating dialogues);
6. Deploying a transformative human resource programme that enables climate resilient rangeland restoration and management and monitoring co-financed by the Botswana biodiversity programme (Ecotagrader, restoration worker, graduate monitor wages, and in-service training);
7. Broadening community participation and sustaining benefits from climate-resilient livestock value-chains (training, financing partnership development support, local-level fund mechanisms);
8. Facilitating financial policy and market shifts to expand rewards for climate-resilient livestock management and technology adoption (industry standards, private sector supply protocols, and consumer awareness/demand).

78. Through these actions, the following outcome-aligned results and co-benefits will be realised and captured in the project’s logical framework statement and paradigm shift:

1) Component 1 results and co-benefits: climate and rangeland information will be available and used by stakeholders to increase climate resilience and reduce GHG emissions; village-level targets and conditions for meeting commodity-based trade standard requirements will be established, and a knowledge management system that facilitates data gathering and analysis enhances planning ability and knowledge of climate mitigation and adaptation. The development of national policies and programmes support more inclusive sustainable development; regional and national platforms share lessons learned for replication in similar communal rangeland areas.

2) Component 2 results and co-benefits: Professional herders are trained and deployed in communal rangelands with re-deployed government funding for job creation and inclusive

last mile beef value-chain development; Improved rangeland management increases natural plant cover (decreased bare ground and bush encroachment), increases presence of palatable species that provides more resilient fodder resources for livestock-based livelihoods. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 53

## Global Context

### Objectives
- Enhance climate change adaptation and mitigation efforts in developing countries.
- Promote sustainable development by reducing greenhouse gas emissions.

### Outcomes
- Increased resilience to climate impacts through improved infrastructure and services.
- Enhanced capacity for climate change management at national, sub-national, and local levels.

## Co-Specifics

### Objectives
- Ensure alignment with the Green Climate Fund's strategic objectives.
- Foster collaboration among stakeholders in project implementation.

### Outcomes
- Strengthened partnerships between government agencies and civil society organizations.
- Improved coordination of climate change initiatives across sectors.

## Results

### Project Outputs
1. Enhanced capacity for climate change adaptation at national level.
2. Increased access to clean energy technologies.
3. Improved disaster risk management systems.

### Project Outcomes
1. Reduced vulnerability to climate-related disasters.
2. Enhanced resilience in critical infrastructure and services.
3. Strengthened institutional frameworks for climate action.

## Project Outputs

### Output 1: Enhanced capacity for climate change adaptation at national level
- Capacity building programs for government officials on climate change mitigation strategies.
- Development of national climate change action plans.

### Output 2: Increased access to clean energy technologies
- Installation of renewable energy systems in public buildings and schools.
- Promotion of energy-efficient appliances and lighting.

### Output 3: Improved disaster risk management systems
- Establishment of early warning systems for natural disasters.
- Strengthening of emergency response capabilities at local levels.

## Project Outcomes

### Outcome 1: Reduced vulnerability to climate-related disasters
- Decreased frequency and severity of weather-related events.
- Enhanced preparedness for extreme weather conditions.

### Outcome 2: Enhanced resilience in critical infrastructure and services
- Protection of water supply systems from flooding.
- Strengthening of power grids against storms.

### Outcome 3: Strengthened institutional frameworks for climate action
- Development of policies to support sustainable development.
- Increased public awareness about the importance of climate change mitigation.

## Benefits and Risks

### Benefits
1. Improved quality of life through reduced exposure to climate-related hazards.
2. Economic growth driven by increased investment in renewable energy sectors.
3. Enhanced global reputation for environmental leadership.

### Risks
1. Political instability affecting project implementation.
2. Lack of public support leading to low participation rates.
3. Technical challenges in scaling up successful interventions.

## Assumptions

### Assumption 1: Adequate funding and resources are available throughout the project duration.
- Funding will be provided by the Green Climate Fund as per the approved budget.

### Assumption 2: Stakeholder engagement is effective and continuous.
- Regular meetings with key stakeholders to ensure alignment of objectives and progress tracking.

### Assumption 3: Technological advancements support successful implementation.
- Availability of advanced technologies for climate change mitigation and adaptation.

B.3. Project programme description

80. The Project goals are to increase populations' resilience to climate change and reduce Greenhouse Gas Emissions in Botswana's communal rangelands and livestock product value chains through enabling and monitoring gender equitable governance, climate-resilient livestock farming, and engaging the private sector. Specifically aligned with the GCF Performance Management Framework, the Project will:

- Strengthen institutional and regulatory systems for climate-responsive planning and development (A5).
- Strengthen adaptive capacity and reduced exposure to climate risks through innovative livestock production support, rangeland restoration, employment and career development, and enabling market transformation (A7).
- Strengthen the implementation of gender equitable land use reduction processes (A8).
- Improve land and livestock management in resilient multi-species (livestock and wildlife) ecosystems that contributes to emissions reductions (M9).

81. The project approach is focused on capacity building at the individual, community, and institutional levels through a participatory and collaborative adaptive approach. This requires cohesive, intensive interventions that build links between people, communities, and institutions and creates an informed network that develops common experience, tools, and learning. The Project will draw from its own experience as well as from its organizational experience and lessons learned from similar programs. CI and project partners will also engage in a continuous dialogue with communities and institutions on an ongoing basis throughout Project implementation. This level of direct engagement is necessary to achieve the ambitious goals of the Project.

Project Phasing and Implementation Clusters

82. The Project will be implemented over three distinct phases, In the Foundational Phase, tools and systems will be developed and tested at nine demonstration sites, selected based on their "readiness" (see Feasibility Assessment, Section 4 for more detail). Simultaneously, a private sector engagement strategy will be developed and tested. Site Replication (Site) will prepare for rangeland rehabilitation roll out in these sites, and during the third phase, Project implementation will be scaled up across the nine demonstration sites across the Project sub-district. Private sector interest and sourcing from Project sites is expected to increase over the three Project implementation phases: Phase 1 will develop and test tools and systems at nine demonstration sites; Phase 2 will scale up to a low-to-moderate annual output of 500-1,000 animals per year by two local abattoirs, as product quality improves and demand increases. This will also be accompanied by improved livestock condition and farmers begin to understand market systems; and Phase 3 will attract larger licensed abattoirs in the Project areas to source an increased quantity of animals from participating farmers.

83. This Project timing, based on community consultations and the lessons learned from Herding for Health in South Africa, is realistic and allows adequate timing for engagement and capacity building to ensure the Project's success in creating transformational impact through demonstrated behaviour change. The final six months will provide a wrap-up period

### Component 1: Strengthening institutions and support systems for climate responsive planning and management.

85. A review of relevant national policies and programmes, and consultation with stakeholders, revealed limited sectoral coordination, lack of management or local management structures, overexploitation of natural resources, and a lack of integrated information systems to inform climate responsive planning on communal lands[1]. These factors were found to compromise effectiveness of natural resource management and livelihood development on communal rangelands. To address these shortcomings, the activities within this component aim to enable communities to make and enact decisions that are informed by the Project’s findings and recommendations. Ensuring policy-makers are aware and using information from the Project to create an enabling environment is critical and provides an opportunity for the Project to influence national policies on land use planning, reducing poverty, increasing access to education, reducing unemployment, and increasing human-wildlife conflicts. This component is aligned with GCF Priority 1: “Strengthening adaptive capacity of communities in climate change responsive planning and development (AS)” and “Strengthened awareness of climate threats and risk reduction.”

86. Component 1 of the Project will facilitate alignment of decision-makers, support institutions through technical assistance, and develop tools that contribute to consistent and effective approaches to building new stewardship capacity for communal rangelands. This component is foundational for the Project and will be a primary focus for the first two years.

[1] FS Section 4, Appendix 4.6 (Cassidy et al., 2018).

Of Project implementation. The activities under this component contribute to the following outputs:

1. New gender equitable structures and systems for climate responsive planning and implementation by communal populations are operationalised;

Key Deliverable: Eighty percent (80%) of communal populations in target regions understand the role of VDCs, Village Development Committees; Farmers Associations and Community Trusts to plan and govern collective land and livestock management.

87. Village Development Committees (VDCs) are the most local level of government recognised by the Government of Zimbabwe for the purpose of receiving funding.
As such, VDCs are the vehicle that determines the “public works” that will be prioritized for the government’s Livelihood Programme and make all applications to the government Livelihood Programme. In addition, VDCs are also responsible for implementing community structures, such as traditional councils. Village Development Committees also have legislated powers in terms of land use planning and management.
VDCs are a key vehicle to embed the Project activities to avoid duplication and enhance sustainability. However, the effectiveness (and representativeness) of the VDCs in the project sites ranges greatly from site to site. The VDCs in some sites are very effective while others have common feature across all consultations. C is aware of the risk of “elite-capture” even in these research findings. In addition, capacity building and training for VDC members and capacity-development are part of the project design to address this risk.
Additionally, several actions recommended in the ESMP (Annex 6) and GAP (Annex 8) to structure engagement with VDCs and other community structures are being implemented. These include ensuring inclusivity, gender-equity, and leadership accountability. It is possible, and even probable that some of these actions will be more challenging than others in the short term as they are implemented in some communities due to the complexities of community power dynamics.
In these cases, the Project will engage other functional local structures to achieve the outcomes for the Project. The ESMP (Annex 6) and GAP (Annex 8) recommend that VDCs be adopted at the VC level. Utilising the experiences, methods, and tools developed in the demonstration sites, the Project will work with the Ministry of Land Reform and Resettlement to develop a model for adoption by the VC level. The model will be piloted in priority Village Grazing Areas - with an estimated 44 VDCs reached by year 3; and 104 by year 6. The target outcome is 80% of all 104 VDCs are engaged in active Rangeland

## Stewardship Agreements as a mechanism for rangeland governance and continued development of Stewardship Agreements for Botswana's communal rangeland climate adaptation plan.

The purpose of this funding is to transition stakeholders who are used to "Project-based thinking" (i.e., short-term projects) into a more long-term, real transformational thinking about building a rural restoration economy. The development of these agreements will be facilitated by the Farmer Facilitation Team (FFT), which is essential for sustainable adaptation at scale in these vulnerable areas.

The facilitation session will also provide a platform where Project staff can co-develop implementation strategies with FFT members and other stakeholders, including visits to each cluster from a main "urban village" for the Farmer Facilitation Teams. This will be provided as part of the project's capacity building activities on the value of these exchanges to building a network with strong farmer engagement skills.

### Activity 1.1.2 Build collective understanding and equally empower male and female participation in Rangeland Stewardship Agreements within Botswana’s legal and governance framework

In line with FPIC guidelines and all ESS and GGP recommendations presented in the proposal, the Project will introduce the use of Rangeland Stewardship Agreements (RSA) as the backbone of a new communal rangeland ecosystem management approach. The RSA is a Community Agreement for Conservation Areas (CAC) tool used by CI and others globally to build and enforce sustainable management of community-held natural resources. They are particularly effective for responding to unanticipated changes in climate, land use, or other factors that can affect the ability to adjust management requirements more quickly than a legislative approach and are therefore well suited as an alternative to traditional communal land tenure systems negotiated directly with the land users of a site and therefore can integrate indigenous knowledge systems into management strategies. Stakeholders felt that RSA was a better form for negotiating for communal rangeland use in Botswana’s legal and governance framework, especially in Communal Commons where only wildlife activities are allowed under the country's laws.

*A consensus is a gathering that empowers local communities as clients who work with others to develop the best interpretation of community needs. The process is most often utilized in agriculture, but CI has used this approach to design ecosystem strategies.

The Project will also seek advice from the Department of Land Resources under contract law, that the Project will seek to promote into new agriculture, rangeland management, and climate legislation (see Output 1\.5\).

The facilitation of training sessions on gender staff will be hired by C1 to work as part of the Project team to implement the ESMIP and GEM frameworks. Training events, specifically on the legalities and best practices for implementing Rangeland Stewardship Agreements, will be held at least once per year. Rangeland management will be fully incorporated into the Project and further embedded in all local institutions through leadership training led by sub-grantee Rare and public commitments to Rangeland Stewardship Agreements will be signed in a representative equity principles and validated through village-level signing ceremonies.

97\. VDCs’ Rangeland Stewardship Agreement signing ceremonies will take place within the target communities. Public commitment is recognized as a key motivator for behaviour change, and these events enable communities to celebrate their achievement in what can be a difficult consensus-building journey. These ceremonies are also an opportunity to build community capacity through the creation of long-term adaptive capacity and reduce vulnerability for marginalised members of society.

98\. Assessments will be gathered before and after each training event and captured in the Rangeland Stewardship Information Portal (see Output 1\.4) as a way that guides Future Facilitators to identify areas where further support is required, and to provide feedback on content and training. In this way, the Project puts ongoing, locally relevant empowerment of land-users at the core of its design.

99\. The success of communal rangeland management is directly correlated to quality and transparent governance. When a Rangeland Stewardship Agreement is challenged or needs greater clarity for enforcement, Project staff will support legal assessment and guidance in key presentations and case law. Where required, C1 will also provide ongoing and continuous technical assistance and coaching to VDCs to proactively mitigate risks to the successful implementation of Stewardship Agreements. Trainers will be required to produce a series of training modules on rangeland management and conflict resolution for the Rangeland Stewardship Information Portal\. These modules will also be made available to the broader community for any individuals or organizations interested in pursuing self-development.

# Activity 1: Replicate and amplify Herding for Health (H4H) approach to develop locally appropriate Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA) Rangeland Stewardship Agreements, grazing plan designs, and support partnerships across Village Grazing Sites.

## 100. To maximise potential impact for relevant exchange programmes and other forms of help, CIs will initiate a demonstration site process in villages where VDCs and farmers are willing to serve as Demonstration Sites for their cluster. At training sites, these communities will become important learning centres for future rollout within their “clusters” and locations where the CI can demonstrate the benefits of Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA) approaches and adaptive management. Since demonstration sites need to be running by Year 2, a transparent process is required to identify and select appropriate demonstration site delivery partner (VDC, NGO, or CBO) who will be provided with resources through an in-kind grant. Each demonstration site delivery partner will be required:

* Sign up as a formal partner of the CI for the duration of the Green Climate Fund project and sets terms for coordination to achieve the project goals and workplan objectives;
* Submit a proposal for each demonstration site;
* Commit time to participation in training and lessons learned sessions;
* Identification of a dedicated community champion who is willing and able to be skilled as an EbA facilitator;
* Oversee and manage implementation at the site.

Any legal entity with an interest in a demonstration site may apply. Proposals may be submitted by VDCs themselves or by NGOs/CBOs on their behalf. The application will include evidence of a relationship between the entity and community (beyond simply evidence of a relationship with the community).

The application process will also request applicant entities to demonstrate their human and financial resources for covering these activities. Additional eligibility criteria will be:

* legal person status;
* track record working with targeted community;
* relevant experience in rangeland management;
* evidence of level of interest with target communities / stakeholders.

Should more than one applicant apply for a particular cluster and their capacities on the above be equal, crucial additional considerations will include:

* Project alignment with partner objectives and expertise/experience;
* Spatial prioritization (geographic position) for cost efficiencies for cluster level trainings

## 101. Rangeland Stewardship Agreements will be facilitated by the Farmer Focus Teams (FFT) established under the Green Climate Fund project. The FFTs are established and coordinated by CI and will include representatives of local government officials from the Ministry of Agriculture, Ministry of Gender Affairs, Land Boards, and local NGOs and CBOs. There is a need for the FFT to facilitate the process where the CI, VDCs, and relevant Land Board enter into an agreement with CI to enable its effective implementation in the field. The FFT will also be responsible for monitoring these plans to ensure compliance with project criteria as well as ESS considerations.

The fundamental elements of each Rangeland Stewardship Agreement (RSA) include:


---
**Page 67**
---

### GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSALS V2.0 | PAGE 63

- **Spatially explicit restoration and communal grazing plans developed by farmers with support from MoA, DFRR, and the Project scientific team (Cl).** Climate change considerations will be included in the plan through identification and targeting areas most at risk of desertification, including those prone to droughts, floods, intense rainfall events, and areas that pose the greatest fire risk with projected wind and temperature changes.
- **Description of implementation support that will be provided by Ministry of Agriculture (trained Ecorangers, Monitors, and Restoration workers; Veterinary Support) and by Cl (Grazing Plan Development, Training, Monitoring, and Evaluation).** The Ministry of Agriculture will implement the grazing plan (see Annex 21 Operations and Maintenance Plan for short and long-term monitoring and evaluation). The Ministry of Agriculture will be a party to the RSA for purposes of providing its implementation support.
- **Requirements for participation by farmers include that their animals become part of the village herd, which is managed under the terms of the grazing plan.** This will include livestock programmes; this will include participation in training events, and contributions to livelihoods development (e.g., through the provision of inputs).
- **Consensus-driven sanctions in the case of farmer non-compliance with RSA terms.**
- **A sustainability plan that describes what processes and commitments all stakeholders will make to ensure the long-term success of the grazing plans, subject to the post-project implementation period based on local conditions and capacities.**

The RSAs will define the benefit package resulting from the community negotiation process, with final approval from Cl. The goods and services ultimately provided to each Village through the RSA will be determined by the following factors: (1) what is required (e.g., number of Ecorangers, what kind of restoration team and tools, what kind of livestock management support is needed, what kind of training is required for village members; etc.); (2) what level of commitment the community is providing to support the implementation of the grazing plan (e.g., a water boiler will only be provided with full commitment from the community in terms of maintenance and use of the facility); and (3) what level of support is required to ensure equitable participation by indigenous peoples, women, and other vulnerable groups. The benefit package may also include something that is locally required to ensure equal opportunity for participation and benefitting). More detail on the process and how commitments to land and livestock management will be made can be found in Section 4 and its appendices, as well as in Figure 18 below.

To enable single parents and women to participate in the programme, partnerships will be developed with local organizations that provide enrolled childcare as part of the benefit package. For an RSA, Cl will select an appropriate service provider for villages where this service is not already available through Cl's standard procurement processes. This will ensure that all eligible families are able to participate fully and equally in the project (e.g., as Ecorangers or as part of a Restoration Team) and will be a key part of the Project support to Rangeland Stewardship efforts.

The institutions approved of Rangeland Stewardship Agreements will follow the procedures established by the Livestock Management and Infrastructure Development (LMID) program—whereby VDCA submit proposals for the agreement after receiving guidance from Cl. The agreements are then reviewed by District Animal Production Officers, and are approved by District Animal Production Offices. After approval by the District Agriculture Coordinators, the agreements are sent to the Ministry of Agriculture for

# Rangeland Stewardship Agreement Implementation

## Figure H1a: Rangeland Stewardship Development Process

### Rangeland Stewardship Agreement Components

#### Rangeland Stewardship Agreement — MoA, Land Board, VSC, All Livestock Owners
- Legally binding agreement that a development partner (Land Board) enters into with the community to support implementation of land use plans.
- Agreements will be signed by the Community Development Officer and Village Area Coordinator.
- The agreement is valid for 10 years.

#### Grazing Support Package — "In kind Agreement"
- Legal agreement between Conservation Trust Fund and the VSC, Land Board, and Livestock Owners.
- This agreement provides a framework for supporting livestock grazing in accordance with the Rangeland Stewardship Agreement.
- The agreement is valid for 10 years.

#### Private Sector "Supplier Contract"
- Legal agreement between the livestock owner and the private sector supplier.
- This agreement provides a framework for implementing the grazing support package.
- The agreement is valid for up to five years, with an option to extend by one year at a time.

Building the capacity of government to coherently govern change through improved communication, coordination, and deployment of support Programmes that will proactively reduce national GHG emissions and build adaptive capacity - which will become increasingly critical as climate change impacts worsen over time. #### 108. CI will identify and facilitate complementary learning collaborations between VDCs and broader agricultural land, water, and health strategies. The Project team (CI) will ensure representation of all relevant actors and provide a platform for the opportunity to share their priorities and are involved in grazing strategy development and complementary training on livestock management and animal husbandry. CI will also support livestock production. In the first year of the project, the Project Lead and Area Managers (CI staff) will engage with national departments to further embed and align Project activities and monitor progress against agreed targets. The MoA will be responsible for coordination with Ministry: Land, Water and Sanitation; and the Ministry of Health. The MoA will support opportunities of leveraging opportunities through representations of the project at Rural Extension Coordination Committee (RECC) meetings and Regional Development Coordination (RDC) coordination meetings.

#### 109. Known potential collateral benefits from prior funding for Health Project studies and local interventions provide insight on how the Project team can further leverage adaptation and mitigation benefits within the Project. For example, depositing manure on old croplands to enhance soil carbon sequestration potential and climate-resilient crop production; improving water use efficiency in livestock management through VDCs as part of their livestock management strategy, constructing new boreholes for watering animals away from rivers and streams, and providing alternative water sources for livestock; using water bearer deployment for optimal use by livestock and wildlife, and integration of de-worming activities to break the cycle of the tapeworm/cattle worm life cycle. The latter is particularly important for market access as cysts in the tapeworms are not accepted in formal markets and prevents private sector actors from sourcing from commercial farms.

#### 110. This activity will deliver at least one new partnership Per Project area with the relevant ministries and will further strengthen institutional delivery of a coordinated response to reducing climate vulnerability of target communities through the establishment of a climate-resilient livestock management system, which is an important component of the project.

### Activity 1.2.2 Support the development of a Rangeland Stewardship job creation initiative under the Ipleseanga Programme within the Ministry of Agriculture and Local Government

CI will work with the Ministry of Agriculture and Ministry of Local Government to develop and deploy a transformation strategy and legislative engagements for the (Ipleseanga) programme to implement job creation for rangeland restoration and Ecosystem-based Adaptation in the target area. CI will also support the Ministry of Agriculture and Ministry of Local Government with experienced government programme specialists to design and test new regulations and standard operating procedures for the Ipleseanga Programme. CI will develop training and deployment mechanisms for the first 500 EcoRangers and Restoration workers for the new initiative in Year 1 and at the demonstration sites (Phase 1: 500 workers). Over the following years, CI will support the Ministry of Agriculture and Ministry of Local Government to employ a total of 2,300 workers over two periods, intake 3-phase (2: Phase 2) and 1500) and phase 6 (Phase 3: 800 Phase 3) that will employ 700 workers. CI will also support the Ministry of Agriculture and Ministry of Local Government providing to Mok on the creation of SOPs and implementation systems for the (Ipleseanga Rangeland Stewardship Programme based on CI's experience in a similar job creation initiative in South Africa. CI will also provide technical assistance to the Ministry of Agriculture in Activity 2.1.1 training of (Ipleseanga) workers). CI will also support the Ministry to develop annual work plans and budgets for the Ipleseanga programme. CI will also provide funding from universities in Botswana who undertake studies on the efficacy and efficiency of the programme at building climate resilience and reducing poverty as contributions to project reporting. The project will also include a component to monitor co-benefits and measuring 'technical effectiveness' of the interventions is a key element of sub-activity 12.2.3. CI will provide technical assistance in the development of monitoring tools (including COVID prevention and response) and Personal Protective Equipment relevant to most natural resource management interventions can guide the development of similar proposals for the Ipleseanga initiatives both during and after the project period.

### Activity 3.2: Expand capacity of Ministry of Agriculture, Department of Veterinary Services to respond to climate-induced diseases and infections and enable Commodity-Based Trade in the Project Areas

112. To address one of the key risks and barriers to adaptation and market access by communal farmers, the Project aims to significantly increase animal health management capacity across the target areas, noting that improved animal health is known to translate into both mitigation (e.g., reduced emissions from livestock) and adaptation benefits (e.g., increased development, infrastructure, and human resource capacity to address the needs of communal farmers). The Department of Veterinary Services (DVS) will be supported to refine their protocols and procedures to include current and projected climate change impacts and a capacity building plan for DVS staff. This will be done in close collaboration with the DVS Facilitation Team. The Project will also contribute to key resource capacities for meeting trade standards, including through support from the Department of Trade and Industry.

113. As the science of climate change and veterinary health and disease control is rapidly evolving, CI will provide drones to the DVS and refrigerators to each village (many vaccinations and veterinary medication require refrigerated storage), and thus local response capacity for climate-induced diseases and infections. The DVS will be supported to respond to climate-induced increases in disease outbreaks. CI will also provide network support to the DVS to ensure that they are able to access information on new vaccines, new veterinary staff to build understanding of the positive and negative impacts of various land and grazing management activities on livestock health in coordination with Ecorangers deployed by CI.

114. CI will engage a consultant to help the DVS develop regulatory protocols that consider climate-induced risks in routine checks, particularly those that can enable Commodity-Based Trade (CBT) compliance in the context of climatic conditions (i.e., different regulations for different seasons). The DVS will be supported to access information on regulatory checks based on climate situations that drive disease risk. This will include training for DVS staff and Ecorangers, as well as support from Animal Health staff who will be trained in these tools, research results, and protocols at annual Project Learning Weeks. As with the communities, DVS staff will also be supported to create a database of best practices for climate-induced disease control, including using lessons learned from Rare trainings. These experiences will be captured as part of the Project’s Knowledge Management Framework. The Project will support Ecorangers in quarantine management and technology-enabled use of drones for vaccines and medicine distribution**.

Oxfam’s New Rangeland management curriculum developed and operationalised to expand skills for conservation and regenerative grazing. Key Deliverable: An Ecoranger Training Curriculum that is formally registered and embedded in national Rangeland Stewardship programme.

115. Botswana has a depth of agricultural and natural resource expertise that is inaccessible to

### Activity 1.3.1: Create and monitor deployment of a new national curriculum for climate-resilient livestock herding to build adaptive capacity at the individual and community level.
#### 116. Activity 1.3.1 will draw on information from traditional herding practices and academics to integrate into a new national curriculum for climate-resilient livestock herding. This will be an education programme that will become a required component of the (peleigeng) Ecorangar programme. Skills used and trained in the broader H4H programme for restoration (as provided by Buan-CiCE) will also be integrated into this new curriculum, including the Fire Restoration Worker curriculum which will fire management strategies such as grazing of fine brushwood, controlled burning of grasslands, and firebreaks to prevent fire spread, cooling fires in the rangelands, and basic fire-response skills for safety and risk reduction.

#### 117. The formal certification process of the curriculum will bring dignity and new skills to the individuals employed in the public works programme and the practical nature of the course will be supported by a range of field visits and practical training sessions. It is envisaged that expert-supervised courses projects that support implementation of Rangeland Stewardship, Fire Management, and Livestock Health and Disease Control strategies will be selected for the training programme and so the Project will provide support for climate resilience at both the household, community, and ecosystem level. Institutionally, it will also allow Buan-CiCE to expand its capacity in the field of education by establishing its Centre for In-Service and Continued Education (Buan-CiCE) to include lessons learned in the implementation of its teaching and modernize its former university programme to include climate considerations.

#### 118. CI will enter into a Grant Agreement contract with Buan-CiCE to implement this activity. CI will be the Executing Entity for this activity, as it will retain decision-making authority by approving the curriculum design and development of training materials and the rapid form for this activity. These terms will be explicit in the grant agreement between CI and Buan-CiCE.

#### 119. Buan-CiCE will use the first year of the Project to finalise the development of a national qualification for Ecorangars, as well as certificate courses for Restoration Workers.[^15] Buan-CiCE will also develop a curriculum that is aligned with the National Curriculum and substance acceptable to CI, with the Ministry of Agriculture for the implementation of the training in Activity 2.1, as it is envisaged that this activity will be integrated into the monitoring and evaluation framework during the project period. Drawing on the support of the Herding for Health Training Collaboration, which includes the African Holistic Livestock Centre, the South African Department of Agriculture, and the University of Pretoria, Buan-CiCE will work with a specialist consultant team to design a curriculum that provides progressive training in eight core skills[^16] over the three-year implementation period of peleigeng job

122. A Rangeland Stewardship Information Portal will consolidate existing and emerging databases (e.g., BRIMP, StatsBotswana Economic Impact of Drought) with Project generated data to provide a key tool for tracking climate change impacts on communal rangelands and the livelihoods of pastoralists in Botswana. The information provided by the portal will be used at all levels of government to inform policy and programmes over the short-, medium- and long-term, including the development of national strategies. At the local level, local and regional levels will be important as the impacts of climate change become more severe over time. The portal will become a central information management database that can be accessed by pastoralists, extension officers, government officials, and researchers to better understand existing Eta effectiveness and intervention options on mitigating GHG emissions from livestock production in Botswana. The Portal will be developed under the Project period, but the management of the Portal (with CI approval and oversight) may transition to the appropriate government entity after the mid-term review. The Project will also work with the relevant stakeholders to ensure that the data collected by the portal is so that the Portal can measure regional economic impacts of the Project relative to historic data and other regions.

### Activity 1.2: Establish a Rangeland Stewardship Information Portal as a tool for climate-responsive planning and monitoring

**123.** CI will create a new Rangeland Stewardship Information Portal to consolidate information that facilitates planning and development of climate resilient rangelands. The portal will link land management data with existing systems in collaboration with MoA, DPFR, and linked to the Department of Meteorological Services (MET) early warning system. The purpose of the integrated system will be to capture information on rangeland degradation, as well as wildlife population trends, and to provide a platform for the National Rangeland Monitoring Assessment system and MoA (Selegong Wage Accounts) to provide monthly monitoring data on livestock numbers and grazing pressure. The portal will also link existing web-based applications to a central report-generating database that is housed in the cloud and accessible to all departments requiring or inputting information.

The tool will be used to support the implementation of this activity when capacity mandate, and portal design requirements are better defined.

**124.** A primary use of the Portal will be to support Farmer Facilitation Teams to co-develop eligible projects for climate change adaptation and mitigation in rangelands. The use of the Portal to manage information, monitor job creation investment impacts, and generate reports for national and international obligations is pioneering and transformational. Visualisation tools will be developed that are similar to those used by the Department of Water and Forestry on South Africa’s Working for Water: Water Information Management System (WIMS) but embedded in a rangeland context. The Portal will also provide an opportunity to build on the last decade. It can also increase update of Botswana Animal Identification System for livestock traceability (BATS) - drawing on lessons from Namibia's traceability system. The Portal is also expected to support the development of climate change adaptation strategies that measure the impact of climate and herding on ecosystem health, livestock health, and human livelihoods in rangelands. It will also provide a platform for the collection and measurement of this on regional economic resilience to climate change, particularly climate shocks **1**. Trends Earth will be used for change monitoring and linked to new Italian-funded weather stations. The portal will also be used as an information hub for graduate members in the field who augment evidence support behind remote-sensing data.

## Project staff and participants will iteratively design and create feedback reports to be provided via VDCs and/or other platforms such as the Green Climate Fund’s (GCF) Land Stewardship Information Portal on climate, livestock condition, rangeland health monitoring data, and other relevant information. These reports will be generated at least once a year. A communications team will spend 50% of their time working with farmers and experts to help design visualisation tools to present monitoring information back to farmers. The system will be designed so that the farmer can easily access this information and make changes provided, to document any change in adaptive capacity. The purpose of this effort is to expand the use of the VDCs beyond just data collection and reporting, to support collective and individual and collective decision-making and this analysis will be a core part of the graduate monitors and Project M&E team job functions. Should carbon financing be able to be linked with the information provided by the VDCs, then there should also be greater transparency to the community on the basis for calculations and associated payments.

## Complementary use of WiFi hubs with local entrepreneurs will also be encouraged where possible (see 3.1 and selection criteria for local entrepreneurs). ICT literacy training and a mentorship program will be provided by the service provider selected for the development of the Portal. During this phase, at least three different groups of users will be trained in the use of the system. The effectiveness will be assessed for resulting understandings (pre- and post-training assessments), uptake (number of times information is accessed), and perceived ease of use and usefulness. Lessons learned from these training sessions will be used to improve future training, which can then roll over for amplification sites. In addition to community-based users, special training on the use of the system will also be provided to 100 farmers and extension officers by the service provider to 460 local officials and 40 national officials. Graduate monitors will be used to support ICT and analysis efforts at key deployments as a means of bringing policy-makers into the process. Training will be provided by the Technical Director, her/his team, and a professional consultant team based in each region.

## An ICT consultant, based in the Districts, will be deployed to ensure ongoing support is in place for the VDCs. The ICT consultant will also provide training on the use of the system and will also focus on putting into place transition arrangements and plans for continued maintenance and use of the Information Portal after the Project implementation period.

Activity 2\.1\.1 Implement inclusive and gender-equitable recruitment, deployment, and in-service training of Itelegeng Encroancers, Restoration Worker Teams, and Graduate Members as part of the Livelihoods Development Programme.


137\. In 2016, the Office of the President Poverty Eradication Unit suggested that the employment investment co-finance for a rangeland stewardship project should be over 50% (of total Itelegeng) and not less than 40%. At the time of implementation of the project, Itelegeng was employing 60,000 people per month and as a result, the project proposal developed by the VDCs included a target to employ at least 35% (of total Itelegeng) in rangeland stewardship. This would be equivalent to over 21,000 people employed under the Itelegeng employment. At the same time during concept development, the director expressed that because the majority of Itelegeng beneficiaries are women, most beneficiaries will be women and therefore the Project should target women as a priority. The total financial set was for 3,600 women. From (Itelegeng), a total of 5,500 individuals will become Encroancers, 1200 will become Restoration Workers, and 480 will be employed under the Grazing Area through activity 2\.2\.1) and 500 graduate monitors that will be deployed via Activity 2\.1\.2 on all activities described in Output 2\. These targets provided the framework for community-based rangeland stewardship. The Project will provide training to Encroancers, VDC traditional authorities, farmers, and broader communities (see Annexes 7, 8, and 9). PPG commitments include that Encroancers will be responsible for managing their land on their own safety, but also given their household responsibilities. For this reason, the Project includes a component to provide training in skills such as livestock management, crop production or, at least return home at night, and can be paid for task-based activities such as sawing of restoration mats, filling of erosion gullies, thinning of bush encroachment, or ponding of short-term water sources. The Project will also include a component to provide training in Stewardship Programmes as designed in Output 1\.2 and to comply with conditions in this project document. In addition, the Project will include a component to provide stipends to women based on application submissions from the VDCs in target communities; applications will be completed with the support of the Farmer Facilitation Teams. 142. CI will enter into an in-kind grant agreement to provide technology and equipment**10** for VDCs, NGO partners, Ecorangers, and Restoration Teams to use to implement the agreed Rangeland Stewardship Agreement. These “Grazing Support Packages” will be based on a standard template that includes: mobile phones, laptops, tablets, GPS units, Ecoranger uniforms, mobile accommodations, mobile water solutions, monitoring technology, as well as routine maintenance.

143. CI will enter into an in-kind grant agreement with a grantee organization—NGO, CBO or farmer group—to provide the grazing support packages to the VDCs. The grantee organization will be responsible for procuring durable goods for the Grazing Support Packages from vendors (in accordance with its own procurement policies and procedures) and deliver them to the grantee organization. Per the terms of this agreement, the grantee organization will be required to maintain and distribute the goods to the implementers as per the project plan. The grantee organization will also be responsible for submitting activity monitoring reports. No funds are transferred to the grantee NGO/CBO/organization.

144. Project M&E and Farmer Facilitation Teams will undertake spot checks on treatment of the equipment procured as part of the in-kind grant agreement (Grazing Support Package, see Table 14). The spot check will be conducted at least once a year to ensure compliance with the terms of the Rangeland Stewardship Agreement. Non-compliance will result in the reassignment of goods procurements to another VDC. Beginning in Year 7 of the Project, disposition and maintenance plans will be developed by the Farmer Facilitation Teams and approved by CI. With GFI consumption data from the Ecoranger teams, CI will assess the condition of the equipment at the end of the Project based on the value of their successful participation in the Project reporting process and demonstrated motivation and capability to maintain it.

## Component 3: Promoting climate-sensitive enterprise development and value-chain investments to sustain transformational change

### 150. Complementary to the overall M&E system, CI will implement an Impact Evaluation (IE) better determine project-attributable impacts on the resilience of targeted populations and reductions in GHG emissions from livestock and increased rangeland sequestration**.

This IE will be implemented by a team of independent evaluators with expertise in Project staff, government partners, and beneficiary populations. The IE will rigorously compare outcomes between Project areas and non-Project areas to determine whether or not changes are taking place within the Project target areas and communities. By analyzing data on livestock health, offtake/death/reproduction rates, as well as measuring soil carbon levels, the IE team will be able to assess the effectiveness of interventions in Project areas.

### 151. Government and GCF investments in rangeland stewardship for climate change resilience building are catalytic in nature. Sustaining and growing adaptive capacity requires aligning value-chain incentives and supply-chains for rangeland products that are also resilient to the impacts of climate change. This can be achieved by providing technical assistance, training, and extension services are complements**; and promoting new value-chain development opportunities for Project areas. The GCF will support the implementation of a range of market-based approaches amongst economic activities, such as those presented in Table 12, will enhance adaptation and mitigation outcome sustainability in the Project areas as well as more broadly across the region.

### 152. Component 3 will leverage Project lessons into value-chain and finance policy transformation to support climate change resilience building interventions and the overall sustainability of the Project. This component will expand the Project contribution to the GCF.

#### Botswana Climate Change Commission and other Industry Players
CI will engage with key industry bodies, all five export platforms, and all local authority platforms and businesses in regular meetings and on-hosted events with DVD that promote climate-smart agriculture (CSA) practices and the benefits of CSA to farmers. CI will also facilitate farmer participation in Rangeland Stewardship Agreements as part of value-chain development.

#### Citizen Entrepreneurship Agency (CEA)
CI will work with the CEA, a public-private investment vehicle, to develop loan criteria that include climate-smart agriculture (CSA) practices and the benefits of CSA to farmers. The CEA will provide funding for CSA activities in Botswana through its existing lending portfolio and financing initiatives. Via the CEA, CI also will integrate climate-resilience awareness and training into its business development services. This will facilitate a critical component of the development of other value chains and regional economic resilience.

#### Enterprise Agency (LEA)
CI will work with the LEA to provide funding for CSA activities in Botswana through its existing lending portfolio and financing initiatives. Via the LEA, CI also will integrate climate-resilience awareness and training initiatives into its business development services. This will facilitate a critical component of the development of other value chains and regional economic resilience.

#### Meat Naturally Botswana
CI will continue to work with Meat Naturally as a strategic partner and secure the involvement of the communal farmers in the Project via the business stewardship / structure. Through Meat Naturally, CI will also provide funding for CSA activities in Botswana through its existing lending portfolio and financing initiatives. This engagement will facilitate a critical component of the development of other value chains and regional economic resilience.

### Output 3.1
Market readiness trainings, enterprise development support, supply chain facilitation, and local level funds build the enabling conditions for improved low-emission livestock value chains and regional economic resilience. Key deliverable: At least 176,000.

#### Activity 3.2.1 Design, implement, and measure impact of an awareness campaign on climate-change, low-emissions productions, and Rangeland Stewardship production with broader red-meat value chain, including consumers

**164.** The Project will run an information and awareness campaign aimed at generating commercial farmer interest in rangeland stewardship and the willingness to hire and engage with Erccangers and Restraint Teams; trade-partner support to reward communal farmers in Rangelands for their efforts towards increasing livestock productivity, income and resilience in meat communities that target community rangelands. Campaign strategy development will be supported through a sub-grant to the non-profit Rare to ensure these strategies are based on the science of behaviour change and are designed with an understanding of how to measure impact measurement. The campaigns will be run at local and national levels using an appropriate mix of communication channels. Rare will also design and implement baseline, mid-term and final assessments to ensure the campaign is reaching key segments of the broader red-meat value chain.

165\. In collaboration with ongoing NEF, FAO, GIZ, and European Union-supported efforts, the CGI will identify partners to be selected and at the cluster-level during Project implementation, will contribute criteria and case-studies for value-add certification of market segmentation from “climate-friendly meat” and associated livestock products (horns, hides, etc.), and will engage with relevant government agencies and civil society organizations who will engage with communities to support development of value chains for low-emission livestock production. CGI will also provide technical assistance to partners. CGI will engage a consultant team to produce a review of technological and financial opportunities related to building climate change resilience in the red meat value chain (e.g., improved feed efficiency, heat stress mitigation technologies, water-saving technologies and pricing strategies to incentivize climate-resilient livestock production). CGI will also be responsible for developing a business plan for Project beneficiaries as well as improve certification systems that include all value chains and explicit mitigation opportunities.

166\. The report will be made publicly visible and shared with relevant private-sector stakeholders. CGI will organize the launch workshop and follow up meetings with key government stakeholders, particularly the Botswana Meat Commission, the CEDA, and the Local Economic Development Authorities (LEDAs) in the Project areas as well as the District Project areas. To address the specific barrier of market access from Foot and Mouth Disease Zones of the Project target sites, CGI will support Department of Veterinary Services to focus on improving disease surveillance and control measures in these zones. CGI will also provide documentation support to the DVS for the policy-implementation dialogue meetings to ensure criteria are understood and implemented by Farmer Facilitation Teams, farmers, and by other stakeholders. CGI will also provide technical assistance to CEDA, which is available and shared with CEDA who will also be supported by the DVS CBT Director and the Enterprise Development Officer (EDO) in the Project areas. CGI will also provide support for financing applications for livestock farming throughout the country. In this way, the Project will unlock opportunities for communal farmers, but also establish a proactive plan to influence sustainable livestock and wildlife disease management practices throughout the country.

169. Conservation International Foundation (CI), through its CI-GCF Agency, will serve as the Accredited Entity (AE) for the Project. The CI-GCF Agency is responsible for the overall oversight of project implementation, including financial and administrative monitoring and supervision (through reporting, audits, and annual site visits) and review and approval of the Executing Entity’s (EE) annual workplans and budgets. CI-GCF will also be responsible for providing technical assistance to the EE in the development of the Project Implementation Plan, Project Results and Outputs, reporting to the GCF, and project closure and evaluation. CI-GCF will enter into a variety of agreements (including sub-grant agreements, services agreements, and MOUs) with the EE.

170. CI will self-execute this Project. CI Foundation, acting through its country office in Botswana (referred to in this FP as “CI or ‘CI Botswana’”),[256] will be the Executing Entity for all activities of this project. CI Botswana will be responsible for Project execution, management of sub-grant agreements with implementing partners, and reporting to GCF. CI Botswana will also ensure alignment of its work with the Government of Botswana policies and departmental contributions to achieve Project outcomes. The CI Botswana country office will have a dedicated team that will manage this project.

171. For more than 30 years, Conservation International (CI) has been protecting nature for the benefit of all. CI employs more than 1,000 people and works with more than 2,000 partners in 30+ countries to protect biodiversity and promote sustainable development. CI Botswana has undertaken interventions across 77 countries, protecting more than 601 million hectares of land, marine and coastal areas. CI’s work has also prevented the loss of 115,000 hectares of forest from deforestation in Botswana over the past two decades. CI has an extensive network of professional contacts in government, non-governmental organizations (NGOs), civil society, and private sector institutions. Current CI Botswana staff, with the assistance from CI’s Africa Field Division and CI HQ, will be responsible for the immediate mobilization and recruitment efforts after project approval. The CI Botswana country office has already established strong financial and operational systems in place for the implementation of an EU-CBT project and AFD projects. These systems are also being used to support the Nguniwa demostration site for the GCF project, and GCF project implementation will benefit from the LEU and AFD project management experience and systems.

BUAN-CICE has been providing quality in-service and continuing education in agriculture and natural resources since its establishment in 1995. BUAN-CICE designs, delivers and evaluates training programmes that are accredited by the Botswana Accreditation Council (BAC) for ISO 9001:2008 and other internationally recognised standards. It offers more than 50 course curriculums. ### Government Partners and Governance Structures:

179. The primary government ministry partner (Owner) for this Project will be the Ministry of Agricultural Development and Food Security (MoA). CI will enter into an MoU with the MoA for this project. The MoA will serve as the lead agency for the implementation of the project, including the development and Adaptive Management, Implementation, and Enterprise Development and Commodity-based Trade (CBT) Value Chains. CI-Botswana will also sit on the PSC and sub-committees. CI and the Government Partners will be represented in the PSC by a representative from each of the following: a variety of government departments, ministries as well as academic, traditional authorities, NGOs, private sector, and civil society organizations. The MoA will ensure that there is an open dialogue and buy-in from across government and stakeholders, as well as facilitate the distribution of project information to all relevant stakeholders in order to support successful project implementation. CI will ensure representation of large export abattoirs, small private abattoirs, and Meat Notch (MNP) processors on the PSC and sub-committees for the development of governance structures. ## Beneficiary Calculations and Selection Criteria at the Village Level

154. Interventions for Project beneficiaries at the village level will be selected based on the livelihood interests of the individual / household – whether livestock farming or interest in one of the alternative-livelihood rangeland product value-chain opportunities. Anyone living in the most vulnerable community who is interested and volunteering to the Project given

## GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 97

will be expected to participate in awareness and training engagements. In order to calculate the potential for beneficiaries from the Project, in the absence of recent data on communal farming households, the MoA Agricultural Coordinator for each district was asked what percentage of their farming households were estimated to be practicing some form of communal grazing lands. This percentage was then applied to each district population data to calculate an estimate of the number of communal grazing land users in each district.

Through local value-add and rangeland-associated economic activities, an additional 71,000 beneficiaries are expected, equalling 247,000 beneficiaries in total or 80% of the Project area (Annex B). The majority of these beneficiaries will be women. This is due to CCI’s experience in South Africa where social or value-chain barriers exist that prevent uptake of livestock and rangeland-based enterprises by men. In addition, the Gender Affairs and Local Enterprise Agency (as per the GAP strategy described in Annex 6) are key in the implementation arrangements to ensure women are aware and able to participate in the Project. The number of beneficiaries from communal grazing land users is estimated to be lower as a result of current socio-economic conditions and limited access to markets, credit, extension services, training, and improved stewardship employment and value-chain activities. Female beneficiaries are estimated to be 125,000 or 51%, nearly equal to the current ratio in the population of the Project areas.

### B.5 Justification for GCF funding request

166. Despite significant co-financial commitments, the Project requires GCF grant investment to catalyze action and address the complexity, urgency, and scale of investment required.

While government, donor, and private sector funding must be secured and aligned to support the proposed interventions, there is a need for additional resources to implement climate-smart approaches and multi-stakeholder coordination across communal rangelands and livestock production efforts to achieve transformational impact at scale. The government's commitment to provide 20% co-financing is critical in this regard.

Numerous departments and donor projects have the potential to contribute additional resources, but these are fragmented and not aligned with the Project. If these resources are coordinated and driven by awareness and proven mechanisms to promote climate resilience and ecosystem-based adaptation, the high levels of poverty among rural communities can be reduced significantly. The Project has a strong case for a reimbursable financial mechanism.

While statistics on average income for Project beneficiaries in Botswana were not available, the average annual per capita income in rural areas of Botswana in 2015 was approximately $10/month/household.[13]

187. The implications of climate change impacts for Botswana, where the government is already dealing with climate adaptation challenges, demands concerted action and support both locally and internationally to address the scale and pace required.

Botswana, as a highly exposed country in its culturally critical livestock sector in particular, needs to urgently promote and adopt transformative measures to respond to quite rapid changes in local weather patterns. The Project will provide a framework for scaling up these approaches, the urgency and scale of effort required to increase resilience for its most

[13] Statistics Botswana: Statistical yearbook 2015 - https://www.statsbot.org.bw/sites/default/files/publications/Statistical%20Year%20Book%202015.pdf


---
**Page 103**
---

### 188. The private sector seeks to engage and support climate resilient livestock production but does not have the resources to instigate transformational change. The private sector is unable to provide sufficient investment in new infrastructure and technology at a scale to abate degradation on communal rangelands and allow livestock from these lands to be exported. CDE will work with the private sector to develop and implement plans for new livestock supply opportunities for existing export operators in the Project areas, all of which are operating at significantly less than their operational capacity[20]. CI will also encourage the private sector to invest in climate-resilient and low-emission production by working with the largest national investor, CDEA, to create sustainable finance options and criteria as part of its investment strategy for climate-resilient and low-emission meat availability for domestic consumption in Botswana.

#### 8.6 Exit strategy and sustainability

### 190. Livestock market access incentives and resulting improvements to farmer incomes and contributions to local level support funds are the primary tools to promote ongoing maintenance of rangeland restoration activities and Ecoranger salaries after the initial donor investment period ends. The three private sector operators in the Project area (Botswana Agricultural Investment Company (BAIC), Ngamiland, or Batswana) have completed purchasing arrangements with RSA-compliant livestock producers. These agreements will ensure that the farmer commitments of the RSA are included in the purchase agreements between farmers and buyers, that incentives for conditional management are maintained, and that the RSA-compliance requirements are met. The Project will engage with the three private sector operators at Phase 3. Meat Naturally Botswana will be a business entity that must be set up as a legal entity within each lifespan; it will break even in year 3 and achieve a low but positive EBITDA margin of 3.7% by year 5[21]. During the start-up phase, Meat Naturally Botswana will be supported by Meat Naturally Africa, but ultimately will be a financially viable structure.

[20] Department of Veterinary Services, 21 Jan 2021: DVS monitors carcass numbers from at registered abattoirs in the country.
[20] Annex E. Appendix II A. Meat Naturally Botswana Business Plan


---
**Page 104**
---

191. Supported by growing demand for low-emissions meat, a key to Project sustainability will be securing and enabling traceability of high-environmental and social standards that are included in Rangeland Stewardship Agreements. Within the Ngamiland area, there is a demand surplus of 50 tonnes of meat products for the tourism sector in the Okavango Delta due to limited supply from local producers. The Project will work with BMC to ensure its willingness to pay for consistent quality of product, particularly when those products are sourced locally and supplied at competitive prices.

192. The Rangeland Information Portal (RIP) will provide a consolidated monitoring and evaluation system that will not only confirm impact of the GCF and government investments, but also act as conduit for additional ecosystem service payments that are directly tied to ecosystem services provided by rangelands (e.g., carbon sequestration, biodiversity, water indicators, gender equity, and poverty alleviation indicators). These payments are currently being considered for selected project sites in collaboration with Project stakeholders and Meat Naturally. The lessons learned and best impact practices that are identified will be brought into operationalising sustainability for this Project.

The Project impacts and activities will be sustained via expanded capacity of rangeland stewardship agreements, which will provide a framework for the following: 1) financial incentives for climate smart land and livestock management; and sharing lessons via targeted policy engagements to promote additional scaling and replication of Project activities. The riparian areas in the Okavango Delta are currently under pressure from overgrazing and red meat value chains are clearly visible and improved market conditions enable a range of new opportunities. In terms of financial sustainability (see Financial Viability Assessment Section 2), all the proposed EBA measures are financially attractive with a positive NPV value that will incentivize long-term implementation of the GCF and government co-finance. The Project will also provide technical assistance, capacity building, and operational resource provision to enable collective herd management. Returns across short, middle, and long term are expected to be significantly higher than the current scenario and certainly much higher than BAU practices in the face of climate change.

194. Awareness raising as a component of broader community engagement and empowerment is a critical component of the Project, as understanding and ownership underpins success beyond project implementation. The Project will engage with stakeholders at all levels through stakeholder engagement (following a Rights-based Approach) that will embed communication channels through Rangeland Stewardship Agreements that are transparently informed and reflective of community needs. The premise of stewardship agreements is that they are a fair deal, with conservation actions performed in exchange for benefits to the local population.

195. The Rangeland Stewardship Agreement documents, negotiated actions, and co-designed benefits have proven to be effective at promoting uptake and real behaviour change (see

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 2.0 | PAGE 101

## Farmers, VDCs, and Land Boards will be guided by the Farmer Facilitation Teams to design their own Rangeland Stewardship Agreements.

### Examples of agreements include:
- South Africa: Farmers agreed in the initial signing of the agreement that they would not sell or lease their land.
- Botswana: Farmers agreed to take over the employment of Ecorangers as a farmers' association once the three-year investment by the government in their training is complete. 

### Mechanisms for ensuring compliance:
- Financial incentives such as premium cattle contracts and grazing fees.
- Voluntary agreements with farmers associations providing more favorable payment terms for RSA-compliant farming practices.

### Sustainability considerations:
- These mechanisms will be discussed with all RSA participants in Botswana at the outset of the engagement process so that sustainability is considered from the beginning and not the end of the project. 
- The focus on three key incentives: financial compensation, motivation, and sustained implementation of regenerative land and livestock management.

### Reduced risk and loss:
- Reduced risk of loss of animals to drought and predation is often a sufficient incentive for farmers to sign onto a Rangeland Stewardship Agreement.
- In one study in 3 communities in Ngamiland, Botswana, 84% of the farmers had lost animals due to drought; 8.5% had lost animals to predation and 5.2% had lost animals to disease.

### Access to markets:
- Long-standing trade barriers for communal farmers and the need for the collective grazing systems and record-keeping access.
- Commodity-based Trade markets provide incentive for sustaining commitment to RSAs.
- CI has found that private sector partners can sustain the incentive by lifting the prices paid to measure livestock production, which is a key driver of compliance.

### Botswana as a partner:
- Botswana is one partner that has committed to converting RSAs into "supplier contracts."
- As part of this commitment, CI will work with the government and other private sector actors to solicit and encourage similar commitments.
- CI has engaged the BMC on RSA compliance price premiums and contributed the idea to the national Beef Cluster Development Project (BCDP) to include a premium for RSA-compliant cattle.

### Termination of government subsidy:
- In South Africa, the government only funds a given village grazing area for 3-4 years. 
- In Botswana, while grazing has not yet imposed a time limit on its support, it appears that this will need to be addressed in the future.


---
**Page 106**
---

197. The Project design also includes a standard review of site sustainability at the end of year 3 before a second three-year investment is made. This process enables incremental adoption and continued improvement in land management practices by farmers and their communities, based on their experience and awareness of both market and climate conditions. Where farmer or livestock numbers are low, it may be necessary to negotiate with the government for an additional sustainability plan to be negotiated. For example, a particular site could offer to become an Ecoranger Training hub in exchange for continuation of Ecoranger salary payments. In some locations, where there is no local community, it will be necessary to identify and train a community representative who can be supported by the government through the [paleging] programme may be necessary. This will be identified as part of the Project’s Market Assessment.

198. In addition to farmer contributions to a local level funds, tourism and livestock value chain partners will be solicited to provide a portion of their profits/revenues into these funds in recognition of the co-benefits the improved livestock management contributes to wildlife and value-added tourism. A detailed business plan for this fund as well as other sources of funding will also be developed during implementation including carbon financing, and/or from best practice projects. See Feasibility Study Section 4 Market Assessment and Sustainability Plan for more information.

199. While RSA sustainability plans will include funding for Ecorangers and associated restoration efforts, the Government of Botswana will likely continue to fund and expand the use of its [paleging] program to support the interventions developed and piloted by the Project. The paleging budget is currently about US$20 million per year and therefore it is expected to remain well-funded over the coming years. The [paleging] program provides a unique opportunity for Botswana to employ local people (currently 15,000 individuals per month for 3-month rotations) and the government is actively seeking professional development avenues for this programme. The contribution the programme will make as part of the Project’s sustainability plan is likely to be significant in terms of supporting implementation of the BUAN-CICE site-service training after the Project. By showing the success of the paleging program, the government has already demonstrated its commitment to mitigation goals; the government is likely to continue to fund this mechanism and increase its support over time. The current [paleging] Director is also recommending Ecorangers in this role as strategic partners.

200. During the Project period, CI will expose Botswana to other successful strategies for sustaining rangeland management labor and monitoring support as part of social protection investments. These include the Ethiopian Productive Safety Net Programme (EPNSNP) and the South African Natural Resource Management Programme™ Both have been implemented in areas with similar land use patterns to those found on communal and croplands and they provide good evidence of building household resilience in the long-term. Ethiopia continues to spend US$900 million per annum on its programme in support.
