## South Africa’s National Resource Management (SANRIM) programmes also provide a simple and cost-effective model for restoration programme.

The initiative, which is reviewed in Annex 2 Section 4 Appendix-4.7., shows the investment the government makes annually to their land restoration programmes, the return on the investment, and the policies as to how the money is spent. The SANRIM programme has been running since 1998 and is a result of collaborations with Conservation International, the Working on Ecosystems Programme (WEP), and the South African National Biodiversity Institute (SANBI). It provides development opportunity, with three-year support for wages and in-service training for Ecorangers. Periodic communication with the senior manager for the programme provided an estimated 10% per year increase in employment levels. The average annual income for years three for R362 (100%) per beneficiary (NRM currently employs 100,000 people annually) with no additional costs to government and a significant reduction of illegal land clearing activities after the investment. This shows that the contribution to broader unemployment and economic vulnerabilities to climate change are being sustained.

## The Project design embeds stewardship linkages to both direct and indirect chain incentives. Working with existing partnerships, C4I will explore value chain opportunities to maintain and encourage organic continuation and replication of the restoration techniques. At the end of the Project, C4I will have a suite of tools for scaling up the project, including a model for supplier contract agreements between farmers associations and private sector buyers to continue their relationship and ensure sustainability of supply and demand in the value chain.

Through the H4H project, C4I has worked with 100+ smallholder farmers on land restoration work and regenerative grazing practices (See Figure 18). C4I will also work with private sector partners to scale up these efforts. The Project will continue to support the Meat Naturally members, particularly during years 8 and 9 of the project. This strategy was proven effective in C4I’s H4H work in South Africa where former NGO extension officers now have permanent positions as part of the private sector.

## The Project strategic partner, Meat Naturally, will also offer shareholding in the business for compliant farmer groups and distribute profit-share if, and only if, farmer-groups continue to show good environmental and livestock management. This Meat Naturally model has proven successful with 100+ smallholder farmers on land restoration work and regenerative grazing practices. The Project will continue to support the Meat Naturally members, particularly during years 8 and 9 of the project.

Meat Naturally has been highly successful in replication and expanding into neighboring communities that never had initial NGO funding but were able to self-organize into an association and secure a shareholding position with Meat Naturally. The Project will continue to provide support for this expansion. Meat Naturally has assessed and determined there are similar conditions in the RED zones of Botswana and has plans to enter these markets in 2021/2022. As such, the partnership with Meat Naturally Botswana is a key part of the Project exit strategy.


---
**Page 108**
---

204. Partnerships with auditing bodies for sustainable livestock production (e.g., Botswana Farm Assured) and ecosystem indicators (e.g., Climate Change Indicators, Biodiversity Status), green infrastructure, land use planning, biodiversity hotspots, and other key sectors will be developed to ensure optimal internal monitoring, independent auditing, and employment of remote sensing systems that will allow data from the Rangeland Stewardship Portal. Ultimately, this monitoring system will enhance traceability and sustainability, and enhance the value and prices received by farmers for their livestock.

205. The Project will also seek to inform relevant policy platforms in a way that supports long term sustainable development. The Department of Agriculture will be involved in the implementation of Botswana to build resilience to climate change will be involved in the implementation of this Project and as a result, Project initiatives will be mainstreamed via the PSC into national planning for example, through the National Development Plan (NDP). It is envisaged that beyond the Project period, lessons learned and achievements live beyond the Project Implementation period. This will include but not limited to: building capacity in the Department of Agriculture (and others), building a more climate-responsive Department of Veterinary Services, expanding compliance and purpose of the Botswana livestock traceability system (BATS), empowering farmers through farmer facilitation teams, strengthening the role of formal advisory role in communal land management decision-making, and ensuring climate-resilient agricultural practices are adopted. This will be achieved by aligning with CEDA funding criteria. Farmer Facilitation Teams will strengthen the Ministry of Agriculture’s capacity and forge new partnerships with civil society to reach remote rural areas to ensure that all farmers have access to information on best practices.

206. The Department of Veterinary Services has committed to update positions initially financed by the Project as part of their budgetary requests for increased support to enable Commodity-based trade. It has also committed to taking over DVM for drones as necessary to maintain its level of support to rangeland stewards post-Project as indicated in its letter provided in Annex 25.

207. All other Operations and Maintenance for equipment will be funded by the Project or implemented through existing agreements with communal villages on grazing areas, as well as areas that have been compliant with their Rangeland Stewardship Agreement for a three-year period. For areas that require replacement equipment, CI will work with farmers and project partners to ensure that they are replaced in a timely manner using funds that are not dependent on GCF funds. For farmers that have not met compliance agreements, the expectation is that the Ministry of Agriculture for re-deployment as replacements or in the further roll-out of the Rangeland Stewardship Programme to other communal areas in the country. D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section refers to the performance of the Project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF’s Investment Framework.

### D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

208. The proposed Project will contribute to both mitigation and adaption impacts presented in the GCF Investment Framework. Specifically, it will contribute to:

- **M4: Sustainable land management** through improved rangeland restoration, and through sustainable forest management and conservation and enhancement of forest carbon stocks.
  - A1.0 Improved livelihoods and enhanced livelihoods of the most vulnerable people, communities and regions
  - A4.0 Improved ecosystem services and ecosystems services

209. GCF Funded-impacts will be achieved through the deployment of Ecorangers and Restoration Workers on 4.6 million ha of high-biodiversity rangelands. Impact will be achieved by employing active vegetation erosion-prevention, and fire management techniques; improved livestock grazing practices; restoration of degraded land for landscape and its ecosystems through planned rotational rest, and intensive corralling at concentrated feeding points to improve the overall ecosystem function (MF). To enable the development and sustainability of these actions, institutions (AD), adaptive capacity (AT), and awareness (AW) will be strengthened across all stakeholders involved in the project.

210. Mitigation Potential: The Project activities will lead to a 30-48% reduction of annual GHG emissions from the livestock sector in the Project areas from a baseline value of 565,173 tCO2e/year. The project will also achieve considerable emission reductions through improved rangeland management and land rehabilitation. Considering the projected rate of uptake of improved livestock practices and rangeland management (80%), the Project is expected to reduce GHG emissions by 449,065 tCO2e, or 79% of the baseline; 315,000 tCO2e in year 10, 250,000 tCO2e in year 20 as a result of restoration of target rangelands and reduced emissions from the target area livestock. This is equivalent to ~47% of Bolivia’s total GHG emissions reduction commitment under NDCs. It should be noted that this does not include the reduction of livestock emissions in their NDC commitment strategy. However, it is important to note that the Project will contribute significantly to national development strategies that include this source of emissions, as well as nationally appropriate mitigation actions given the large proportion of the country’s population involved in livestock production.

211. Adaptation Potential: The Project will decrease loss of economic assets, diversify livelihoods, and improve income generation opportunities thereby directly increasing the adaptive capacity of 247,000 individuals in Bolivia’s most vulnerable farming ecosystems. This is equivalent to approximately 35% of the total population in target regions and 10% of the national population. Most of the targeted households fall below the national poverty line and the global poverty line of <$1.90 per day. Given this

The estimated 6,000 individuals who receive professional development at Ecorangers, Restorers, and the Green Climate Fund (GCF) funded training will develop adaptive capacity as a result of these newly acquired skills that are in high demand. ### D.2. Paradigm shift potential

#### Potential for scaling up and replication

**214.** Africa contains 25% of all global rangelands, or 1.3 billion ha[20]. These unique ecosystems support some of the last populations of mega-herbivores and predators. They also provide critical ecosystem services such as water, habitats for biodiversity, and these critical ecosystem services are investments on cultural dimensions. Healthy rangeland systems in Southern Africa can sequester 100 Mt CO₂ per annum[21]. The potential for carbon sequestration which may equal that of the Amazon rainforest[22]. Southern Africa’s rangelands have experienced a dramatic decline over the past century, with an 8% increase in woody plant encroachment (including invasive alien plants) over the last 50 years[23]. The current rate of land degradation is unsustainable and climate change is likely to further exacerbate the degradation of rangelands[24]. Projected climate impacts include a “CO₂ fertilization effect”, higher temperatures, larger rainfall events, more frequent droughts, and increased frequency of extreme weather events[25]. Climate models also project drier, hotter conditions with increased length of dry seasons in Southern Africa. These changes will likely lead to desertification which will lead to a decrease in grazing capacity, reduced food security for vulnerable populations, and increase the need for transformative solutions across the region.

**215.** An analysis conducted on the applicability of the Herding for Health model across Eastern and Southern Africa[26] identified 30 million hectares across 18 countries that would benefit from restoration engagements on communal lands and that this could generate an emissions reduction benefit of 210Mt-2,732Mt per annum[27].

## Map of areas across Africa where the Herding for Health model and lessons from this GCF Botswana project could be relevant

### Figure 27: Map of areas across Africa where the Herding for Health model and lessons from this GCF Botswana project could be relevant


#### 216. Within Botswana, implementation of the Project model will be most relevant for the Chobe area, and in the Kweneng and Ghanzi Districts. Herders and communal farmers in these areas are extremely vulnerable to climatic change both as a result of increased risks associated with livestock production and the lack of alternative livelihood options, as well as low adaptive capacity resulting from few investments in their well-being. Examples from elsewhere show that participatory approaches involving local communities and stewardship programmes that value the traditional knowledge they have can be transformational for the individual and the community.[5]


#### 217. Over the last seven years, Conservation International and its partners have been able to implement a model of livestock management in Botswana called HHH model that simultaneously improves livestock management and the livelihoods of poor rural farmers. Through scientific research and development, this model has demonstrated how market access strategies achieve the significant potential to sustain catalytic investments in restoring Southern Africa’s degraded rangelands.

Through this Project, we will be able to test a new version of the HHH model at scale. The HHH model at a national scale to quantifiably demonstrate how these changes contribute to livestock methane emissions, increase sustainable rangeland carbon storage, and contribute to critical ecosystem services required for the long-term resilience of resilient rangeland ecosystems.


#### 218. The H4H model is currently being implemented in various scales in Southern and Eastern Africa: Mozambique (Limpopo National Park, Maputo Special Reserve), South Africa (three landscapes), Zambia (Simalaka Conservancy) and Kenya (three

### Potential for Knowledge Sharing and Learning

#### 2019. Conservation International is part of several important platforms including the Evergreen Agriculture Platform, the Global Alliance for Livestock and Aquaculture Growth that are looking for implementation models like those in this proposed Project. Conservation International also operates in more than 30 countries globally and conducts regular learning events to share lessons learned from the lessons learned and successes of this Project with stakeholders all over the world.

#### 2021. The Project will become a flagship for implementation of the Herding at Scale. Programmes scaling on to the national level will be shared with other H4H programmes in Southern Africa, including development of those in Botswana. Herding at Scale is also regularly profiled within SADC CAREDESA events and will play a key role in the upcoming SADC CAREDESA conference. Conservation International will share lessons learned from these platforms, the development of communications tools, and through its Africa Division, CI will share the Project approach, tools, and lessons learned to build regional understanding on how to scale up livestock production systems that are sustainable and resilient.

#### 2022. The extensive level of government buy-in and active participation in the Project will also contribute to sharing knowledge and experiences from this Project. A variety of ministries and departments of the government of Botswana will be engaged in this Project, including the Ministry of Environment and Tourism, the Ministry of Agriculture, and others. These institutions will share lessons learned with other national governments through a variety of engagements and be able to support the replication of project activities and models in other countries.

223\. The Project will operate on the principle of maximal transparency. The Rangeland Stewardship Portal will be created to be open source and the Portal will be freely replicated anywhere for use by any interested party. Best practice implementation will be shared widely with governments, civil society organizations, and beneficiaries so that they can benefit from the wealth of information collected.

224\. CI will also host a Gaborone Declaration for Sustainability in Africa (GDSA) community of practice on rangeland stewardship. The GDSA is an umbrella organization for interactions with the GSDSA and national government. CI will respond to invitations by governments, civil society organizations, and other stakeholders to provide technical potential, promote national agricultural legislation, and support corporate policies that enable regenerative, climate-resilient rangeland management. Through the governance mechanism of the GDSA, CI will also provide technical assistance on financial incentives and advise on streamlining legislation across ministries to support rangeland restoration and sustainable land use.

225\. The project will also create gender-sensitive communications materials, tools, and data that will be shared publicly through CI, the GCF, Herding for Health, the government of Botswana, NGOs, radio, and social media. These communications activities are critical for sharing information about the Project’s goals and challenges in southern Africa and for informing the people of Botswana about this Project.

### Contribution to the Enabling and Regulatory Environment

226\. The Project is designed to result in the adoption of climate-resilient rangeland management practices in Botswana and to institutionalize these improved practices at multiple levels so that they permanently transform rangeland management and its rangeland economic development strategies (including Grassfed Beef) in Botswana and can be applied more widely in Sub-Saharan Africa.

227\. The Project’s Farmer Facilitation Teams and Rangeland Stewardship Agreements ensure restoration investments use the established principles of Free, Prior, and Informed Consent. These agreements also provide a legal framework that is designed to engender consensus and build local capacity that translates into successful Ecosystem- Based Adaptation activities (See Feasibility Assessment, Section 4 for more details).

228\. The Project takes a holistic approach and engages and advises the BMC, CEDA and private sector entities on ways to conserve water and sustainable ecological and social gains via a long-term rangeland stewardship charter. Table 10 shows that private sector production will be of value to private sector actors and by demonstrating the economic benefits of climate smart production, may compel other actors, including governments to adopt new models or create the incentives and enabling environments that will allow this model to thrive.

### 231. Other Project Impacts that will transform the enabling environment in Botswana include:

- Transformation of the (pelagie) Programme, the government of Botswana's job creation initiative, into a vehicle to employ Ecotourism and graduate monitors for rangeland restoration and climate change investment projects. The government currently supports nearly USD 80 million per year (pelagieing and parks) or 10% of the country's formal employment (see Annex 6). The government is seeking to develop new professions, like Ecotourism, to better utilize this investment while building resilience in rural communities.

- Formalization of a professional qualification for herding that includes climate change training and roll-out through the Botswana University Agriculture and Natural Resource Centre for In-Service and Continued Education. This professionalization of herding will bring new respect for herdiers and provide a path to both professional success with maintaining their cultural traditions and indigenous knowledge.

- 6000 Ecotourism, Restoration Workers, and Graduate Monitors deployed in current beef value chains and new monitoring systems in Botswana, thereby enhancing local traceability system (BATS) that can be converted into increased market value and sustainability.

- Empowerment of communal farmers in ecosystem governance and climate resilient supply chains that build community structures and social enterprises for adaptive capacity and financial sustainability.

Overall contribution to Climate-Resilient Development Pathways

232. The rehabilitation of Botswana's rangelands could contribute not only to local ecosystem health, livestock production, and improved livelihood capacity and rural populations' food security, but also to global climate resilience through maintenance and building of rangeland carbon sequestration potential. The Project activities are specifically designed to build on and support efforts in low-carbon development by the adoption of improved rangeland management to both improve farmers' lives and reduce emissions.

233. Nationally, the Project activities catalyse innovative new approaches for the communal livestock sector and its stakeholders and partners that contribute to National Transformation Strategy priorities of economic diversification, improved competitiveness and productivity, and engendering self-reliance and dignity. This paradigm shift will be achieved through the Project's delivery of the following:

- Strengthened citizen and local governance structures to be responsible for their own climate-resilient development.
- Direct employment for 83 Project staff (71% based in rural target districts) and 6000 professional herdsmen, called Ecologists, Range Management Workers, and Graduate Monitors (100% in rural districts).
- Developing and deploying a professional qualification and accredited training curriculum for herdsmen developed and implemented by BUAN's Centre for In-service and Continued Education.
- Re-purposing of job-creation programme investments from the Ministry of Local Government into restoring degraded rangelands and supporting improved livestock management in communal rangelands.

236\. The Project enables improved food production that has lower carbon emissions than feedlot produced meat or crop production which removes above-ground carbon from vegetation. By bringing communal farming and training unemployed youth to support a more sustainable red meat supply chain, the Project can prevent deforestation and reduce greenhouse gas emissions associated with the production of red meat. D.4\. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

Project responsiveness to vulnerability of Botswana's rural poor

237\. More than 70% of beneficiary families in the Project area are at risk from livestock production and climate change. Livestock producers make less than the global poverty threshold of $1.90 per day[24], making them extremely vulnerable to extreme climate events and the impacts of climate change. The Project seeks to increase this population's livelihoods in a sustainable way to effectively protect this traditional and culturally important economic activity, which is critical for food security among Botswana's population depends. The very low income levels of the Project beneficiaries necessitates use of a GCF grant mechanism, rather than a reimbursable financial instrument.

238\. The Project will support Botswana in its efforts to increase socio-economic equities. While Botswana is classified as a middle-income country (MIC) due to significant wealth from mineral resources and oil production, it remains one of the poorest countries. Current grant programming, including [peleging], that aim to provide social safety nets have not been able to overcome cyclical, often climate-induced poverty, and high levels of income inequality. The GCF Project will contribute to Botswana's national poverty rate of 53.3%[24]. Unemployment remains high (approximately 20-27%) and the [peleging] Programme is increasingly unable to meet job creation demand due to failed crops and loss of livestock from climate change. The Project will provide a sustainable livelihood in areas where extreme drought conditions attributed to climate change have led to loss of livestock from starvation with devastating effects on economic security at both national and household levels[24].

239\. The Project approaches will address unemployment, inequity, and climate resilience in underutilized communal village grazing areas. In these target areas, the challenges most often mentioned by farmers during stakeholder engagement were lack of good fodder, lack of water, and poor livestock condition. Through the Project's Rangeland Stewardship Agreements, solutions to these issues will be delivered through

**Page 123**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 119

## Coordinated grazing plans that allow for the recovery (rest) and natural regeneration of rangelands.

Rangeland Stewardship Agreements, farmers, Land Boards, and VCDGs will be supported to organise and work collectively to sustainably manage their grazing resources. Encouragers, supported by government investment in job creation, enable the implementation of these grazing plans and improve the keeping-of-herd health that can unlock market access options for communal farmers.

### 240.
The Project will organize farmers to improve the impacts of Government support programmes. The Government of Botswana has put measures in place such as subsidies on fertilizers and seeds, but these are not always available or affordable. These provide more benefit to commercial farmers than those in the communal sector, who are predominantly the rural poor. By organizing communal farmers, they will have additional leverage to negotiate for assistance from the government – thereby reducing the disparities between them and the commercial farmers. In addition to addressing food stress, the government provides farmers with economic incentives to off-fate (sell) cattle, but this incentivisubsidy is only available to those farmers with access to functional water and credit facilities. By organizing communal farmers into a single entity that has access to rural communal farms, the Project will make this economic incentive available to a larger vulnerable population during drought events.

### 241.
The Project will build adaptive capacity in districts with high rural and indigenous populations. Only 4 of the 104 villages have >75% of their income from non-agricultural sources. The majority of food in Malawi is produced in Ngamiland and Bobonang and Madinare in Botswana provide demand centres that are largely dependent on provision of agricultural products, particularly meat, from surrounding areas for their population food needs. In the communal areas, 80% of the food consumed by households in this area is consumed at household subsistence. In Ngamiland, 15% of the farmers are illiterate and 51% only have primary education.[23] Climate change more dramatically affects these communities as they are already vulnerable to droughts due to a high proportion of indigenous people and the Project will build adaptive capacity in these areas and provide an opportunity to capture and transfer indigenous knowledge of climate adaptation strategies into formal herding training programmes.

### 242.
The Project will be particularly responsive to the rights of women and indigenous people in the Project Areas. They will have a key role in designing and confirming access with the rangeland stewardship agreements, and representation targets are established in the ESMP and GAP to ensure equitable benefits for social, environmental and economic development opportunities.

The Project will address the urgent need to overcome tenure as a tool to control livestock in communal lands. This is a challenging task, as there are currently no institutional arrangements or legal frameworks governing of lands (the Project will aim to

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 120

## Work with VDCs and Land Boards on including collective management within their current mandates (Herding). Herding, supported by government co-finance investment in job creation and skills development, will provide the tools to control livestock movement and monitor rangeland conditions. Herding and holding of livestock provides an opportunity to move rangelands from overgrazing and livestock husbandry. This, in turn, build rangeland and livestock resilience in dryland ecosystems while the livelihoods of vulnerable pastoral communities are improved. In addition to the adaptive capacity to manage grazing lands, this project will also dispose of C1-processable long-term assets to beneficiaries and supporting entities in the 104 village grazing areas, and community planning and resource allocation for the maintenance and use of these assets. It is expected that direct barriers to adaptive management are sustainably addressed (see Annex A).

### Needs of Beneficiaries

The needs of beneficiaries were determined through an extensive consultation process conducted during proposal development. This engagement was carried out in accordance with the principles of Free, Prior, and Informed Consent (FPIC) with initial introductions to local communities, including indigenous peoples, made through NGO representatives and government employees (often veterinary officers) with pre-existing relationships with the communities. Please see FP Annex 7, Summary of Consultations for more information on community engagements.

## Project Responsiveness to Institutional Strengthening Needs

### Enable Government Ministries to Adopt Transformational Change

The Project will enable three government ministries to adopt transformational rather than incremental change in order to respond to climate risks. Through their close engagement with the Ministry of Environment, Forestry and Natural Resources, the Department of Botswana’s Ministry of Agriculture, Ministry of Local Government, Ministry of Environmental Affairs, Ministry of Lands and Survey, and Ministry of Water and Range Services, all will be strengthened. This Project relies on the cooperation between these government bodies and the Project will support their coordinated activities, which will result in more effective implementation of climate change adaptation strategies.

### Gender Equity

The Project also contributes to the gender equity obligations of Botswana’s National Climate Change Strategy and the Ministry of Gender Affairs has offered its list of trained interns to assist with the project. This will ensure that female participation is high. In this way, the project is helping this Ministry achieve its goal of mainstreaming gender awareness into policy implementation. Further details on the Project activities and how they relate to the expressed needs of stakeholders obtained during consultation are provided in Table 18a.b.

## Alignment with National Climate & Development Policies

### D.5. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### 247.
This Project is well aligned with the Botswana Government's Vision 2036 goals for "an effective climate change response and the long-term just transition to a climate resilient and lower carbon economy and society" with the intention to "efficiently manage inevitable impacts of climate change, while enhancing Botswana's social, economic and environmental resilience and emergency response capacity." The Project aims to support implementation of this Vision in the three Project areas and contribute nationally to the development of:

- **The draft National Adaptation Strategy for Botswana** to be submitted under the UNFCCC as a National Adaptation Plan (NAP) which can draw on Project lessons learned regarding Ecosystem-based Adaptation and Climate-resilient Agriculture.


---
**Page 128**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 124

* New fiscal instruments or allocations and enhancement of existing mechanisms (e.g., iplegeling) that support targets expressed in Botswana's NDC; and.
* Design of NDC calculations and commitments of Botswana, and, by sharing Project experiences through GDSS and UNFCCC policy platforms, the Project will contribute to Botswana’s international collaborations with other UNFCCC parties and Gambia, Djibouti, significantly increasing efforts towards mitigation and adaptation opportunities from rangeland and livestock management.
* Adoption of Tier 2 IPCC emissions inventory methodologies. Previous GHG inventories in Botswana used IPCC Tier 1 methodologies and have indicated the goal of updating these inventories to Tier 2. This project will provide an evidence base for this transition and thereby allow for increased accuracy of GHG reporting by the Government of Botswana to the UNFCCC.

### 248.
The Project aligns with the draft Green Climate Fund Country Programme²³⁹, four of the five National Priority Areas for Climate Finance²⁴⁰; and National Climate Change Policy Strategy (NCCPS)²⁴¹. The Project will support Botswana to implement a strategy that makes the livestock sector more resilient to climate change through a coherent approach that will enhance preparedness at all levels of governance and improve rangeland integrity in degraded areas. This will be achieved by implementing a range of actions on degraded rangelands to reduce the impacts of drought on human, livestock, and wildlife populations—and integrate this with the Draft Drought Management Strategy. Finally, it is expected that the Project will support Botswana to take necessary steps to restore degraded communal rangelands, reduce rural populations’ vulnerability to climate change and adopt a low carbon development pathway with multiple potential benefits as part of an inclusive economic development strategy.

### 249.
The Project supports the National Climate Smart Agricultural Programme (2015-2025) through Component 2, specifically targeting building resilience and associated mitigation co-benefits in the agricultural sector. This Component is aligned with Botswana’s National Programme to Combat Desertification (2006) objective “3: To facilitate sustainable land use, stabilize soils and combat desertification and mitigating effects of drought”, where youth and women are specific targets. The Project also will support the national Drought Management Strategy, which is currently in draft form, with a focus on building capacity for early warning systems and community mobilisation. Eorangere and Rangeland Restoration Teams will be trained in alignment with the Education and Training Sector Strategic Plan (2015) and contribute to improved

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 125

The Project also supports Botswana's development policies. The importance of mainstreaming adaptation planning into sectoral planning, including rangeland Ecosystem-based Adaptation is already highlighted in existing climate change documents and the National Climate Change Policy objectives of reducing impacts of climate change on agriculture and limiting greenhouse gas emissions[34]. Additionally, the Project's strategies and Gender Action Plan address three policy goals of the Climate Adaptation Strategy:

- Promote equitable participation of women farmers and female-headed households in Smart Agriculture (CSA) programmes;
- Empowering communities, especially women and youth to actively participate in the implementation of climate change response measures in both rural and urban areas, including women's voices on natural resources management through their equitable participation in CBNRM processes; and
- Ensuring that gendered differences of climate change are mainstreamed into climate change education[34].

The Project activities are designed to be gender responsive or transformative at all levels, enable the full participation of women and female-headed households in disaster management, participate fully in public gatherings through the provision of childcare, and address the specific needs of women and girls as well as the disproportionate targeting of these populations. The Project also recognizes that elderly, children and people living with disability are dependents that may experience greater vulnerability to climate change impacts and seeks to mainstream gender equality into all project activities, awareness programmes, technologies, finance and capacity building.

Finally, the Project supports the following development priorities of the new administration as indicated in the State of the Nation address (SONA) presented in November 2020[35]: SONA language below in italics:

- The Land Administration Procedures Capacity and Systems (LAPCAS) project was to provide technological solutions for effective management of land. As part of the LAPCAS initiative, Government will establish a National Spatial Data Infrastructure (NSDI) by 2021, which will allow for the implementation of the objectives of the NSDI expected to contribute to the Digital Government Transformation (SmartBotswana) and also enhance the implementation of the National Spatial Plan (NSP).

## Role and Capacities of Project Partners

### 253. Three ministries are direct participants in the Project, which will strengthen their capacity to integrate climate change considerations into their planning and support functions.

*The Ministry of Agricultural Development and Food Security (MoA) is the Project Lead and host organization. The MoA implements numerous programmes aimed at social inclusion and poverty reduction through its National Agricultural Strategic Development Plan. Specific initiatives that this Project will draw on are Livestock Management, Dairy Development, Livestock Health Services, Department of Agricultural Research (DAR) and Department of Animal Production (DAP) livestock genetics, and DAP’s livestock component in MoA’s Rural Extension Centres can be used as venues for training programmes, providing practical on-the-ground. MoA extension officers and veterinary staff will also be key members of the Farmer Facilitator Teams to provide technical support to farmers.*

*The Ministry of Environment, Natural Resources and Tourism implements climate rangeland and wildlife ecology knowledge and outreach and CBNRM policies are critical to the Project. Local and district Department of Forestry and Range Resources (DFRR) officials will be key members of the Farmer Facilitator Teams to contribute rangeland and soil rehabilitation expertise into the community-based monitoring and reporting system. DFRR staff will also provide feedback into reports on international obligations, such as SDGs, UNFCCC, GSOA. The Project will also provide reports to the Ministry’s Climate Change Committee who will draft national climate change policies and strategies for the country. Finally, the Ministry will be engaged in policy work related to formal recognition of Rangeland Stewardship.*

The BMC primarily sells to high-end buyers and when supply is insufficient or of poor quality, it has to force itself to turn down for extended periods. Therefore, the Project impact will help ensure a stable supply of quality cattle to the BMC and create new, sustainable opportunities for communal farmers.

### Regulatory Environment and Context

255\. Botswana’s regulatory environment is particularly favourable for the Project’s successful implementation. Three key areas of the regulatory environment will impact the Project's success or failure.

- Legislation creating job creation for the public good has been in existence since the 1960s, when a new President committed to calls for transformation of the (pelenging) programme (some of which came through stakeholder engagement during the development of this FP) in the recent State of the Nation address on 5 November 2020 to “re-engineer” the pelenging programme on an integrated approach to planning and implementation of development projects at the local level” and “to empower beneficiaries through capacity building and development of technical skills for sustained livelihoods, the pelenging programme is being restructured. The new structure is secured and sustained and associated evolving strategies to re-vamp (pelenging) will continue. Should this momentum falter and the (pelenging) programme not be willing to transform, the Project will need to be restructured significantly as this commitment from a recognized leader is important for the project.

- Legislation on biodiversity conservation and veterinary disease management have historically been in conflict in Botswana. Veterinary fences established through agricultural regulations have cut off wildlife migrations and led to damaging environmental and wildlife impacts that appear to contradict the country’s progressive and far-reaching vision of sustainable development. The Project will address this issue as part of its Feasibility Study Appendix 4.6 (environment and conservation agreements) and Appendix 5.6 (veterinary disease management and CBT). The Project promotes new management practices for wildlife fences, such as the use of electronic fencing. The adoption of these practices is expected to lead to a reduced need for enforcement against illegal killing of wildlife or maintenance of fences that have limited effectiveness across the Project area. These new practices are consistent with the National Vision 2036 (National State of the Nation address on 5 November 2020.) New regulations for co-rural quarantine were promulgated in 2020, creating the foundation shift from exclusively state-run quarantine facilities. With small revisions, these new regulations will be applicable for communal herds managed by Ecorangers.


---
**Page 134**
---

which GCF officials indicate will be in place by March 2022 and can be tested at the Project demonstration sites.

* Legislation on economic inclusion and beef cluster development are new initiatives that have gained political momentum since 2018, with new regulations expected in 2021 to support both. Parastatals CEDA and BMC, among others, are already embracing the tone of the political movement and as a result have committed (CEDA) indicated interest in supporting the project. The government has also indicated that how resilience can become a modus for transitioning and building local, inclusive economic empowerment to achieve a paradigm shift for the livestock sector. Additional information on the regulatory and policy environment is available in Annex 2, section 4.

### D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness

#### 256. The GCF investment into this Project provides a transformative spark that will compel intersectoral collaboration of current investments across job creation, CBRM, and livestock production into new working arrangements to facilitate effective deployment of EIA. The Project will align and coordinate government and NGO investments into a single coherent framework for the development of a sustainable model for smallholder farming livelihoods dependent on these rangelands. The scale of this leveraged investment is unprecedented in Botswana and sets an important precedent for how the government and civil society funding can be used in the future to match appeals for global climate finance for implementation of the Botswana's Climate Change Action Plan.

#### 257. While Ci and Gb explored the option of running the Project through 15 sub-grants, due to the staled nature of government and limited presence of NGOs/CBOs in two of the three target areas, it was determined that the most effective approach would be for Ci to implement the Project directly with a focus on building capacity within the five mine clusters. This approach will create a cadre of skilled individuals that can move into a variety of roles across the country and provide a strong foundation for expertise for replication of the work in Botswana's other districts. As an organization, Ci is committed to building local capacity, and opportunities for employment staff into other institutions and organizations are being explored.

#### 258. The Project will deliver tangible benefits that respond to direct needs of beneficiary communities. The rate of uptake of improved management practices will be rapid by utilizing the existing network of extension officers and the participatory approach that incentivizes inclusion of rangeland restoration support in the programmes of numerous partners and stakeholders, and the development of farmer-owned social enterprises. This is further supported through the integration of climate-smart ecosystem restoration and adaptation activities with broader development programmes. The Project will also provide a model for scaling up the successful EIA approach successfully deployed in South Africa's communal farming context. As such, it has a high potential for rapid delivery that can then be used to unlock further internal government and donor sources of financial investments for replication in Botswana and elsewhere in Africa.


---
**Page 135**
---

**259.** Investments in this restoration programme that fully supports communities' existing livelihoods, creates a new scale of impact, and can be achieved at a GCF-funded cost efficiency of $80 per hectare. The low cost of restoration is important as the annual cost to achieve the 1.5°C target by 2030 is estimated to be US$35 billion, equivalent to 3.2% of the country's GDP.

**260.** Through a Financial and Economic Analysis (FEA), it is projected that the Net Present Value (NPV) generated from this project scenario will be US$148 million, representing an annual benefit of US$59 million for the 30-year Project scenario. This represents a more than seven-fold increase relative to the Without Project Scenario, and significantly more than the NPV generated by individual Project components. The FEA is based on the same assumptions as the Financial and Economic Analysis (FEA) includes only one drought event during the model period. The FEA does not include any additional benefits from carbon sequestration in the Project scenario—however, these estimates will be revised when improved baseline data are collected. The Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR) of the project is calculated to be 140%. However, for Projects such as this one, with fluctuating annualized net benefits, it may not be possible to calculate an exact cost per beneficiary at this time.

**261.** Given that the total cost of the Project is US$87 million, and that the project should result in the mitigation of -21.5 million tCO2e, the cost of mitigation is estimated at US$4.09 per tonne of CO2e. This compares to a cost of US$36 per tonne of CO2e allocated for mitigation, the cost per tCO2e is US$13.76. This potential to enhance carbon sequestration at a low cost per ton of CO2eq will provide an attractive element for Botswana and other countries with similar mitigation objectives can adopt similar approach.

**262.** The total cost per direct beneficiary over 5 years is US$49 (US$8.36 per GCF grant year). This figure should be regarded as reflecting the costs incurred in the Project target areas (particularly Kalagadi – ~0.5 individuals/km) but is reasonable when compared with investments for similar projects on the continent. A similar programme, the Emissions Reductions Programme (ERP), has been implemented by the Government of Botswana at a cost of US$135 per beneficiary for the first five years starting in 2027[24]. Due to the high population density and low economic activity in the Kalagadi region, where the project may be selected by communities, it is not possible to calculate an exact cost per beneficiary per region at this time. Based on implementation across three similar sites in Botswana, the average cost of implementing a restoration programme that includes transport costs are high relative to the number of people benefiting. Low population density and low economic activity, coupled with high transport costs, reduce overall economic activity, slower rangeland recovery timesframes, and smaller local demand for goods and services. Based on these factors, it is likely that Kalagadi will have the highest cost per beneficiary in Botswana. The Kalagadi region also has some of the most vulnerable populations, and also have rich indigenous cultures where some of the best lessons on practices that maintain ecological integrity under extreme climate stress can be learned and replicated.


---
**Page 136**
---

263\. The co-finance investment to be provided by the GCF’s [deploying programme for the employment and in-service training of 6,000 individuals], and the administrative costs associated with these employees is significant but reflects Botswana relatively high minimum wage for agricultural employment (which increased by 17% on July 1, 2020) compared to other countries in the region. The salaries of skilled workers, and a mining-based economy, salaries for professional positions are also relatively high compared with other countries in the region. For comparison; however, the average annual salary in Botswana is US$35,846, which represents generation of a full-time job, which was US$12,132\. Currently, South Africa invests US$250 million per year on social protection programmes for people who depend on natural resource benefits\*\*. The co-finance contribution to this Project represents only 10% of the total [deploying programme budget] per annum. As such, once basic systems for an effective and efficient project management are in place, the cost-effective replication of the Project model across the country will be probable and cost-effective through the use of existing infrastructure.

264\. Cost effective replication throughout Africa can also be anticipated. Over the last 15 years, the number of African countries implementing major social protection Programmes for poor and vulnerable people has tripled, with internal and external (particularly international) support\*\*. The GCF’s [deploying programme] will respond to COVID-19 economic slowdowns and new opportunities to influence their deployment in Africa. This is particularly relevant as the launch of the Decade for Restoration. Through GDSS, AFR100, and other efforts, other African countries will be exposed to lessons from this Botswana Project and be able to integrate the rangeland restoration model into their own efforts.

# PROJECT IMPLEMENTATION AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigation measures

### Selected Risk Factor: Land use conflicts/competition

#### Category | Probability | Impact
---|---|---
Technical and operational | High | Medium

**Description**

Land use that undermines the fact that the rest or rehabilitation of areas within a Rangeland Stewardship Agreement is the top risk to Project goals. This issue is greatest in areas targeted for privatisation under the recent revision of the Tribal Grazing Land Policy (2019) and where grazing rights are held by both communal and private landholders. This issue was discussed at length in the Project’s Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA). Although there were no known conflicts with regard to grazing rights, this issue could have an impact for if it proliferates in future years. The Project will work closely with communities to ensure that any such issues do not impact to its project goals.

Traditional authorities over grazing land rights also presents a challenge for the Project to overcome. The presence of communal and private livestock farmers is common across the Project areas, but not universally across the Project area and reflect a context addressed successfully in the past in other Herdiers for Health efforts through open dialogue and engagement with both communal and private livestock farmers.

**Mitigation Measure(s)**

Due to historical memory of collective grazing benefits, the barriers to integrated management, as long as individual farming units are not defined, will be minimal. The Project team will use the negotiation process to reduce risk of land-use conflict and identify potential mitigation measures. This will include working with communities to ensure that any conflicts over grazing rights are resolved in a manner that allows for opportunities for integrating collective hard management with community neighbours and private landholders within an agreed upon framework. The Project will also work closely with Traditional Authorities to ensure that they are included in the project’s management plans. Project demonstration sites have been selected in areas where such conflicts are non-existent or minimal, but where there is a need to address grazing rights issues. These demonstration sites will be used as learning opportunities for communities and will be shared with the Farmer Facilitation Teams during the Training of Trainers. Rare training is also deeply rooted in conflict resolution and behaviour change science and they have been selected for the membership role for the project because of this experience.

The training and involvement of Land Board representatives, traditional authorities, and VDCs or principles of climate smart rangeland management and implications for land-use plans will be continuously emphasised in all educational materials. The Project team will work with communities to ensure that grazing rights are clearly defined and encouraged to engage in a broader landscape agreement for maximum resilience and may be identified to play a role in the development of such agreements. In addition, the Project will work closely with Traditional Authorities and incentives are provided towards equal participation in the Rangeland Stewardship Agreement; the Project will work with communities to ensure that grazing rights are clearly defined and encouraged to engage in a broader landscape agreement for maximum resilience.

The aim of the RSA is to spatially define the limits for grazing to minimize conflict with wildlife. Annual re-negotiations allow the Project to adjust grazing or land management conflicts that are identified during implementation. During project implementation, the Project team will work closely with communities and Traditional Authorities to ensure principles for RSAs and identify migration hotspots/sensitivities or seasonal movements that should be avoided within the RSA boundaries. The Project will also work with communities to ensure that grazing rights are clearly defined (interests vs livestock produce) and have shown many areas for potential solutions to reduce conflict including, but not limited to, fencing, water points, and grazing management plans.


---
**Page 173**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 169

Customary land tenure (traditional law courts) may be used as a final resort by a Village Development Committee or Land Board in the case that a land use conflict cannot be prevented or resolved through Project interventions. In such cases, customary land tenure systems and/or the provisions of the Land Act (2018) are utilized. This would be based on the historical involvement of tribal authorities and land overseers in resolving disputes over land use and access to resources. The project will provide training for Village Development Committees and Land Boards on how to handle such situations. CI would help vulnerable communities understand the legal process and their rights and may present technical evidence where required/requested in the process.

## Selected Risk Factor 2: Perverse/counterproductive market incentives

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | Medium |

### Description
Traditional relationships with the Botswana Meat Commission and livestock market middlemen could undermine incentives for improved livestock management practices. To address this potential problem, the Botswana Meat Commission (BMC) has offered prices that enable farmers who do not practice good animal husbandry practices to receive a higher price than those who do. This is particularly true in the case of cattle producers who are not practicing good hygiene and sanitation practices (e.g., paying for a herder, vaccines, dips, supplemental fodder in times of vegetation stress, etc.). While the intention behind this policy was to provide incentives for farmers to improve their livestock management practices, it has had the opposite effect. It has encouraged unmanaged animals that cause environmental degradation and disease outbreaks that increase climate vulnerability in the Project area. The BMC is also a major buyer of cattle products from the Project area. If the BMC continues to buy cattle products from speculators who are active in some communities in the Project area, this could reduce incentives resulting in lower participation in the Project activities.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The Project will work closely with partner Meat Naturally Botswana (MNB) and other private sector actors to encourage price-based incentives that harness current value-chain participant purchase power or offer better market access for farmers who are practicing good animal husbandry practices. The Project will also seek to ensure that speculators meet their targets at a lower cost by hosting an auction for commodities in Rangeland Stewardship Agreements (RSAs) and other livestock products, including cattle, sheep, goats, and poultry, as well as dairy products. See the Feasibility Study for specific MNP approaches that will be used to reduce this risk. This risk is likely to be mitigated by the Project but will have a demonstration site able to show higher returns from improved market access activities.

## Selected Risk Factor 3: Institutional and regulatory barriers to integrated and adaptive management

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

### Description
Current government policy and subsidy/reimbursement programmes for the livestock sector may reduce effectiveness or work against the objectives of the programme, such as disease control measures, or livestock procurement schemes. Reforms to government programmes are under discussion but may cause confusion and conflict with the Project’s activities.

Similarly, local government changes and changes in coordination mandates that evolve after the 2019 election may also slow implementation until new local government cross-sectoral communication and coordination protocols are established. The Project will work closely with local governments to ensure that they have a clear understanding of its role as well as the roles of other stakeholders involved, including private sector actors. This risk is likely to be mitigated by the Project but will have a demonstration site able to show higher returns from improved market access activities.


---
**Page 174**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 179

### Mitigation

The Project will focus and invest in coordination, system alignment, and budgetary processes as key priorities for maintaining the current favourable regulatory environment. Activities under Output 1.4 and Output 3.2 will help to engage the highest-level decision makers and the broader public as witnesses to Project impacts and contributions. The Project will also undertake a communications campaign to raise awareness of the project’s objectives, outputs, and results. This will include a series of communications activities, and the impact of this communication effort will be assessed through subsequent implementation and monitoring phases.

The proposed inclusive structure of the PSC and component sub-committees will also directly lead to cooperation and coordination among stakeholders at all levels. The Project will ensure that all stakeholders are involved in meetings, workshops, and dialogues from the national to local level; the project will ensure that all stakeholders are informed about the progress made by the Project and its results. Please see paragraph 198 for further elaboration on Project approaches to reducing risk from the regulatory environment.

The project intentionally included Ecosystem and Restoration Worker salaries as the project co-finance due to the continuous nature of this work-support government program as a building block in the transition towards sustainable transformation. Expecting the Project’s delivery of salaried workers to be a significant driver for increased employment, it is hoped that any reduction in co-financing will not have an adverse impact on the government’s job creation investment. The inclusion of support for Ecosystems, Restoration Workers and their supervisors as part of the project co-finance will ensure that there are sufficient resources available for an ongoing basis to assure co-financing is materialized as described in the Project Budget, including through periodic financial reporting on co-finance.

### Selected Risk Factor 4: Natural rangeland ecosystem dynamics prevent recovery

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Low |

**Description**

As climate impacts increase over time in Botswana, any area of recovery and/or new water sources are likely to attract wildlife resulting in an increased grazing pressure that could reduce the positive effects of the Project. Where appropriate, it is recommended that the Project consider alternative solutions such as tourism promotion or better adaptation strategies for livestock production. It should be noted that this risk is not expected to be materialized over a period; it is anticipated that this risk and its impact will be localized and temporary in nature. However, persistent

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 171

## Mitigation

Ecotourism training will include management strategies for managing interactions and conflict between wildlife and livestock, and communal grazing plans will seek to minimize these interactions. Adaptive strategies will be developed based on the results of the baseline study. The Project will also work with communities to increase their awareness and basic fire-fighting partnerships will be mobilized with DFRR in all high-risk areas. Additionally, the TNC will provide training for community members on how to use the tools provided by DFRR to mitigate making to mitigate the impacts of these events. This Project will be realistic and take a practical approach of feasibility of implementation. The Project will also work with communities to develop grazing plans that are more suitable/better/more lucrative for a target grazing area, this will be communicated and support for other benefits from government or donor project will be facilitated by the Project.

In addition, the Project will utilize its close working relationship with the Ministry of Local Government to expedite and ensure drought relief kits for Project areas in time to help capture resources, which is occasionally experienced.

### Selected Risk Factor 5: Project staffing and scale-up

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

#### Implementation
The Project will require CI Botswana to transition its operations from a small current office to a fully staffed team ultimately totaling 63 employees by Phase II of the Project by ensuring that the phased approach to implementation and staffing is well supported.

There are risks that delayed recruitment, onboarding, and training of qualified Project staff may delay project activities or that the new staff experience team-building issues that impact project management and implementation.


## Mitigation

To ensure the timely and effective implementation of the Project, including during the initial scaling-up phase, CI Botswana will develop a detailed staffing plan.

The timeline for the first year of the Project is structured to allow for sufficient time for staff recruitment, onboarding, training, and capacity-building of new staff hired for the Project. Hiring will be done in phases; the hiring of key project staff will start at Phase I of the Project. The hiring process will be completed by Phase II of the Project.

CI will use its own resources to begin recruitment for these positions prior to Project FAA effectiveness. Other hiring will be done through a combination of internal and external recruitment processes, including leveraging CI’s prior projects and extensive livestock farming experience in the country; there will be a significant pool of qualified local candidates available. CI will use a transparent and well-publicized open recruitment process to fill all positions.

CI will also create mechanisms for sharing the significant existing expertise and experience from across the organization into new project teams, including through mentoring and coaching programs, as well as knowledge sharing between current CI Botswana staff, CII’s regional and global operations and technical staff, and CII’s headquarters. This will ensure that there is a strong foundation of capacity to support the scaling up of activities on the ground that will address recruitment, administrative and financial functions, procurement; and grants / contract management


---
**Page 176**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 172

Specifically, Project staff will have counterparts in the CI South Africa Programme as well as Hering for Health Projects. These include the Country Director and GDSA Executive Secretary, the CI Africa Field Division Hering for Health Director, Climate Change Officer, Grants Management Specialist, M&E Specialist, and the Conservation Finance Director. These supporting staff have extensive experience with the AHEAD project and will be able to provide guidance on the implementation of the Project. The Hering for Health Director, who worked on the AHEAD project, is currently supporting two demonstration site projects in South Africa. The Hering for Health Director will also work closely with the CI Africa Field Division Hering for Health Director which includes the Botswana region focal area for this Project. The Hering for Health Director will be employed by the Project as per personnel and will support recruitment and team building.

Finally, CI will create a dedicated start-up team which will include key Project personnel: CI's Africa Field Division, and the Hering for Health Director. This team will provide guidance on the management of other large projects including a multi-year, multi-partner GCF project in Madagascar. This project start-up team will be supported by the CI Global Finance Team to ensure that all financial aspects are covered during this phase of the Project. The start-up team will support the creation of an effective and efficient detailed implementation workplan for Phase 1 of the Project to keep project execution on schedule.

## Selected Risk Factor B: Prohibited Practices, Including ML/FT

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Prohibited practices | Low | Medium |

The Project involves a wide array of actors, including government, sub-grantees, vendors and service providers, and beneficiaries. However, based on a calculation of the potential for prohibited practice risk indicators, CI assesses Business to be at low risk in terms of prohibited practices. The Project will follow all GCF policies and procedures concerning AMUC/FT, and the project's direct recipients of GCF funds, sub-grantees, vendors, service providers, and beneficiaries are subject to the same prohibitions against prohibited practices as in applicable laws and Bushell’s Rules. CI has put in place measures to prevent and mitigate risk. The impact of prohibited practices and prohibited transactions in place at CI will be limited and mitigate risk. AMUC/FT and prohibited practices threaten the reputation of CI and the Project.

### Mitigation

CI has several procedures and controls in place to prevent, mitigate, and identify risk. In order to comply with GCF policies and procedures concerning AMUC/FT, CI has put in place a number of measures including:

- **Risk assessment**: CI conducts an assessment of Prohibited Practices (PP) risks that grant recipients and includes any required mitigation measures.
- **Monitoring and reporting**: CI monitors the risk of prohibited practices through its internal monitoring system (PP including red flags and how to report violations), and all grant and services agreements (used by both CI and third parties) include a clause requiring compliance with GCF policies. The Project will also be subject to GCF policies and mitigation measures is part of project monitoring, including site visits and CI internal audits throughout implementation.
- **Training**: CI provides training on prohibited practices risk for all CI employees.

To report any suspected risks, any CI employee, employee of an executing entity, grantee, partner, or vendor, or beneficiary may report a grievance or violation of these policies anonymously online or by telephone through CI's hotline. All reports will be investigated and reported to CI’s Anti-Fraud Policy & Guidelines for further investigations. Full details can be found on CI’s website at https://www.conservation.org/about-us/policies/reporting-legal-or-unethical-conduct-statement.

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment

The Project is being proposed as Category B or "moderate risk" according to the GCF Project classification. This is because the Project has the potential for limited adverse environmental or social risks and impacts that are few, generally site-specific, largely reversible, and readily addressed through mitigation measures.

### Summary of risks and mitigation measures

#### Environmental
- **Protection of natural habitats:** A lack of action from post-floods of Project interventions may consequently delay Project delivery and result in the continuation of unsustainable practices. Additionally, as the Project will be implemented under collective management, it will require shared responsibility with local communities regarding national legislation and international standards regarding issues such as waste and pollution management.

#### Mitigation factors / measures and management strategies
- **Mitigation measures to address this risk include:** participatory land use mapping; adopting guidelines in a participatory manner (including the involvement of indigenous peoples, women, youth, and FPC); ensuring that Village Development Committees are established at the village level with representatives from beneficiary communities; and facilitating inter-village communication. The Project will also undertake using a stakeholder participation approach to identify potential risks.

#### Social
- **Involuntary resettlement:** The Project's requirement for participation of land users in project locations may trigger restrictions of access, where cattle owners currently have access to grazing areas used by other people's grazing areas. Previously, local communities had been able to graze their cattle on these lands without any coordinated migration of cattle projects because of scarce rangeland resources made even scarcer due to the Project and additional control fences.
- **Indigenous peoples:** The environmental and social suffering seeking to protect their land and livelihoods is triggered by the Project design, as interventions target Basana communities. These communities are an indigenous community who live as herders at cattle ranches in the project area. As such groups, Basana communities are particularly vulnerable to exclusion and marginalisation.

#### Mitigation measures
- **To mitigate this risk:** The Project includes training and support to enhance the capacity of land users to participate effectively in sustainable livestock management. Community training is also provided on the importance of adopting good practices and behaviours towards sustainable, collective use of natural resources.
- **Baseline mapping will be carried out before baseline stakeholder mapping to provide information for better understanding of the project area.

#### An Indigenous Peoples Plan has been developed
- To mitigate against this risk: The Project includes a range of activities that will be carried out to provide baseline data on indigenous peoples' access to natural resources, demographic profiles, economic and social conditions, and their use of resources. Additionally, Free, Prior and Informed Consent (FPIC) will be embedded at all stages of the project. This will include raising awareness of the human rights of minorities in training sessions for staff working with indigenous peoples; a full FPIC process will be undertaken.


---
**Page 178**
---

# Labour and working conditions:

There are risks related to the commitment of gender-based violence (GBV) in the context of the Project. As pastoralism is currently a male-dominated livelihood in Botswana, there is also the risk that women and children will be exposed to labour and gender equity issues with a view to identifying the most effective ways of eliminating these risks. The following mitigation measures are proposed: i) conducting baseline assessments on GBV; ii) developing policies and guidelines for addressing labour and gender equity issues with a view to identifying the most effective ways of eliminating these risks; iii) embedding prohibitions into conservation agreements, including those related to wildlife codes of ethics; iv) embedding penalty #### 266.

The ESMP also provides guidance on the project implementation (drafting of stewardship agreements, etc), the specific nature of any other residual environmental and social risks that remain will be assessed as part of delivery.

#### 267.
Risks will be managed through the measures listed below:

- Involving an experienced environmental and social safeguards specialist throughout the Project planning, design and implementation processes.
- Establishing methodologies for the environmental and social assessment of each of the activities under the Project.
- Ensuring the monitoring and evaluation (M&E) process includes adequate review of environmental and social risks. This will be done by an M&E specialist with specific experience in managing environmental and social considerations.
- Assigning formal roles and responsibilities for environmental and social management across the Project team.
- Establishing a reporting procedure for environmental and social concerns related to the interventions, including through the project level grievance mechanism.
- Incorporating environmental and social safeguard considerations into the training, capacity building and technical assistance delivered through the Project.
- Ensuring environmental and social risks and safeguards are considered in documentation to be produced under the Project, including in stewardship agreements, training materials, human resource policies and codes of ethics.
- A robust grievance mechanism will be put in place for any project stakeholder to file grievances and have them addressed effectively by the Project.
