## G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### 268. During Project preparation, a gender assessment was undertaken to understand the differential vulnerability to, and impact of, climate change on women and men in Nigeria. Based on this analysis, as well as an analysis of the proposed Project solution, a gender action plan (GAP) was developed.

The process to produce these components included a desktop review and analysis of existing policies and legislation, including customary law, as well as key informant interviews, household questionnaires, focus group discussions and consultation workshops with Project stakeholders and beneficiaries.

### 270. The assessment describes the formal and customary legal policies and norms that govern women’s rights in Nigeria. While the formal legal framework is upheld in national legislation and the formal judicial system, in compliance with international human rights standards, customary law is not uniformly enforced by tribal structures and customary courts, which generally have fewer protections for women. For example, the Administration of Estate Act allows customary law to be applied in cases where a woman dies intestate (without a will), leaving men at the expense of women. Moreover, although the Abolition of Martial Power Act (2004) prohibits discrimination on grounds of gender and provides that men and women equal rights within marriage, the Act does not apply to customary or religious marriages. Women married under customary law are considered legal


---
**Page 181**
---

**GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 177**

G

minors and require the permission of their spouse for household decisions and access to finance.

### 271.
In the Project areas, which consist of predominantly rural, pastoral households, women are more likely than men to be reliant upon remittances from family members, public employment schemes such as (piloting), government poverty eradication programs like (pilot), and other informal sources. Women also face additional vulnerabilities if payments are delayed or withheld. The assessment also acknowledges that women may be at greater risk of exposure to violence and other risks to elements of Project implementation. Women may be at greater risk as a result of their physical exposure during Ecoranger activities or from intimate-partner violence, which is prevalent in many of their households. The GAP includes measures to mitigate against these risks.

### 272.
As well as describing men and women’s relationally asymmetrical participation strategies, the gender analysis also identifies gendered problems and problem-salient aspects related to gendered risks and opportunities related to: i) division of labour; ii) access to financial resources; iii) access to land; iv) livestock ownership v) access to water; vi) access to markets; and vii) decision-making. In addition to gender norms in the household, particularly differing roles as caregivers, a cultural preference for male-headed households is also prevalent. These are related to the gendered distribution of roles and livelihoods in the eco-agricultural landscape. These factors expose men and women to different dynamics of risk associated with Project activities. For example, while women may be more exposed to diversified, ongoing risk, men have greater exposure to particularly severe climate-related risks to their livelihoods; income and wealth are generally concentrated in their cattle livestock.

### 273.
To mitigate against introducing gender biases amongst Project participants and beneficiaries during implementation and after Project completion, the Gender Action Plan (GAP) was developed. The GAP identifies salient gendered problems that contribute to perpetuating or worsening gender inequality and gender-based climate vulnerability in Ngariiland, Bokom and Kigalagadi. The GAP identifies salient infrastructure gaps and barriers related to women’s access to land, water, grazing practices and arrangements; it Ecoranger and Restoration Worker trainings; and it provides a framework for addressing gendered problems and gender-based violence risk during Project activities. The plan seeks to promote gender equality by including actions and targets that will ensure women’s participation in project implementation and management. It also acknowledges that women’s relatively lower baseline access to resources, income and wealth requires specific interventions such as disaggregated data collection, gender-sensitive design features and quantifiable performance indicators to promote women’s participation and benefits from the Project.

### 274.
Some of the actions of the GAP include:
- **proactively promoting women's inclusion in all Project aspects**, including training and gender-balanced employment opportunities;
- **pursuing representative participation** at all consultations and workshop events, which will include providing childcare.


---
**Page 182**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.2.0 | PAGE 178

- providing gender sensitive Project materials, that are accessible to target beneficiaries;
- supporting gender mainstreaming into processes and policies of government stakeholders;
- advancing gender diversity and challenging negative stereotyping in public awareness activities;
- designing and implementing gender-sensitive training that considers the different base knowledge levels, learning methods and training accessibility of men and women;
- ensuring that interventions do not exacerbate GBV by including partnered empowerment, working with husbands and male leaders to accompany women-oriented projects, and ensuring that all project documents specifically include considerations for capturing and responding to reports of GBV.

275. CI will also employ a full-time Gender Officer who will be situated within the Project Management Unit, to oversee the implementation of the GAF, as well as provide input into the gender analysis and gender action plan (GAP). The Gender Officer will be supported by CI's global gender team. The full Gender Assessment and Project-level Gender Action Plan are included as FP Annex 8.

### G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 7 pages)

276. The Project will be executed by Ci-Botswana, and therefore, financial management and procurement for this Project will follow relevant established CI policies and procedures, as well as relevant provisions in the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) signed between CI and its AMA signatories.

#### Financial management

277. CI uses Unit 4 Business World software suite as its enterprise resource planning (ERP) system for financial accounting and monitoring in all its global operations. This system supports the Project's financial management, including disbursement of funds in line with the requirements of CI's AMA. It supports a system of ledger accounts to separate and track income and expenditure by donor, project, and subaward. It also provides a coding system that allows for tracking of costs by cost center, project, and subaward. EE and sub-grantees will be required to submit quarterly financial reports that will be systematically reviewed by CI.

278. An annual audit of the Project will be conducted by an independent external audit firm in accordance with audit standards under U.S. GAAP and furnished to the GCF in accordance with CI's AMA.

#### Disbursement

279. Disbursements made to CI will be recorded on the ledger account for the GCF Project, which can be tracked using the Unit 4 Business World system. Disbursements from the EE to sub-grantees will be made upon CI’s receipt of acceptable progress and financial reports and any other documentation required by the GCF.

280. CI has procedures and controls in place to prevent risk of prohibited practices, including ML/FT (collectively: PF). CI applies rigorous CFT/AML screening to all CI funding recipients. CI also assesses PF risks of grant recipients and includes mitigation measures in its grant

## 251. Violations of PP, fraud, or financial misuse may be reported electronically online or by telephone to CI's Anti-Fraud Hotline. All such reports will be investigated and addressed per CI's Anti-Fraud Policy & Guidelines for investigations.

### Procurement

#### 281. All procurement for this project must follow the Procurement Process Standards as defined in the CI Procurement Policy, which requires that all procurements of goods or services ensure that only those goods or services are procured that are necessary and appropriate; goods be used only for their intended purposes and consistent with CI's Code of Ethics and anti-fraud policies. CI's procurement standards are in line with GCF Discovery standards.

#### 281. All procurement done directly by CI will meet CI's internal procurement process, including non-standardized procurement methods such as placing multi-year purchasing agreements (including approval over a set threshold), quarterly reporting on equipment & equipment, periodic inventory checks, and annual audits. The maximum value of any single contract or purchase contract US$30,000 or more awarded by CI's Botswana office, including for this Project.

#### 281. CI will assess the procurement policies of any subgrantee organization conducting procurement for this Project. Project sub-grantees that meet CI's Procurement Process Standards will be required to use their own internal procurement process and procedures for the Project; otherwise, they will be required to use CI's Procurement Policy and standards. The CI Procurement Process Standards and CI's procurement standards will be included in the grant agreement signed between CI and the grantee organization.

#### 281. CI will apply mitigation measures and monitor all procurement for this Project to ensure that only procured goods are used only for their intended purposes. It will provide training on procurement and prohibited practices for CI staff and subgrantees and will approve annual procurement plans and budgets for the Project. The monitoring of procurement activities will be through quarterly reporting on equipment & equipment and through periodic site visits.

# Secretariat's assessment of FP158

## Proposal name: Ecosystem-Based Adaptation and Mitigation in Botswana’s Communal Rangelands

Accredited entity: Conservation International Foundation

Country (ies): Botswana

Project/programme size: Medium

### I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

#### 1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

**Strengths**

- Conservation International (CI) Botswana will implement this project with methods, leadership and experience that have underpinned successful funding for Health project in South Africa.
- This model of building climate-resilient rangelands for the poorest and most climate vulnerable communities is an innovative approach to a sustainable approach that enables communities to generate revenue from their natural resources and exports that can sustain the changes in climate adaptation.

**Points of caution**

- Government commitment is evident in the Government of Botswana’s involvement. A Botswana government entity called [elegwam] will allocate wages to the target beneficiaries.
- The Botswana Livestock Development Committees (6,000 livestock workers [500 in Phase I, 1,500 in Phase II and 4,000 in Phase III]) shift years over 7 years upon signing of Rangeland Management Agreements with Conservation International (CI).
- This arrangement is critical for maintaining responsibility and accountability particularly with the VDCs as part of capacity building and support. CI will have oversight during the period.
- Bottswana staff will build the capacity of the [elegwam] to manage the communal rangelands in: (i) cattle herd management; (ii) disease control; (iii) restoration of pasturlands, and (iv) delivery.

The Botswana Beef Commission will need to be persuaded to give up its monopsony on the sale of commercial beef. Nevertheless, positive change may be possible due to policy reforms, and thanks to signed contracts with support from herders, ranchers and government experts.


---
**Page 194**
---

### Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

#### Project background

The project interventions are designed to significantly increase the adaptive capacity of the people of Botswana to respond to the negative impacts of climate change on the country's communal lands. The project will exclusively service "last mile communities" that farm on non-tenured communal grazing land and have the following objectives through activities holistically designed for:

(a) Strengthen institutions and support systems for climate-responsive planning and management in communal rangelands.

(b) Reduce emissions and negative livelihood impacts through rangeland rehabilitation and improved livestock production;

(c) Sustain enhanced adaptive capacity and low-emission development through value chain and policy transformation.

The project will be implemented in these three target areas of Botswana: Ngamiland (north-west), Kgalagadi District (south-east), and the Central Kalahari Game Reserve. The Ngamiland GCF project intervention by national stakeholders in 2016 due to their high climate vulnerability, with a population density of approximately 240 people/km² and an average population density of 2.6 people/km².

Data in the proposal show that temperatures across Botswana are rising, as projected to rise from 35°C (95°F) to 41°C (106°F). In Ngamiland, the poorest district in Botswana, more than 8,000 cattle were persistently in 2019 about 20 per cent of the total herd), desiccating and dying. This has led to a significant increase in livestock mortality rates during times of drought as livestock weaken and move into wildlife areas in search of fodder and water. Consequently, there is an increased risk of disease transmission between cattle and buffalo with infectious foot-and-mouth disease. Such contact has closed export markets for cattle from Ngamiland. The project will also address the threat posed by emerging zoonotic disease vectors provoked by climate change; this project will restore the grazing vegetation in Ngamiland and two other regions of communal grazing lands most impacted by climate change. The restoration and management of grazing vegetation will hold more moisture in the soil to


---
**Page 195**
---

make cattle ranching more resilient to drought. This same activity will protect and enhance soil carbon and reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions from soils and cattle.

6. The project, with the extensive support of farming communities, the Government of Botswana, civil society organizations, and non-governmental organizations (NGOs), will replicate and scale up a successful model of communal rangeland management that was pioneered in South Africa by Conservation International.

7. The proposed project is planned to be financed solely by grants, and it is confirmed to be an environmental and social safeguard category B project. The project's indicative costs show that proposed GCF funding will cover 90% of the total cost (USD 132.5 million). This includes USD 67.8 million from the GCF, USD 4.7 million from the Government of Botswana, and USD 59.9 million from other sources. Co-financing will be from the prolonged extending public works programme. The GCF contribution is envisaged to be USD 36.76 million, as developed via the GCF Project Preparation Facility.

### Component-by-component analysis

#### Component 1: Strengthening institutions and support systems for climate-resilient planning and management (total cost: USD 14,578,036; GCF USD 13,988,878 at a per unit cost)

- GCF resources will support the Department of Meteorology and Water Resources to engage with stakeholders to reduce GHG emissions and increase climate resilience of cattle grazing for the last-mile communities. This component will also support the Department of Environment and Tourism in Botswana (north-west), Kgatlago (south-west), and Bobwana (east-central). Botswana government funding is being allocated to support 5,000 livestock producers over 8 years. It will allow them to access improved feed resources, water points, and veterinary services provided by local health monitors, and rangeland restoration workers. Their work will allow communities to meet commodity market demands for beef and milk while reducing the risk of conflict between pastoral interests from escalation of human–wildlife conflict. This component will also invest in data gathering and analysis tools to improve decision-making on land use planning, livestock management ability and knowledge of climate adaptation and migration impacts.

- This component will enhance planning and development of climate-resilient information portal to consolidate information linked to the early warning system managed by the Department of Meteorology and Water Resources. It will also support the Department of Environment and Tourism in rangeland degradation, as well as wildlife monitoring systems for farmers and encroachers. It will link with the Department of Agriculture and Livestock Development, the Ministry of Environment and Tourism, and the Ministry of Agriculture's Ipingwe page accounts to provide monthly monitoring data on impact and productivity for decision-making at the level of village development committees (VDCs), livestock producers, and other stakeholders.

#### Component 2: Reducing GHG emissions and negative livelihood impacts through new job opportunities in rangeland management (total cost: USD 14,564,854; GCF USD 13,970,047 at a per unit cost)

- GCF resources will support the execution of rangeland stewardship agreements with affected communities. This component will also provide incentives for livestock producers to be incentivized by improved livestock production and training support that will be sustained through the project. The Department of Environment and Tourism in Botswana (north-west) will rangeland science and gender specialist support to develop a baseline assessment and recommendations for a three-year grazing plan to inform the development of rangeland stewardship agreements with affected communities. This component will also fund 104 VDC grazing area baseline assessments, completed and stored in the rangeland stewardship


---
**Page 196**
---

information portal and in VIC rangeland stewardship agreement counterparties. CI will use an in-kind grant agreements to provide technology and equipment for restoration teams to use in implementing the agreed rangeland stewardship agreement. The "grazing support package" of in-kind grants will be provided by CI to each grazing team, with a total value of $10 000 per unit of equipment per 600 animals. It will include a predator-proof livestock enclosure, encourage unrestricted access to water and feed, provide for the installation of solar-powered fencing as well as routine maintenance. The equipment will be offered through an in-kind grant between CI and the organization supporting VICs with the implementation of the rangeland stewardship agreement.

13. This component will enhance engagement and cooperation with the private sector, in particular livestock producers, by building on a solid foundation of the alignment of existing and planned work of partners already active in livestock value chains.

**Component 3: Promoting climate-sensitive enterprise development and value chain investments to sustain livelihoods and reduce vulnerability**

14. GCF resources will be directed towards sustaining adaptive capacity for growing the long-term market demands of derived cattle products from communities completely dependent on livestock production, while also supporting existing value chain incentives and supply chains for rangeland products that are also resilient to the impacts of climate change. The development of these enterprises is complex and will require the removal of barriers at multiple points along the new value chain for the project's beneficiaries. Investments producing key outputs will be made in market-readiness, technology transfer, and capacity building initiatives. These include Mead Naturally Botswana. The project entry points are the National Strategy Office and Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Water Resources (MAFWR), the Department of Entrepreneurial Development Agency (CEDA) and its sister institution, the Local Enterprise Authority (LEA), as well as the private sector through the Mead Naturally Botswana enterprise initiatives. Mead Naturally Botswana is a social enterprise association that emerged from CFS with support from CI. The project will also work closely with the Ministry of Agriculture in partnership with H&M in providing market access incentives and innovative financial flow models for communities involved in climate-smart livestock management.

15. This component will focus on policy, institutional change and finance policy transformation to support the demand side of project interventions and the overall sustainability of the enterprise development initiatives. Enterprise development are new initiatives that have gained political momentum since 2018, with new regulations expected in 2020 to support both. The parastatal CEDA and BMA, among others, are already embracing the need for a more inclusive approach to rural development and private sector interest (BMC management) in supporting this project as an exemplar of how climate resilience can be achieved through sustainable livelihoods, increasing local, inclusive economic empowerment to achieve a paradigm shift for the livestock sector.

**Component 4: Evaluation and project management cost (total: USD 271.834; GCF/AFD/LI/000000000)**

16. Project area monitoring and evaluating officers, and the Department of Forestry and Range Resources will ground-truth incoming data and undertake basic measurements and analysis as representative components of the impact monitoring system will measure and report

**Page 197**

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

- The project will serve **247,000** direct beneficiaries (175,000 people in the farming population and 71,000 people in value-added, rangeland-related economic activities), 51 per cent of them women, including overall:
    - **2,290,000** indirect beneficiaries (representing the entirety of the country).
- About 30 per cent of the population in the target regions, and 10 per cent of the national population; and
- **5,500** ecologists, 500 restoration workers and 83 project staff who will acquire new professional skills.

Adaptive capacity and awareness will be strengthened across all stakeholder groups – communities, farmers, herders, government and the private sector. The project will also support the restoration of degraded land in the target regions through a combination of natural regeneration and climate-resilient livestock production. This will include the application of 9 demonstration projects to restore degraded lands and improve livelihoods for local communities and will apply a tailored grazing support package. Support will include: active vegetation; erosion-prevention measures such as contour bunding, terracing, and interseeding; and innovative corralling to concentrate manure and break up hard-packed soil surfaces.

Total emission reductions during the implementation phase are expected to be **47 MTCO2e** (10 per cent of Botswana’s national carbon budget). This is 47 times greater than Botswana's nationally determined contribution target of a 15 per cent reduction in national emissions) The mitigation potential of this project is therefore significant.

The project conducted the baseline and additional analysis using a project-specific approach, as no national GHG inventory exists for Botswana. The results are presented below. This has demonstrated that the current practices will continue in the absence of the project, owing to common practice and finance barriers, and thus the project is additional. The baseline and project scenarios were developed based on the guidance provided by the UNFCCC and IPCC Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories (2019 Refinement). Appropriate emission factors were used where available from the literature or from the Botswana Department of Environment. It should be noted that Botswana's most recent national GHG inventory uses the less conservative Tier 1 approach. There are no plans to update this inventory in time for submission with the project application. However, the project approach has been accepted as it is more conservative and aligns well with Botswana’s plans to switch to Tier 2 in next submission of the national GHG inventory. The project has also provided the data sources and references used in the estimates of mitigation impact.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium to high


---
**Page 198**
---

## Sustainable development potential

The project aims to influence government policy and repurposing of the pledging funds. Climate-resilient rangeland management that promotes ecosystem-based adaptation and mitigation will be implemented in a way that maximizes co-benefits. Other benefits will include: support for the ecranger programme; increased income from the sale of cattle, sheep, goats and other livestock (e.g., through the Grassed Beef Cluster strategy); increased engagement of women, youth and men; reduction in human-wildlife conflict; improved management of project sites under the Wildlife Coexistence Strategy of the Ministry of Environment, Natural Resources Conservation and Tourism; reduction in risk of zoonotic disease outbreaks and more resilient livestock; provision of opportunities for rural communities to participate in decision-making processes through their involvement, including increased access to credit through CEDA and Local Enterprise Authority loans offered by the financial sector.

### Needs of the recipient

Scale: High


---
**Page 199**
---

24. The proposal has justified the need for ecosystem-based adaptation measures in response to climate and livestock risks given the dependence of the target populations on income from livestock production. Characterized by poverty and climate vulnerability, the beneficiaries are rural communities who depend heavily on livestock as a source of income irrespective of cultural ways of life, given the challenges of changing climate and modern economy.

25. The proposal offers a unique investment in terms of the nuanced needs of the different groups it seeks to support. It is proposed that women and children will be supported through rangeland stewardship activities while men and young people will be supported through VDCs which act as a partner for the implementation of this proposal.

26. Justification has been provided for the country-specific allocation of GCF funds and the need for strengthening institutions and implementation capacity.


### 3.5 Country ownership Scale: High

27. The proposal addresses high-level goals described in national strategic documents of Botswana, including those of:

(a) The National Adaptation Plan Framework (2020), which (i) supports community-based adaptation leading as this relates to subsistence livestock farming; and (ii) stresses the need to engage and leverage the private sector through insurance schemes and finance mechanisms. This is in line with Botswana’s national development strategy EKOA, which is a partner for the implementation of this proposal;

(b) The country's nationally determined contribution, which explicitly refers to implementing projects that will reduce the livestock sector to reduce methane emissions, mainly from enteric fermentation.

28. The accredited entity (AEI) has conducted consultations with the relevant local stakeholders and communities in Botswana.

29. The project’s proposed contributions to the design and testing of new regulations/legislating policies within the Ministry of Agriculture and the Ministry of Local Government are a clear indication that this proposal is country-owned.

30. The country's commitment is evidenced through the USD 54 million co-financing contribution from the Government of Botswana.


### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: High

31. The GCF-funded cost of mitigation is USD 1,721 per tCO2eq. Over 20 years the total project cost of mitigation is estimated at USD 45.2 million.

32. In terms of benefits, the average cost per beneficiary is USD 195 (USD 95 GCF cost only; USD 148) for Total beneficiaries, it is estimated as USD 102 per beneficiary. This is justified due to the relatively low population of Botswana and the dispersed population in the selected project sites.

33. In terms of improving ecosystems, the aimed improved management of natural resources will result in a net increase in carbon stock by an average of 45 tCO2eq/ha/year, for which the US$180 is GCF cost. The low cost of restoration is significant as the annual cost of mitigation per hectare estimated to be USD 353 million, equivalent to 3.2 per cent of the country's gross domestic product.

# Environmental and social safeguards

## 4.1 Environmental and social risk category and safeguard instruments

The project is aimed at strengthening the capacity of communities in the communal rangelands and at strengthening institutions' capacities for climate-responsive planning and management in the three target areas of Ngamiland (north-west), Kgalagadi (south-east), and Botswana (north). The project will also contribute to improved livelihoods, increased food security, increased biodiversity, improved ecosystem health, better water conservation, and increased natural productivity. These benefits are expected as a result of more resilient fodder resources for livestock-based livelihoods. The project also promotes the sustainable use of rangelands through climate-resilient land-use planning and management. It is expected that there will be reduced human-wildlife conflicts and that the project will provide more sustainable farming and ecotourism-based livelihoods for the communities.

The project is proposed as Category B, having potential limited adverse environmental and/or social impacts. The project has been assessed against the AEI's environmental and social risk assessment criteria and is within the AEI’s environmental and social risk acceleration level. The project has prepared an Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) and an Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP).

Compliance with GEF environmental and social safeguards (ESS) standards: The following ESS standards are relevant to this project:

### ESS 1: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts

The potential environmental and social risks and impacts of the project have been assessed. Environmental and social risk assessments for each of the identified impacts were conducted. The various safeguard instruments have also defined specific monitoring, reporting, and corrective action procedures to manage the environmental and social impacts posed by the project. The project intends to monitor various environmental and social indicators through the Rangeland Stewardship Information System (RSIS). These include livestock population trends, availability of fodder, stocks of carbon in soil and vegetation, and fire vulnerability. It will also design systems for monitoring and reporting on the management of rangelands at three levels: community, local government, and national level. A comprehensive environmental and social monitoring plan will be developed which will indicate the frequency of monitoring various indicators and the specific data that need to be collected as well as the need for corrective actions. The monitoring and reporting procedures will facilitate early detection of any adverse impacts and allow for specific action plans for their implementation. The monitoring plan will further ensure that reporting is continued on a regular basis and will include responsible personnel who will carry out the mitigation and improvement measures in case of any identified risks.

### ESS 2: Labour and working conditions

The project seeks to employ an equal number of women and men as labourers, although there are some areas where the provision of labour as herders is a risk that the women and girls may be expected as free labour. As pastoralism is also a male-dominated livelihood in Botswana, there is the potential for gender-based discrimination. The project will collect baseline data on the spatial patterns of land-use by men and women as well as on the cattle and small stock herds managed by each group. This information will help to identify where these risks are avoided or appropriately mitigated. The project approaches will also ensure that all workers, including women, receive fair wages for their work while respecting the minimum wage. The AEI has also considered the impacts of projects on working conditions, terms of employment, workers organization, non-discrimination,


---
**Page 201**
---

### ESS 3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention

The project focuses on rangeland restoration activities where no use of pesticides or ex-situ livestock feeding or damming is required. The project will also address resource efficiency and pollution prevention, including raising awareness and support to smallholder farmers in the shared rangelands.

### ESS 4: Community health, safety, and security

The collective rotational grazing approach proposed for this project may bring unprecedented numbers of animals to be herded on a single piece of land. This will likely result in new or increased community health, safety and security issues that will be particular to this type of land and livestock management system. The project will therefore need to develop an appropriate Community Health, Safety, and Security Management Plan (CHSSMP) for the project area. The CHSSMP must include a risk assessment plan that must be developed through a participatory stakeholder engagement. To this end, the project has articulated an Indicative Community Health, Safety, and Security Management Plan (I-CHSSMP). This will guide the development of the CHSSMP for the project area.

### ESS 5: Avoidance and/or mitigation of involuntary evictions

While the project will focus on voluntary and consensual agreements, it has captured concerns from stakeholders on the potential risk that the project may inadvertently lead to the eviction of pastoralists who directly or indirectly to the loss of traditional/subsistence livelihoods of migrant settlers. To manage this risk, the project will employ participatory planning of the grazing areas that will be impacted by the project and ensure that all affected parties are involved in the process of negotiations among all affected livestock owners will be achieved. Guided facilitation processes will be used to help stakeholders reach agreements on land use rights and apply appropriate safeguards in reaching conservation agreements. This includes analysis of any proposed evictions, including those that may occur as a result of the project, which can affect people who depend on their livelihoods. Documentation of the consultation process will be provided for all affected parties. The project will also ensure that no action is needed through an appropriate process framework to voluntarily restrict access and use of land by pastoralists in the shared rangelands where the project will be implemented. This is particularly important as the focus of the project is exclusively on the last-mile communities under communal land tenure systems (tribal lands).

### ESS 6: Biodiversity conservation and protection of natural resources

Biodiversity conservation and the protection of natural habitats is at the forefront of the project activities through the design and implementation of the degraded rangeland recovery. However, to achieve a satisfactory outcome, farmers in the shared rangelands should have complete buy-in so that collective action can prevent continued degradation of the land. To this end, the project will ensure that communities will not invest in areas where compliance for climate-resilient grazing and restoration planning is not achieved. The project will therefore provide technical assistance, focused training, information sharing, and process facilitation in behavioural change to be able to mobilize farmers and the communities in making informed decisions in their


---
**Page 202**
---

participation. The project will likewise enhance the capacity of land authorities as regards sustainable land and livestock management.

GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS 7: Indigenous peoples. The AE through the ESIA has determined that six Basarwa settlements will be included in the second phase of project implementation. These communities are vulnerable to adverse impacts related to climate change. As some members of this community live as herders at cattle posts owned by the government, they have limited access to land and water resources. The AE will capture their concerns and indicate measures to reduce risks of exclusion and marginalization in the project design phase. This will be done through a series of consultations with the affected communities, a process for free, prior and informed consent and plans for continuing stakeholder engagement.

(ESS 7) Indigenous peoples and cultural heritage. The project may have adverse impacts on cultural heritage. Farmers and communities involved in the project will be engaged in the planning phase to ensure that their concerns are addressed. Any potential impacts on cultural resources will be appropriately considered and avoided.

Implementation arrangements. The AE through the executing entity (EE), Government of Botswana, has established a Project Management Unit (PMU) which will consist of full-time staff who will be responsible for the delivery and coordination of project activities. The PMU will also be responsible to the ESIAF including the various risk mitigation measures to ensure consistency with AE and GCF policies, including on environmental and social safeguards. The project will likewise involve a number of stakeholders in the planning phase as well as during the planning, design and implementation stages of the project. Further details as regards responsibilities for the PMU and other arrangements related to project delivery, reviews and monitoring and evaluation are expected to be finalized during project inception.

Stakeholder engagement. The project has conducted various consultations with different stakeholders including government departments such as the Ministry of Agriculture and environment sector, local governments, private sector and climate and range management organizations. These consultations have been carried out through initial workshops, individual meetings, focus group discussions and validation workshops. The AE has ensured that all these activities are carried out in the local language. Various activity-level stakeholder engagement plans are also developed to ensure that the project is delivered with the support of stakeholders. The AE ensures various components of the project to be considered, particularly on how the project is going to affect them from their perspectives.

Grievance redress mechanisms. The AE has designed a Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) to facilitate the resolution of grievances at the project level to allow for an early identification and mitigation of potential risks related to project-related grievances. In addition, the GCF independent Redress Mechanism and the AE's Grievance Mechanism (Through the Director of Compliance or Ethics Officer) will also be made available to address any grievances that may arise during implementation. The Secretary's indigenous peoples focal point will be available for assistance at any stage, including through the project inception phase. The GCF independent redress mechanisms will also accommodate project-related gender-based violence concerns.

4.2 Gender policy

The AE has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan, and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.


---
**Page 203**
---

### Gender Assessment

The gender assessment has benefited from a desk review and information collected through stakeholder consultations at the three project sites. It demonstrates the existence of an enabling environment in the country to promote gender equality and women's empowerment, and the commitment by the Government of Botswana (GoB) to mainstream gender into its policy and the GCF Gender Policy. The Government of Botswana has policies in place to address the inequalities between men and women, as well as the GoB’s National Gender and Development mandate all line ministries to mainstream gender in sector policies and programmes. The Constitution already promotes equality for all regardless of race, place of origin, political opinion, colour, language, religion or ethnic background. Section 108 of the 2009 Constitution ensured that all relevant laws were aligned to the principles of the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the provisions of the Southern African Development Community’s Protocol on Gender and Development.

The gender assessment clearly demonstrates the opportunities and challenges that women in the project sites face, particularly with respect to their access to resources on communal rangelands. It also illustrates the differentiated negative impact of climate change on rangeland-dependent communities, which are predominantly rural and poor and depend on livestock for livelihoods. For women, the challenges are seen in terms of increased water stress and the need to travel further distances to collect water; reduced access to land for grazing and food production; potential for conflict in the home, as there are differences in priorities on the use of financial resources; and limited opportunities for income generation due to their position in hierarchical systems and men’s dominance in decision-making in almost every aspect. Women are also less skilled and have little opportunity to engage in alternative employment opportunities that could provide them with an additional source of income.

The other source of income for the communities is cattle. Cattle ownership is dominated by males, who control all aspects of the livestock enterprise, including decisions about which type of income can be generated. In the case of men, as the local economic and environmental situation deteriorates, they are more likely to sell their livestock in order to meet basic needs. Some women turn to sex work or transactional sex to earn income times of climate stress. These activities are often stigmatized by society and are not considered legitimate sources of income for many women survive through income generated from working in spiegling, while women those more fortunate can engage in small-scale farming.

The savings are mostly spent on meeting their household needs and supporting them in fulfilling their care responsibilities. Challenges to women’s personal land ownership, inheritance and joint titles remain significant. Women have limited access to credit facilities or other financial services covered by the project; women own at least a ploughing field as a minimum, for which they have a certificate of title, but this is not enough to secure loans from banks or other institutions in the homes they live in, which is an advantage in terms of assets. Single women, although much poorer economically than married women, are more likely to own land.

The identification of the existing gender-based challenges and opportunities has allowed the AE to develop a gender action plan as part of its overall action plan to mitigate climate change-induced negative impacts, in particular on women.

### Gender Action Plan

The AE has submitted a gender action plan complying with the requirements of the Gender Policy. The plan identifies key areas for gender mainstreaming including responsibilities (gender experts in place) and budget. The activities respond to the findings of the gender assessment and are designed to address the identified gender-based challenges aspects, including: training and gender transformative employment opportunities; participation of women in all consultations and workshop events, supplemented with childcare; production of gender-sensitive materials; and provision of financial support for mainstreaming gender into processes and policies of government departments.

### Risks

#### Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

1. GCF is requested to provide a grant of USD 36.7 million towards the total project cost of USD 97.4 million. The funding proposal presents USD 60.9 million in co-financing from the Government of Botswana and CL will be also the AE and the executing entity (EE) for the project.

#### Accredited entity/executing capacity to execute the current programme (medium risk)

1. CI will serve as the AE and EE for the project. It has a 20-year track record in conservation. It has been operating in Botswana for over 20 years and will scale up operations to facilitate the project. Of the GCF funding, 4% per year will go to cover staff costs on CI. Therefore, CI is well positioned to manage and execute the project and also to plan, finance, and manage capable new and existing staff.

#### Project-specific execution risks (high risk)

1. Co-financing: Co-financing by the Government of Botswana is subject to annual budget review and approval processes. The AE declared in the term sheet that it does not have the legal standing or authority to enter into binding agreements on behalf of the government, its representatives, and does not undertake any legal covenants in relation to the effectiveness or continued availability of co-financing. The co-financing is critical to all elements of the project.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP15B

## Proposal name:
Ecosystem-Based Adaptation and Mitigation in Botswana’s Communal Rangelands

## Accredited entity: 
Conservation International Foundation

## Project/programme size: 
Medium

### I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

#### 1.1 Impact potential Scale: High

##### 1.1.1 Rangelands are important ecosystems in many parts of the world.
- They represent about 70 per cent of the earth’s land surface (excluding Antarctica) and cover >90 per cent of the Southern African region.

##### 1.1.2 Managed rangelands offer substantial climate change mitigation potential through improved management of soil, which will deliver multiple benefits for pastoralists utilising them for livestock-rearing.
- Sustainably managed rangelands are a primary example of utilizing nature-based solutions and ecosystem-based approaches to deliver climate mitigation.

##### 1.1.3 Pastoralism is a key aspect of agriculture in Southern Africa, accounting for a substantial share of the region’s GDP and employment. It also provides livestock products that are important both for the regional markets as well as substantial export value.
- It is an important aspect of the culture and traditions of the countries in the region, but has now become highly exposed to the risk of climate change.

##### 1.1.4 The project will directly benefit about 8 per cent of the population of Botswana, and indirectly benefit a further 20 per cent.
- This represents an estimated reduction in carbon emissions equivalent to the equivalent of 17 per cent of Botswana’s 2016 carbon dioxide (CO₂) emissions. Given the project size and the proposed GCF investment, these are substantial impacts that can be achieved for a relatively small investment.

##### 3.3.3 Adaptation impacts

4. The proposed project will support Botswana's communal livestock farmers in the
- Bobirwa (northeast-central), Kgalagadi (south-east) and Ngamiland (north-west) regions to adapt to the anticipated climate change impacts of increased temperatures, more frequent dry seasons, and increased frequency of drought and fire. It aims to do so through active restoration of degraded rangelands, improved water management practices, enhanced vegetation cover and soil moisture and fertility, improving grazing potential and productivity, and preventing overstocking through regular offtake.

5. Climate change is expected to result in a potential for increased temperatures and a higher frequency of climate-induced disasters across Southern Africa.
- Botswana's second national communication (2016) to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change estimated an increase of 2°C and a 3–9 per cent decline in rainfall by 2050 across the country.

As a region, Southern Africa is highly vulnerable to climate change, and Botswana, as an inland,
- dryland country within Southern Africa, is especially exposed to climate risks.


---
**Page 210**
---

6. These expected climatic changes are likely to further accelerate agricultural land degradation already under way as a result of climate impacts combined with unsustainable rangeland/monocrop management practices, and thereby reduce agricultural productivity. This would in turn increase poverty levels and food insecurity.

7. Botswana is one such country that is particularly vulnerable to these climatic changes, being characterized by high mortality and is particularly exposed to climatic shocks. According to the NDC-Government of Botswana (2015), 43% of the population lives in areas with a high vulnerability index and only the eighty-fourth most ready to respond. Thus, while Botswana has some capacity to adapt, natural resource degradation will present significant challenges in order to ensure that climate change does not fundamentally disrupt traditional economic patterns or lead to irreversible change.

8. However, because of the specific impact of climate change on Botswana and the intervention regions of the project is not straightforward. The independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) recognizes there is a dearth of point-source-based long-term meteorological climatic data, an issue that will be a priority for the project to address. Limitations apply both to the time over which data exist, and the geographical range they cover.

9. Impact assessment was conducted by the TAP against the criteria set out in the accredited entity (AE) and validated as a grizzly to long-term observational data. The AE then confirmed these against the available observations representative of the target regions. This exercise showed fair consistency between the modelled and observed precipitation for the months of December, January and February. However, it did not provide an indication of a gradient decline in the Standardized Precipitation Index (SPI) as expected from the climate change scenario. The AE concluded that a gradual build-up of drought in the target areas.

10. The reasons for the selection of projection scenarios are therefore clear. However, it must be accepted that this is based on less than observational data would be. Nevertheless, considering that Botswana is a sub-Saharan country lacking long-term meteorological observations, this is considered to be an acceptable approach given the limitations in model validation and also the weakness in the quality of model outputs for future projections of climatic conditions.

11. Mitigation impacts

12. Livestock on rangelands are estimated to contribute 2-4 per cent of global greenhouse gas emissions, similar to the aviation sector. Interventions in soil restoration for improved carbon sequestration and reduced enteric fermentation through improved management and fodder production have been identified as key mitigation measures within the project area. It has been estimated that the global carbon mitigation potential of rangeland can be as high as 1.1 billion Gt CO2-eq.

13. The land area to be improved through this project covers over 8 per cent of the total landmass of Botswana. By restoring the quality of such a large share of land and thereby enhancing its productivity, it is estimated that the project will deliver emission reductions of more than 21 million tonnes CO2-eq over its lifetime. Once fully implemented, the project is expected to reduce emissions by an amount equivalent to just under 17 per cent of Botswana's total emissions in 2016.

14. Of the climate mitigation results, just under 90 per cent will be achieved through soil sequestration and improved grassland management, with the remaining 10 per cent from brush removal that also allows grass cover to increase underneath brush canopy. The interventions and materials used are considered to have a low environmental impact and this are considered highly sustainable and expected to lead to improved livestock conditions.

15. This impact potential is further enhanced due to the high level of replicability across other rangelands in Botswana and beyond. Overall, the independent TAP considers the mitigation impact of the project to be high.


---
**Page 211**
---

1.3.3 Data quality

* The independent TAP understands that the foundation for a better understanding of, and ability to react to, climate impacts is the existence of a robust and modern climate information system.
* While the project will provide some data into this current system through the implementation of the project activities, it will not be able to fully capture the project’s impact on the capacity of community rangers, investment in expanding and modernizing the overall meteorological observation systems or other related requirements.

1.3.4 Improved meteorological systems that strengthen long-term weather forecasting and enables early warning of drought will also be important for enhancing livestock farmers’ ability to adapt to climate change impacts and reduce vulnerability to climate extremes.

* There are considerable uncertainties with regard to the data quality of soil sequestration of CO2.
* The project will measure this throughout the implementation lifetime, and thus allow for the collection of relevant data that can be considered in the design of future rangeland mitigation projects.

1.3.5 Despite the limitations set out above in relation to adaptation impacts, the independent TAP considers the impact potential to be high.


## 1.2 Paradigm shift potential

* The project interventions are well considered and aligned to deliver a comprehensive paradigm shift from traditional pastoralism towards sustainable rangeland management.
* The combination of rangeland degradation with negative climate mitigation impacts, with increasing competition for land use between livestock production and human settlement conflict, presents a potential downward spiral in the viability of a traditional lifestyle that is important to the economy while preserving and enhancing livelihoods.

### 1.2.1 Potential impact

* The project interventions are well aligned to move towards rangeland pastoral livestock management towards a positive feedback loop to support sustainable approaches through improved access to markets, increased productivity and better land use.
* In the absence of the project, the livestock sector is expected to continue underperforming compared to its potential to contribute a stronger performance of the economy of Botswana, and to the well-being of communities associated pastoral communities.


## 1.2.3 Risks to paradigm shift

* The risks relate to the dependence of the project for success on parallel activities.
* It will at least in part depend on the successful implementation of initiatives that do not form part of the project but are necessary for the overall success of the project, such as the transfer of livestock from Africa to private sector operators. Close engagement with the Government will be necessary to ensure these happen in a manner fully aligned with project implementation.

### 1.2.3.1 Innovative approach

* The project is also innovative and builds on the successful Herding for Health project in South Africa.
* This innovative approach, which will be replicated in the intervention areas of Botswana, has shown that there is potential to deliver lasting paradigm shift and behaviour change by livestock farmers, whose increased market access provides an ongoing incentive to practise sustainable rangeland management. The approach has been adopted beyond the initial project areas there.


---
**Page 212**
---

1.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

The sustainable development potential of the project is substantial. However, it is also considered high risk and needs to be carefully managed during the implementation in order to ensure the achievement of the potential outcomes.

### Economic co-benefits

The national herd is an increasingly important element of Botswana's economy, and beef is the second most important export; non-meat demands are excluded. Traditional livestock management on communal land accounts for 98 percent of the national herd, and the changes promoted by the project will have a significant impact in this area. The project has the potential to substantially widen economic impacts by providing alternative routes to market, employment, value addition opportunities, and upscaling of agricultural workers. Therefore, it has the potential to contribute significantly towards poverty reduction and rural income generation in-country.

### Social co-benefits

Livestock are a core element of rural culture and traditions, and social cohesion is critically important to the delivery of a successful project. Social co-benefits include


---
**Page 213**
---

# Environmental Co-benefits

The proposed rangeland stewardship agreements will include environmental conservation measures, requiring a sustainable, targeted approach to managing bush encroachment and wildlife conflict. For example, the proposed different approaches to managing bush encroachment could include prescribed burning, mechanical clearing, as well as improvements to animal welfare through the training of eco-rangers in basic veterinary skills, will deliver significant co-benefits.

## Restoration activities

Restoration activities, such as revegetation, erosion prevention, break-up of hard surfaces, fire management, livestock grazing practices that allow planned rotational rest, corralling and fencing, and other land use changes, can have a positive impact on biodiversity by maintaining floral diversity and habitats for small fauna.

### Gender-related co-benefits

Women are a salient minority of livestock owners in Botswana. For example, women own 25% of the cattle in the Central Districts. Therefore, they can be expected to have an important stake in the successful implementation of the project at the same time, women are more vulnerable because of the traditional roles assigned to them, which increase pressure on their resources and reduce their access to information and services related with social safety nets. Project activities addressing farmers' welfare will therefore benefit women farmers as well.

## Comprehensive gender action plan

A comprehensive gender action plan has been developed. Its implementation will be assured by a full-time gender officer in the project management unit, supported by the global gender focal point at GCF. The gender action plan includes measures to promote training and employment, targeting training based on different knowledge levels, and learning and access opportunities for women farmers. It also includes measures to prevent and respond to the targeting of potential incidents of gender-based violence. The independent TAP welcomes the inclusion in the project of a gender action plan.

## Stakeholder involvement

Considering the important sustainable development benefits and the close attention to stakeholder involvement in the development of the project, the independent TAP finds the sustainable development potential to be high.


---
**Page 214**
---

as a sub-Saharan African country, Botswana is recognized as being particularly vulnerable while having urgent and immediate needs. Moreover, the project will focus on adaptation and mitigation in the critical agriculture sector through livestock and rangeland management. The livestock and rangeland sector is of great importance to the economy of Botswana, and the largest agricultural export by value in 2018. It is particularly important for rural regions.

The direct beneficiaries of the project amount to about B per cent of the total population of Botswana and are members of marginalized groups, often dependent on the social safety net. These marginalised communities are highly vulnerable to climate change impacts, especially drought, which lead to loss of herds, poorer health of animals, and lower market prices.

The project will address these marginalized communities directly and exclusively, focusing its support on them and excluding commercial livestock farming. It will work with community-based organizations (CBOs) and local government authorities through land stewardship agreements. The establishment of new pathways to market and the introduction of management practices that reduce vulnerability in the livestock sector, such as the ability to sell meat and a virtuous cycle preventing overstocking, will be highly beneficial to these communities and the country overall. This approach will hold high relevance to local needs.

Considering the above, the independent TAP rates the needs of the recipient as high.

### 1.5.3 Alignment with national climate strategy and policies

The project is aligned with the nationally determined contribution (2015), which stresses the importance of adaptation in the agriculture sector and specific actions outlined in the second national communication (2013). The national legislation is currently under preparation and is expected to include agricultural interventions.


## 1.5.2 Capacity of accredited entities or executing entities to deliver

The AE for the project is Conservation International (CI).

The Government of Botswana has requested CI also to be the executing entity for the project, in accordance with the agreement between CI and the Conservation International Foundation.

Recognising the request, CI will self-execute this project through the Conservation International Foundation. This means that CI Botswana will be responsible for project execution, management of subgrantees and their respective activities, as well as all other aspects of project implementation (including subgrant agreements, services agreements, and memoranda of understanding) for this project.

CI has relevant experience and expertise in the sector as well as in the region in the implementation of projects related to climate-resilient agriculture and food security. CI has already self-executed similar projects in Botswana and other countries, including


## 1.5.3 Engagement with civil society organisations and other relevant stakeholders

The formation process included consultations and meetings with relevant ministries, stakeholders and donors. To further assess the impact of climate change on livelihoods and the potential impact of the proposed measures, members of local communities were also consulted.

### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium

#### 1.6.1 Overview

The project proposes a budget of USD 98 million, of which about USD 37 million (38 per cent of the total) is requested from GCF in the form of grants. The All will provide about USD 7 million, and the European Union will provide about USD 20 million. Co-financing for projects that are not co-funded as co-financing are being undertaken by Agence Française de Développement and the European Union. The total co-financing amount is USD 61 million, which represents 62 per cent of the project cost.

The climatic objective of the project is to achieve mitigation and adaptation outcomes through improved livestock management practices in the rangelands of Kenya. This requires the participation and active support of GCF as neither the financial requirements nor the technical capacity needs can be met in the absence of its support. Indeed, almost the requested amount will be spent on the purchase of equipment for the training and deployment of eco-rangers. However, it would not enable the necessary training and equipment purchases to ensure that the project achieves its objectives. The project is also expected to have a positive effect on achieving and tracking of climate change adaptation and mitigation results. Moreover, the overall impact will be enhanced by the fact that the project has a high cross-border and in-country replication potential as it is a replication of a similar project in South Africa.

#### 1.6.2 Economic analysis

The funding proposal identifies the following streams of potential benefits from the project:

- improved marketability of beef;
- improved adaptive capacity to climate-induced adverse events;
- climate mitigation benefits from dietary changes to cattle and soil sequestration of carbon;
- livestock management gains from improved training of eco-rangers.

52. The benefit-cost estimation considered a time-horizon of 20 years for the project. While it differs in terms of cost per beneficiary compared with other projects, it is still positive in terms of cost to similar projects undertaken by others. It also has high cross-border and in-country replication potential as it is a replication of a similar project in South Africa.

53. Analysis using the GCF’s internal benefit-cost ratio (BCR) methodology and agency estimates reveal a conservatively calculated positive NPV of USD 234 per beneficiary, compared to a GCF cost of USD 209 per beneficiary, leading to a GCF cost-benefit ratio of 1:1.12. The beneficiary


---
**Page 216**
---

NYF is lower than the overall investment per beneficiary, but this analysis does not account for other benefits, including the substantial climate mitigation impacts through carbon sequestration by soil enhancement and dietary change in cattle. Calculated over the whole project cycle, the NPV of the project is USD 120 million, which represents a net present value of the avoided CO₂ emissions, which is calculated at USD 271 million, based on the project discount rate and the carbon price of USD 5 per tonne of CO₂. The NPV of the project exceeds the investment cost, and will achieve a ratio of 1:3.26 of project investment to NPV.

On the face of it, the high allocation of funding to staff and the ostensible replication of staff roles in different countries is not justified (see map). It should be noted that the project is covering immense areas (see map). It is inevitable that, given this geographical challenge, the cost of travel and accommodation will be the primary cost factor. This addresses the initial concerns of the independent TAP regarding the budget focus on expenditure for training and salaries, rather than equipment.

In view of the above considerations, the effectiveness and efficiency rating of the project is medium.

### The Green Climate Fund

#### Decision 10/2014/GCF/05

**The development of a project utilizing ecosystem-based adaptation and nature-based solutions for adaptation and mitigation in a rangeland and livestock setting is welcome, as these are both underserved areas of potential climate, and in particular migration, action.**

- The initial assessment by the independent TAP considered as highest-priority items: political feasibility; the alignment with the strategy of the biodiversity conservation activities; sustainability/nutrient resource; and the need for additional work on indicators. The AE and the Secretariat have addressed these issues and provided further information to the TAP, which has substantially strengthened the proposal.

- The independent TAP therefore recommends this funding approval proposal for approval with the following conditions:

  (a) Satisfaction of the following condition prior to the second disbursement under the FAA:
    - Delivery by the Accredited Entity to the Fund of a comprehensive plan for monitoring and evaluation of the project, including indicators for measuring other resources between cattle herders and/or their Associations, and between herding communities and private sector entities.
  (b) Inclusion of the following covenants in the FAA:

    (i) In the event that a climate information and early warning system project, outside of the scope of this project, is being proposed for Botswana by another accredited entity to the GCF, which includes building capacity to collect hydro-meteorological data, the AE shall make every effort to coordinate with such efforts to coordinate, in the extent commercially reasonable and practicable, with the implementation of this project. The AE will also ensure that the design and implementation of such other GCF project, so that both projects are aligned as far as possible on the basis of their respective objectives of both projects.
    - The costs of such coordination are eligible expenditures under component 1 of the Funded Action.

- The assessment of the results of the mid-term independent evaluation to be submitted to the Fund, an assessment of the impact on the Funded Activity’s objectives and other relevant matters.

- Regulatory and market developments in the meat industry of the Host Country with a particular focus on the role of Botswana Meat Commission;

- Behavioural changes based on the value chain activities, in particular changes to transport costs and time for livestock products from the targeted villages under component 3 of the Funded Action;

- The development of the Botswana market for private abattoir services; and

- The impact of project outputs on private sector entities and volume of sale of livestock and livestock products concluded between the targeted villages/communities and private sector entities and their cooperatives.

Inclusion of the following provisions in the FAA:

- If, based on the mid-term independent evaluation and/or relevant APBRs submitted by the Accredited Entity, the GCF determines that any adverse changes have occurred to the project’s outputs or results with respect to Outputs 3.1 and 2 as less than seventy percent (70%) of the overall targets under all mid-term indicators, then such change is considered a Major Change in approach and is not justified or addressed in a manner acceptable to the GCF; this may be deemed to constitute a Major Change and shall be addressed in accordance with

# Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment (FP 158)

Ecosystem-Based Adaptation and Mitigation in Botswana's Communal Rangelands

### Impact potential
CI notes ITAP’s positive assessment with thanks.

CI acknowledges the lack of available meteorological data in Botswana. Through the development and deployment of the Project's Rangeland Stewardship Information Portal, cooperation with the Botswana Department of Meteorological Services (MSET), and coordination with the Ministry of Environment, Wildlife and Tourism (MEWT) for the collection, analysis, and dissemination of improved climate data in Botswana during implementation.

### Paradigm shift potential
CI recognizes ITAP's identification of externality risks to the achievement of the Project’s desired paradigm shift in livestock markets. However, the Botswana Meat Commission and other private sector actors are working towards improving market access for livestock products from communal production units. CI supports the ITAP proposal to monitor implementation progress towards the desired market paradigm shift during the Project's mid-term review.

CI also considers the Project-catalyzed transformation of national job creation investments toward restoration and climate resilient sustainable livestock production on communal lands to be a significant paradigm shift towards the deployment of public works programmes to respond to climate change impacts, a model that can be replicated in other countries in the region.

### Sustainable development potential
CI appreciates ITAP’s positive assessment, and will address the identified concern regarding land use conflict mitigation during Project implementation through extensive stakeholder engagements, coordination with Government on dual grazing rights policy, and active

participation in conflict resolution activities - in alignment with mandated authorities and local governance structures.

The Project staffing structure is repeated in each district as the three regional teams will concurrently implement all project activities in region-specific contexts, serving distinct and physically diverse geographies and populations at the high level of engagement required for Project success.
