# EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

Botswana’s agriculture sub sectors: rangeland and livestock are heavily dependent on rainfall and as a result highly vulnerable to climate variability and change. Climate model projections show increasing temperatures and more frequent climatic disturbances in the future across Africa, Botswana's second national communication to UNFCCC estimated an increase of 2°C and 3-9% decline in rainfall by 2050 across the country. These changes are likely to exacerbate agricultural vulnerability and reduce food security.

Annual rainfall is projected to decrease from its current average of about 400 mm, with livestock sector characterized by high mortality and is particularly exposed to climatic shocks. Livestock production systems in Botswana are dominated by smallholder pastoralists who rear sheep, goats, cattle and horses. The livestock industry will be reduced. This will exacerbate poverty already being experienced by livestock farmers in the country'. The negative impacts of climate change on rangelands are expected to be more severe than those on other land uses such as agriculture and urban areas in Botswana.

The high dependence of rural communities on agriculture has left many women and men farmers exposed to loss of agriculture and rangeland-based livelihoods.


In period of gender sensitive climate adaptation, resilience and mitigation strategies several climate change adaptation projects under the GCF have been implemented. The guidelines for country programmes to follow: Integration of gender in UNFCCC processes, including the recent Enhanced Lima Work Programme on Gender and its Gender Action Plan, requires that all national strategies, policies, plans and programmes related to development programmes and policies are guided by the principle of gender equality. Until recently, however, there has been no comprehensive gender analysis of climate change issues now been done through the country's 3rd National Communication as well as the preparation of the National Strategy on Climate Change. Issues highlighted in the 3rd National Communication includes: lack of gender-sensitive policies and strategies; lack of representation of women Gender Affairs, grassroots women, parliamentarians and CSOs— to enable them to participate fully in gender and climate change negotiations during the COPs and under UNFCCC processes. The GCF has therefore provided funding for a gender assessment that will be guided by the UNFCCC Gender Action Plan under the Lima Work Programme on Gender will be developed and mainstreamed into national climate', programmes and strategies[1].

In line with the Government plans, this gender assessment and Gender Action Plan (GAP) development is being undertaken as part of the GCF-funded "Community Rangelands" project has been reviewed by the Department of Gender Affairs. They have made inputs to align the GAP with national climate change priorities. This report presents the gender assessment study which was conducted in 2018 and provides a basis for developing the gender proposal development for the larger project "Ecosystem and Livelihoods Resilience: Climate Change Adaptation and Mitigation in Rangelands" (hereafter referred to as the "Community Grazing Lands") to be submitted to GCF for financial support. The report is based on a desktop literature review, stakeholder consultations (planning workshops and focus group discussions), key informant interviews, and field visits. It covers women who are married, widowed, and separated) as well as married women in three project sites, namely Ngamiland and Kgalagadi Districts, and Bobiwe sub-District. The Department of Gender Affairs as well as


---
**Page 226**
---

key policy makers working in gender and agriculture in central government were also consulted, in addition to local Chiefs, social workers, extension and veterinary officers in the project target districts.

Women and men, as well as young women and men aged 15–35 in the project sites, are being impacted differently by climate change, in part, because of gender roles. They have adapted in ways that are possible for each group depending on the resources they currently have access to. At present, there is a total of approximately 60,000 jobs in livestock production for women and men — including 500 male and female graduate students — to become Eco-rangers and Restoration Rangers. Women and men will also be trained as Community Livestock Officers for livestock herding and rangeland restoration. Furthermore, poor and vulnerable farmers will be empowered to make informed decisions on climate-resilient livestock production systems and will have access to a range of services that will help them adapt to the changing environment.

The Department of Gender Affairs ensures that women and youth needs are deliberately addressed in all aspects of the project. The gender equality and vulnerability assessment, validation workshops has equitable participation of women and youth among other vulnerable groups*. In year 1 of the project, starting with a core group of 255 individuals, equitably representative of the target population, the Gender Equality and Vulnerability Assessment (GEVA) Stewardship Agreement will be co-developed based on regional and global best practices. Stewardship Agreements are a mechanism for rangeland governance. These activities will support the development of GEVAs in all project sites to ensure that women’s voices are heard.

Gender-based violence (GBV) within the country. Further efforts to address GBV include: i) partnering with organizations such as GVI Botswana by providing training on GBV awareness and caregiving skills; ii) GBV monitoring as an element of the overall project monitoring process, and iii) sharing information about the Grievance Reporting Mechanism (GRM) via communication channels that are most likely to reach women, such that the GRM captures and responds to reports of project-related GBV.

In order to ensure that men, women and the youth in the project sites will benefit equally from the project, a gender analysis was conducted to assess implications of changing climate, particularly for women. The analysis also assessed how women and men differ with respect to access to and control of resources as well as their ability to make decisions in the household and communities within the project sites. Key findings of the gender assessment are outlined below.

### 1.1 WOMEN

In Botswana, women are more likely than men to be unemployed, with a large portion of unemployed women being young and poor.
2. Women in the project districts are more vulnerable than men to climate change as a result of their roles within the household which has focused on looking after children and other family members, while they depend on subsistence agriculture to meet the family’s needs. Relevant differences between men and women include: i) women being responsible for livestock such as sheep and headed their households, with the former showing more signs of desperation with regard to inability to afford school fees, to feed and clothe their children properly when they lose their livestock due to drought. Less poor women, although also participating in


---
**Page 227**
---

3. The vulnerabilities induced by climate change and the responsibilities regarding domestic care work — including looking after children — means that women, more than men, turn to pleegening (a form of wage labor) in order to earn money for food and other basic necessities for the family shows that women in the project sites are more severely affected by poverty. According to UNICEF in 2016 it found that 85% of pleeging households were headed by females.

4. Digging wells by hand, is an added physical burden on women. The water produced is for both domestic and livestock use. Women who have to fetch water from a well are likely to live at the cattle post or near their ploughing fields.

5. Because of their responsibilities regarding pleegening, and work from morning until noon or 1pm, they are also likely to be vulnerable to loss of livelihoods as they are unable to take better care of their livestock and crops while they are working at pleegening. Women are more likely than men to have to leave their children in the care of other family members or children, and therefore often have two jobs —pleegening and in particular keeping goats.

6. Some women are said to turn to sex work to earn income in times of climate stress when women’s livelihoods are threatened by drought or floods. In some cases of this trade, however, their desire to provide for the family outweighs the negative aspects of sex work.

7. Additional responsibilities include collecting water and firewood, particularly those who own goats as they have to collect additional water for the goats from public standpipes.

8. Almost two thirds of women have experienced GBV in their lifetimes, predominantly in the form of domestic violence. The most common form of GBV is ‘forced sex’ or rape. Another is ‘passion killings’, in which a man kills his woman partner when she threatens to leave him.

9. Many women in rural areas do not own productive land and rely on ploughing fields (when the rain falls) for their livelihoods, particularly those who are married to men who have become vulnerable as the husband’s income from cattle herding starts to decline.

10. Women in general are responsible for grocery shopping and this is done with money they earn from pleegening or other remittances they receive from relatives. In ideal situations, married women can also contribute to household income by selling livestock products but have become vulnerable as the husband’s income from cattle herding starts to decline.

11. Women are generally responsible for goat care and production. The project will assist women in the project districts with the signing of the Livestock Agreements as well as to benefit from potential livestock income generating projects.


### 2 MEN

1. As men in the project districts rely a great deal on cattle production as a means of generating income, they are exposed to loss of large numbers of cattle, particularly in the last three years due to reduced communal grazing land and insufficient water for the livestock.

2. Spending time away from home also means that men have less time with their families which can potentially lead to conflict in the home. It also leaves the wives with the responsibilities to generate income through pleegening, for instance, to support the family.


7


---
**Page 228**
---

3. Male farmers, who have primarily responsibility for cattle are greatly affected by degraded grazing land and some migrate their cattle to better pastures during drought. This often results in more land degradation and increased possibility of land conflict with communities.
4. The invasion of wildlife has also led to increased workload for some male farmers as they have had to move livestock further away from the village to avoid conflicts with wildlife.
5. The ability to relocate livestock to better pastures demonstrates the mobility and security of men. Usually, women cannot relocate their goats, cattle nor their children.
6. Male farmers are more likely than women to own livestock (both cattle and small stock), and there have been reported instances of husbands giving their wife's water to livestock, which demonstrates the extent to which livestock ownership is a male domain in this community. Women are less likely to participate in the communal grazing with a reliable water supply for their livestock. Most women and men farmers with livestock (both cattle and small stock) said they would agree to collaborate on land use if it meant that they had access to water for the livestock, i.e., both cattle and small stock (goats).
7. Compared to women, men tend to have more opportunities and enabling circumstances to start or expand income-generating activities, rather than having to rely on social protection or public employment schemes like lepaleging.
8. As men are traditionally the breadwinners in their families, the negative impacts of climate change could be affecting their mental health and leading to increased alcoholism. This can result in a loss of income for women who have been earning from selling livestock products they have earned from lepaleging.

Livestock Ownership

In 2017 women in Central Bodobongi, of which Boborwa is a sub-district, owned 35% of the cattle and men 65%, while women owned 25% and men 75% of cattle in Ngamidale. In the Kigalagadi district, women owned 19% of cattle while men owned 81%. Regarding goats, women owned 40% of the goats compared to men 60% in Central Bodobongi, while women owned 44% of all goats and men 52% in Ngamidale and in the Kigalagadi district women own 65% of goats.

Climate Vulnerability

Data and information collected during the gender assessment shows that both men and women are struggling to adapt to the increasingly frequent droughts. They have lost some or all of their livestock (cattle and goats). Wildlife and human conflict has also increased. For example, in the past five years, 20% of households in Central Bodobongi district reported that wildlife had invaded Ngamidale as they look for water — eating and destroying crops and sometimes causing injuries and even death during the invasion. This has left many members of communities in the project sites concerned about their safety, feeling vulnerable, and in some communities herding of cattle


---
**Page 229**
---

has been stopped completely because of the human, wildlife and livestock conflict. The already inadequate grazing land is also affected by the presence of wildlife.

In addition, the increase in temperature, and heat waves have resulted in the presence of more mosquitoes which has increased malaria incidences even outside of malaria season. It is fair to say that the burden of increased malaria incidences falls on women because they are generally the main carers for the sick. The high temperatures have also led to other health impacts, such as skin rashes and heat stroke.

### Conclusion

This Gender Assessment and subsequent Gender Action Plan (GAP) follow the Guidelines outlined in the Green Climate Fund (GCF) Mainstreaming Gender in Green Climate Fund Projects and the Updated Gender Policy and Action Plan 2018-2020, as well as the Gender Policy in the Environmental Management and Agro-Ecological Land Use Social Management Framework (ESMF). Under this project, measures have been put in place (the GAP and project Logframe) to ensure that women and men benefit equally and that the burden on women and men — as a result of their gender roles — is not increased, but reduced by the project activities.

The proposed project provides a lifeline for many of the poor women in the project sites who will have the opportunity to be employed as Eco-rangers and Restoration Workers. In addition, women in the project sites will also benefit from improved access to water resources through generating activities from the main grazing/herding programme. Improved grazing resources will lead to greater food security, sustainable fuelwood and water catchment areas which can reduce the demand for firewood and other forms of energy. The provision of improved livestock quality will provide food supplies for households as well as draught power and potential for household savings. The project will also help male and female farmers who are experiencing reduced grazing land availability to improve their livelihoods and better livestock management, thus de-stocking to ensure that the available land's carrying capacity is able to support their livestock.

# INTRODUCTION

Botswana’s rangeland and livestock sub-sectors are heavily dependent on rainfall and as a result highly vulnerable to climate variability and change. Projections by the United Nations scientific body warn of more frequent extreme weather events and more frequent disasters in the future (Hässler Africa, Botswana's second national communication to UNFCCC estimated an increase of 2°C and a 3-9% decline in rainfall by 2050 across the country. These changes will have significant impacts on rangeland ecosystems, biodiversity, water availability, productivity. Botswana’s rangeland-based livestock sector is characterized by high mortality and is particularly vulnerable to climate change. The projected increase in temperature will lead to a decline, and as a result its capacity to support the livestock industry will be reduced. This will exacerbate the poverty already being experienced by male and female livestock farmers in the country, and their ability to adapt to these changes will be limited. Climate change is also threatening Botswana’s rural communities, but their sustainability is being threatened by climate change. Rangeland ecological services include provision of grazing resources, food provision, energy source for cooking, water supply, and habitat for wildlife. These are critical in supporting domestic savings. However, under climate change these ecosystems and livelihoods are under threat, with the impacts of increased temperatures and rainfall variability having inter- and intra-gendered impacts, and with differential adaptation strategies to cope with these shocks and disruptions.

Gender considerations were made by the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) at ii) Conference of Parties (COP) 20 – which adopted a two-year “Lima Work Programme” on gender equality in climate change action. The programme includes: i) climate policy; ii) adoption of the Paris Agreement during COP21 – which included gender references in its preamble and decision text; iii) implementation of the gender action plan (GAP); iv) development of a gender action plan for each country; v) integration of gender into national adaptation plans. Integration of gender in UNFCCC processes requires that countries’ National Adaptation Programmes of Action (NAPAs) also be guided by the principle of gender equality. Until recently, Botswana’s NAPA did not reflect this principle and therefore the integration of gender was not achieved. This was demonstrated in the recent development of the National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS). The Ministry of Environment has contributed to the project by providing staff to ensure gender is integrated in this strategy.

As part of its NAPA, the country has since developed a Climate Change Adaptation Strategy that outlines gender as one of the six cross cutting issues. The Strategy emphasizes that “women represent a slightly more vulnerable segment of the population,” and a perspective that accords special attention to women’s needs and rights is required for effective climate change response in Botswana” will be adopted. This is being achieved through mainstreaming of gender considerations into all aspects of the NAPA, including the development of a gender-sensitive – particularly in recognition of the vulnerability of women, children and people living with disability to climate change. Relevant to the proposed Ecosystems and Rangelands Project are several interventions and policy goals outlined in the Climate Change Strategy, to be


---
**Page 231**
---

implemented starting in 2020, namely: i) promotion of equitable participation of women farmers and female-headed households in Climate Smart Agriculture (CSA) programmes; ii) empowering communities — especially women and youth — to actively participate in the implementation of climate change response measures in both rural and urban areas; iii) including women's voices in natural resource management (NRM) processes, particularly in the context of community-based natural resource management (CBNRM) processes; and iv) ensuring that gendered differences of climate change are mainstreamed into climate change education. Integrating gender and climate change considerations is critical to ensure that women farmers and female-headed households in disaster management public gatherings, to address both the higher vulnerability of women and girls as well as their greater resilience. Strategies that are targeted at increasing resilience of most vulnerable groups such as women, children and people living with disability, should be prioritized through provision of means of implementation such as technologies, finance and capacity building.

The negative impact of climate change on the rangelands is experienced differently by women, men, elderly people, and the youth, particularly in already water-stressed areas. The high dependency on natural resources for livelihoods makes women more vulnerable to climate change than male and female farmers to loss of agriculture and rangelands-based livelihoods. For this reason, it is important to integrate gender in sustainable development programmes — including those that address climate change adaptation and mitigation strategies. Women's social and economic power constrain many people’s ability to take action on climate change, with different constraints and impacts on women and men. Gender equality actions can help reduce poverty and increase resilience to cope and prepare climate risks at the national, regional and community levels.

This study was undertaken to enable preparation of a gender assessment report to support the proposal development for the larger project "Ecosystem and Livelihoods Resilience: Climate Change Risk Reduction through Ecosystem-based Adaptation in Botswana's Communal Grazing Areas". The study involved desk review of existing literature, key informant interviews, literature review and stakeholder consultations — including workshops, key informant interviews, and questionnaire surveys. The study was conducted with 102 respondents from 35 households who did not identify themselves as heads of their households. The Department of Gender Affairs, as well as key policy makers working in gender and agriculture in central government, were also consulted.

The selected project areas fall into three administrative districts, including Ngamiland, Kgalagadi, and Central, as well as five sub-districts, including Ngamiland East; Ngamiland West; Kgalagadi North; Kgalagadi South; and Bobine (see Figure 1 below). The project areas were selected in 2014 by the Department of Environment and Tourism (DoET) as being among those with the highest proportion of rural poor dealing with severe impacts of drought. The climate vulnerability across the project areas and target communities within an area varies based on exposure, adaptive capacity, sensitivity, and resilience to climate change. The most vulnerable are those in terms of length of dry season and increasing temperature are greatest in Ngamiland where the largest target population is located and therefore is a climate risk reduction priority for the project*.

Women men and youth in the project sites, because of their gender roles are being impacted differently by climate change, have adapted in ways that are possible for each group depending on the resources they currently have access to.

In year 1 of the project, starting with a core group of 225 individuals, equitably representing women, men and indigenous peoples, the purpose and process for establishing a Stewardship Agreement will be co-developed based on regional and global best practices. Stewardship Agreements will be signed by all stakeholders at the end of year 1.

The project aims to create 6,000 jobs for unemployed youth to become Ecorangers and Restoration Workers. The project will also provide training in sustainable livestock production systems such as herding and rangeland restoration. Furthermore, poor and vulnerable farmers will be empowered to make informed decisions on climate-resilient livestock production systems and will have increased access to markets through the development of value chains. The project activities will include enterprise development and other income generating opportunities from sustainable livestock value chains. The project will partner with the Department of Gender Affairs to ensure that the needs of women and youth (particularly women) are deliberately addressed and the composition of the training workshops, technical experts and national validation workshops has an equitable number of women and youth, as well as other vulnerable groups.

# Figure 1: Project districts

To gather information to support the larger project, the three districts in Botswana namely Ngamiland, Kgalagadi and Botilesa pre-selected during development of the project Concept Note in 2016, were visited to conduct stakeholder consultations.

## 2.1 METHODOLOGY

Several methodologies were used for this study. These were:

- A desk review of relevant documents:
    - The National Gender and Development Policy 2015 and a related National Survey to Establish Baseline Data on Women’s Empowerment that was produced prior to the policy.
    - Revised Botswana Land Policy and Tribal Land Act, 2016.
    - Reports of Government poverty reduction and agriculture programmes such as the Final Report of the Livestock Management and Infrastructure Development (LMID) Programme Phase II Evaluation of the Livestock Management and Infrastructure Development Support Scheme in Seven Districts of Botswana, 2015. The report was based on a survey conducted with three focuses on resource-poor households (i.e., small stock, guinea fowl and poultry), which were selected for their potential to contribute towards improved productivity of cattle, small stock and Tswana chickens, among others.
- Key informant interviews, for example, policymakers, local authority representatives and agricultural extension officers.

- One stakeholder engagement plan consultation workshop in each project target district, followed by kgotla* meetings in Bobwoswa (Setibe Pheliso and Bobonong), Ngamiland (Maun and Gaborone), Kgalagadi (Kgatelopele and Tshwekedi) and Botilesa. The kgotlas were facilitated by a gender expert. Members of the communities were first briefed on the proposed Ecosystem and Livelihood Resilience in Botswana’s Communal Rangelands project during a kgotla meeting. This was followed by a discussion on the project objectives, activities and target beneficiaries. In most cases at the kgotlas meetings did not say much, i.e., not many women spoke in this setting.

- Interviews with women and men household heads using questionnaires developed through the kgotlas. The questionnaire was administered to 100 households in each of the four settlements Kgasabanganye and Spionkile (Ngamiland), Lepokole, Tshwekedi, Gobogano (Kgalagadi) and Hwanehuke, Tshabe and Zutswha. Thirty-two FHMs, forty-seven MHMs and ten


---
**Page 234**
---

married women living in Mithils were interviewed, bringing the total of those interviewed to eighty six. Though the number of married women interviewed was very small, all but one said they were not heads of their households, which supports the notion that married women have been socialised to view and accept the husband as the head. Of these, 27 were single females, 10 were widowed, 35 were separated or divorced, and 8 were married. The rest were either widowed, separated or divorced. The women and men were invited for the interviews immediately after the kick-off meetings. This was not a technical survey but to set the agenda as a basis of what is to be looked at in the gender analysis of the project at the village level.

The gender assessment and subsequent gender action plan follow the Guidelines outlined in the Green Climate Fund (GCF) Mainstreaming Gender in Green Climate Fund Projects and the Updated GCF Gender Policy. The guidelines are based on the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) Gender Policy in their latest (November 2020) Environmental and Social Management Framework. The purpose of gender-responsive planning is to ensure that women’s voices and perspectives are taken into account, as well as to make sure that projects are gender-sensitive as emphasised by the Updated Gender Policy and Action Plan 2018-2020. According to the Policy, gender-responsive means that instead of only identifying and addressing gender inequalities in project design, it also seeks to identify and address historical gender biases. Measures have been put in place by this project (see the Gender Action Plan (GAP) and project Logical Framework) to ensure that both women and men benefit equally from the project activities and that no one is worse off as a result of their gender roles not increased but reduced by the project activities.

## 3.2 GENDER EQUALITY

According to the Human Development Report (HDR) 2017, Botswana is ranked number 84 out of 189 countries within the Gender Inequality Index. In 2017, the country had a 56.3% share of seats held by women in Parliament®. The status quo has remained as much fewer women than men won parliamentary seats during the October 2019 elections. This translates into 6 female and 23 male members of parliament. There are currently only two female ministers out of a total of 25 members of parliament i.e. those that did not stand for elections. This is a stark inequality between women and men, and likely leads to inadequate representation of women’s issues within the government.

The National Policy on Gender and Development (NPGAD), under which the proposed GFC Botswana project falls, and the Minister of Environment and Natural Resources are both female.

The Government of Botswana acknowledges unique partnerships, benefits and contribution between women and men in the development of its society and economy and has put in place several measures to empower women. In 2015, the Botswana Government endorsed the National Policy on Gender and Development (NPGAD). The policy mandates all five Ministries to mainstream gender equality into their respective policies and programmes. NPGAD also calls for an enabling environment, adoption of affirmative measures and capacity building on the integration of gender in all sectors of sustainable development.

NPGAD calls for the elimination of discrimination in order to achieve gender equality and equity and to ensure that both women and men benefit equally from development programmes implemented by the government. Programmes are required to undertake gender analysis in order to identify gender gaps, formulate strategies to address these gaps using relevant indicators, and with the required budgets as well as monitoring and evaluation®. NPGAD has a fully endorsed institutional arrangement which includes district gender committees, as well as civil society for the promotion of gender equality and women’s empowerment.

The Department of Gender Affairs confirms that many of the plans in the operational strategy developed for the policy have not been achieved. However, it is important to note that, through this Department and the Gender Policy, gender has been integrated in national climate change

## Policies, Agriculture Land Rights, and National Policy on Land (which highlights the need to protect women land rights)

Gender analysis and other gender equality centred planning are being promoted to inform gender responsive policies based policies[1] as shown by reporting on gender equality achievements and progress in the National Development Plan Review, and at district planning levels. During the scoping mission, two gender officers were also observing implementing a Department of Gender Affairs programme in Botswana’s Northern District. Nongovernmental meetings on government eradication programmes. A gender officer from the Department of Gender Affairs, Bobirwa sub-district, was also present to provide input and guidance for the implementation of the project discussions. Other good practices regarding implementation of the NGPAGD can be found through the Ministry of Agriculture and Food Security which has developed a draft gender strategy with the assistance of the UNDP.

Community Based Natural Resource Management (CBNRM) is another Government programme that offers limited employment for women and men in smaller rural villages. CBNRM is implemented through community-based organisations (CBOs) and most high-revenue CBOs are found in Ngwane District. The project has created opportunities for women to participate in opportunities for ecotourism[2]. A CBNRM project (caves) in Lepokwe, Bobirwa sub-District, is managed by the VOC and Botswana Tourism Organisation. The Lepokwe Chief confirmed that the caves are visited regularly (by small numbers), and offer employment to about three women and one man from the village.

### Legal Status of Women

Botswana’s constitution states that “every person in Botswana is entitled to the fundamental rights and freedoms of the individual, that is to say, the right, whatever his or her race, place of origin, political opinions, colour, creed or sex[3]. This means that all people, female, male, and others, are considered equal before the law. Botswana has a dual legal system which are common law (which applies in urban areas) and customary law, which applies to law in the rural areas, is enforced by tribal structures and customary courts.

Marriage in Botswana can be governed by both customary law (negotiations, agreements and witnessing of the marriage between the spouses by their families) and common law through the Marriage Act. The Marriage Act states that “no marriage shall be valid unless it is concluded so that the spouses concluded a valid Customary, or other religious marriage, and any other particulars prescribed. Chiefs and Headmen of record shall be ex officio district registrars of customary marriages[4].” This means that all marriages in Botswana are registered whether written or unwritten, in force in Botswana, other than customary law. Customary law means, in relation to any particular tribe or tribal community, the customary law of that tribe or community so far as it is not incompatible with the provisions of any written law or contrary to


---
**Page 239**
---

morality, humanity or natural justice. In cases and proceedings arising from inheritance, the appropriate customary law shall, subject to some provisions, be the customary law applying to the deceased[2].

Both customary and common laws that govern can be interpreted differently by different chiefs and also by those who are married. Discussions with two chiefs in Bobiries and Kpogbadi showed that they have different views on the issue of marriage. The chief in Bobiries explained that for instance, the negotiations between the young woman and young man's parents fall within the Customary Law while moving on to marry and sign the register at the District Office is governed by the Marriage Act. In cases where a dispute arises over property governed by the Marriage Act, Common Law takes precedence over Customary Law in the case that the High Court has jurisdiction. However, if the dispute involves customary law only, then the customary law is requested by the High Court to implement the divorce settlement. Customary court can therefore assist in dividing the property.

The Constitutional Amendment Act of 2004 abolished the marital power over the person and property of his wife, and the marital power which a husband had over the person and property of his wife were both abolished in 2004. The effect of the abolition of marital power was to remove the legal basis for the common law position that a married woman is subject to her husband's authority. This also means that the Act abolishes the common law position of the husband as head of the family. Acts requiring consent of spouse that are relevant to the proposed project are “a spouse married in community of property shall not be required to give consent for any transaction involving the other spouse’s property, livestock, borehole...” The Abolition of Marital Power Act (2004) specifies that the wife can enjoy the same rights as her husband and can make decisions on her own without seeking his decisions. However, this Act does not apply to customary or religious marriages, women married under customary law are considered legal minors, and require the permission of their spouse for household decisions and other transactions.

The Miscellaneous Amendments Act of 2004 ensured that all relevant laws were aligned to the Commission on the Elimination of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) principles, following the adoption of the Abolition of Marital Power Act (2004). The Affidavits and Proceeding Act was adopted in 2013. This Act provides for the taking of an affidavit by a child or other economic income, in order to ease the burden of care on the parent with custody of the child. A Gender and Development Strategy was developed in 2012 to promote equitable distribution of services, resources and opportunities between men and women. Further, Botswana has ratified the SAICG Protocol on Gender and Development.

Notwithstanding the amendments and gender positive legislation above, the Administration of Estate Act—which allows the customary laws to govern inheritance of the estate—has been shown to be a major obstacle in the administration of estates in Botswana. The law does not govern inheritance of an estate. For instance, in the Ngamiland district, it is the paternal uncles within the Tawana and Hambushubu communities that decide whether the estate is divided if the husband dies and leaves behind wife(s). If the wife and/or children happen to remain alive, the estate is not shared because it is assumed that the head of the family is alive and will divide on how


---
**Page 240**
---

The inheritance will be shared[1]. The national policy on land notes that although land policies and laws do not undermine widows’ and orphans’ rights, they can sometimes be dispensed of land belonging to their deceased husbands or parents, by the late husband’s relatives and guardians. This happens as they may not have access to administrative and legal channels to protect their interests.

The Abolition of Marital Power Act (2004) provides women with the same right as men to administer the joint estate when married in community of property. The Act repealed common law practices of patriarchal marital power, and gave men and women equal rights within marriage and guardianship. It also provided for the registration of marriages and the division of property. However, prior to marriage registration spouses are able to indicate that they wish to marry under the old law. This means that a woman can be married in community of property and still retain her marital power over her own property.

The devolution of property on death, which puts women at a disadvantage upon the death of their spouses[2]. Most of the respondents (80%) of the household interviews said they were head of the household. The survey found that women are more likely to be heads of households than men and women. Most of the women surveyed who are married said their husbands were the head of the household.

Figure 4 below shows that many of the gender assessment survey respondents (80%) were heads of the household, while only a small number of single headed households where the larger project is likely to benefit. The consultation found that this group (widows and widowers/heads/parents) tend to, by necessity, be more autonomous and independent but lack the customary social protections of a husband or male partner (and their accompanying livelihoods).

Examples of enforced marital power that were discussed in consultations include women having to request permission from their husbands to wear western/traditional dress; that is built on switch to western/urban lifestyle; and, as it goes for the men who get permission. In other areas the women have accepted that men will never do housework, and they (the women and their daughters) have to do all the work. Other marital power dynamics are discussed below in the context of implications of climate change.

Figure 4. Marital Status of Survey Respondents.

Source: Gender Assessment

One of the chiefs interviewed views Customary Law in marriage as complex — if a husband buys the wife’s car and the car is registered in her name, it is hers and does not fall under shared property. However, if she has children with him, then the car would be considered part of their joint property and would not become part of the property to be divided between the two in the event of a divorce. This means that women who are married but do not have children may lose out on property.

Good interpretation of marriage laws, particularly by chiefs is seen as a way through which women’s property rights can be protected, in the event of divorce or death of the husband. It must be noted however, that this is not always the case and it is important to ask women about their names especially when asked if their names were also on the title deed together with their husband's name.

As the project will be implemented in rural areas where older women are likely to be married under the customary marriage system, this means that women who are not married and who own livestock are likely to have better control and decision-making powers compared to those that are married. It is therefore important for the project to ensure that women who are married reach both married and unmarried women, and that different strategies must be used to ensure that they benefit equally — particularly in relation to decision making over the cattle that will be integrated in the grazing agreements.

### 3.3 DIVISION OF LABOUR

Although the Abolition of the Marital Power Act (2004) repealed common law practices of patriarchal marital power — and gave men and women equal rights within marriage and guardianship over their children— this has not translated into sharing of responsibilities at the household level, as women continue to take the lead in caring for children, elderly and the home. It must be noted however, that the younger generation of young men are regarded to have


---
**Page 242**
---

somewhat transcended this gender stereotype as they assist with household chores previously known to be the role of women and girls.

Division of labour in the home is skewed towards women. Additionally, customary law applied in marriage means that most land intended project women beneficiaries in rural areas are subordinate to men, and therefore have to do most of the housework. Discussions with women and men in the project areas indicate that both women and men get up around the same time. It is an area where women and men share responsibilities for household chores and childcare depending on the area, e.g. dealing with livestock as well as wildlife that is invading communities in Bobirwa (Kabwata) and Kasese Districts. Women are responsible for preparing breakfast and lunch, taking care of the family, which includes preparing the children for school while the men get ready to travel to the cattle post and stay there during the day to take care of the livestock. Daily weekly chores include fetching water from a well or river, cooking food, cleaning the house, washing clothes, sweeping the compound, and feeding animals. Women also have to do some weeding and ploughing and have to be at work from 6am to 1pm. Women that are not married or do not have male partners, often travel to the cattle post after (pleigeng) to water their livestock, and later krali.

There are distinct differences in terms of gender roles between Batebwe (Kagamba) and other tribes in project sites. Batebwe women appear to have more work to do, particularly related to added gender responsibilities which include milk collection and processing milk and preparing wood in the evening. Additional milking can also be done in the evenings and girls are taught as early as 9 years old how to collect water from a well or river, cook food, clean the house, sweep the compound, and feed animals. Women have to do some weeding and ploughing and have to bring them back in as herding is no longer done. The daily clock below, as well as discussions with women and men in the project districts show that men have leisure times particularly in the evenings, while women perform a greater proportion of unpaid domestic work in the afternoons and evenings.

Although the marital power abolition was a historic moment, as well as a welcome development by the women of Botswana, it remains an ineffectively enforced law in relation to division of labor in households. There is no legal or social reason that compels men to assist their wives or partners with household chores and child-rearing duties, especially in rural areas. Notwithstanding, a good example can be found in Gobogab village where men are said to assist women by assisting with ploughing, preparing ploughing fields, mending fences damaged by elephants, herding the herd of cattle from elephants and predators, collecting water and grass for livestock, and herding to keep cattle away from crops. It is also noted that elephants sometimes fill with salt as they search for water. All these chores help reduce the burden on wives or partners, as well as protect the family's livelihoods, i.e. cattle and crops. Men in areas where women have added responsibilities are said to assist their wives by guarding livestock and crops, which is sometimes done at night.

During the gender assessment stakeholder engagement, it was observed that there were mostly women in the project communities, particularly at the tribal meeting places, the kgotla. Many are said to not attend meetings because they are busy with household chores. In some of the districts stated that the reason many men do not attend kgotlas is that they are out drinking and when asked they say they are busy doing men's work. Some abuse alcohol and have lost sense of responsibility, especially in areas where women have added responsibilities.

The project will contribute to equal division of labor in households in the projects districts where women have added responsibilities. Examples include relieving the burden through day care for small children and provision of water for livestock, especially goats which many women own and have to collect water from far away. The project also allows many young women who are interested in joining the Econetizens training programme. Some say they are single mothers with


---
**Page 244**
---

no relatives to assist with taking care of their small children and would need assistance to travel to and stay in the capital city with their small children to attend the Eorangcrers course. Young men whose sole responsibility is to look after the family livestock, or that are those that are currently employed at the cattle posts, will also be supported to attend the Eorangcrers courses while the livestock they look after is being taken care of.

### 3.4 Accession and Control Over Economic, Financial, Physical, Natural and Other Assets

#### 3.4.1 Poverty and Employment

The urban villages recorded a decrease in poverty incidence between the two periods from 19.9 to 15.4%, while in rural areas the poverty incidence marginally decreased from 24.3 to 20.4% between the two periods. The highest poverty levels were recorded in Botswana South, where the datum line dropped from 19.6 to 15%. Kgatlago South had the highest poverty levels at almost 40%. Statistics also show that poverty in Botswana is most prevalent among FHHs compared to their male counterparts. The poverty incidence for female headed households was 32% and 28%, respectively[7]. Poverty in FHHS is compounded by the low level of education, increase in division of labour with regard to taking care of children, taking care of the children's grandparents, and sometimes even the husband who are also often unemployed. Poverty is also increased by loss of livelihoods in the form of crops and livestock.

In Botswana, women are more likely than men to be unemployed, with a large portion of unemployed women being young and poor. In 2016, the national unemployment rate was estimated at 5%, but this figure varied significantly across rural and urban areas. Of the total employed, 5% employed were from poor households and of those from poor households, 37% were female[8]. The proportion of unemployed females in rural areas was 25% and out of the estimated total unemployed youth, 15% were from poor households. Males constituted 43% of the poor youth while only 57% were females. Predominantly, the unemployed poor youth are in rural areas[9] (p. 24).

A total estimated number of persons out of labour force population during the period 2015-16 was 38%, which is 14% less than the national average. For both sexes, participation rate in economic activity was high for young adults. Participation rates for females were higher than their male counterparts.

In rural areas, women dominate informal business and are more likely to be involved in informal business[9]. Women dominate the informal economy, and they form the majority of informal market players that are vending and hawking among other forms. Women also form the majority of subsistence farmers who grow crops for their own consumption or sale. However, it is not clear but women of the Basware ethnic group dominate wild fruit product gathering including that of wild fruits[10].

A study undertaken by UNICEF and the Government of Botswana in 2012 showed that overall, the ilegenging households are more likely to be headed by women than by men (n=36/20). This high ratio may suggest that ilegenging is a good safety net for vulnerable female headed households. The majority of the women who applied for assistance, and received financial assistance, are mostly from women applicants. The UNICEF report indicates that in ilegenging households, the heads were characterized by low educational attainments, with the majority having no formal education at all. This is a positive environment for economic growth in the rural economy and the ever-increasing numbers of young people who are not in employment, education or training (NEETs) outside the programme, at the expense of other economic activities. For example, arable agriculture and livestock farming seem to have suffered from this effect most. This was confirmed by some women interviewed during the gender assessment, who said that they had certainly neglected their livestock as they have to work half day, five days a week in ilegenging.

Some of the women and men that participated in the gender assessment did not have formal employment and relied heavily on government poverty eradication programmes, especially ilegenging. The majority of the women who applied for assistance were unemployed or are also unemployed. Few women were observed selling snacks around the village and at the kgotla meetings. In Kagalabandu, some women weave baskets that they say have no or limited market – they sell them to their friends and family members only. The weaving standards are said to be extremely high and as a result, buy very few baskets from the women at a time. Some women say many of the baskets they have produced are piling up in their homes, with buyer’s right not in the Kgotala. In Zuluhanze, Basarwa women make crafts that they also have few buyers.

Women consulted described how young women are more likely to be single parents and unemployed, even when they have completed secondary educations. During consultations, it was noted that young women who have entered sub-secondary or secondary schooling, but are now not able to because of drought and loss of natural resources, and have limited opportunities for employment in the village. Some young women said that they would like to move or travel to larger villages, towns or even Gabarone to look for work (marketing) since there is little economy in rural areas. During secondary school (when many are boarders, receiving three meals a day), some of these young women have to leave home and walk long distances to school. This is not sustainable or attainable on an ilegenging or subsistence agriculture income, with this relative deprivation leading them to become frustrated and disheartened. Many of these young women who are unable to complete their education would like to work in the formal sector, and would rather be in higher earning tertiary service roles (although these are in short supply).

### 3.4.2 Access to Financial Resources

The gender survey conducted in 2014 by the Department of Gender Affairs found that more men access financial services than women (MDS) loans for cattle farming, mortgages and standard loans while women mostly requested loans for meeting the family expenses such as school fees. During the gender assessment, in the project districts, men said they were dependent on livestock, and have nothing to sell when they lose cattle to drought. The participants mentioned


---
**Page 246**
---

As indicated earlier, many women survive through income generated from working in ilegengend. The women have control of these funds, BWP 567 which is equivalent to ~USD 50 and spend almost all of it on food for their families. However, the amount of money that they earn from this is not enough and it is sometimes not paid on time. Women say they take groceries on credit from the same shop where they buy school fees and other items. They are able to pay off a month's credit only to take the next groceries again on credit from the same shop. In addition, women say they are generally responsible for paying children’s school fees (with family income) and many times they have to borrow money from the same shop where they buy groceries. Under this scheme, ilegengend earners are not exempt from paying school fees, but their earnings are too high to cover these costs. The fact that women are unable to pay off their debts means that their children’s school certificates are held by school administration until the debt has been settled. This can create disruptions to schooling, which will have intergenerational implications for girls and boys alike.

The women and men farmers complained about the low prices they receive from abattoirs and millers (unfavourable price per kg) for the cattle they sell. They feel because of their desperation they are taken advantage of by cattle buyers. Women commented that when they sell their cattle, they are forced to accept whatever price is offered as there is no other option but slaughter men as payment, depriving the women’s children of much needed protein. Some farmers suggested that their cattle should be given powers to set the price per kg on behalf the community and not the abattoirs or millers who exploit them.

Since men are likely to have better paying jobs than women, and as such, are more likely to have better collateral to secure loans compared to women. Men also have more access and control to the productive resources such as land which they can use as security for the loans at the credit facilities. Women say that they are not able to get loans from any of these credit facilities because money is controlled by the men though some say they give the money to the wife to distribute among the family’s needs. The men said that because of frequent drought, their cattle related economic activities have been disrupted leading to loss of livelihood for immediate and extended families.

Both women and men are vulnerable to climate change and there is potential for conflict in the area as the father may choose to purchase supplementary feed for the livestock, while the mother would need more food for her family. The proposed mobile abattoir will help reduce this problem through better management and feeding of livestock that is kept in collective communal areas. The proposed mobile abattoir will also bring down costs — additional costs of transportation of the cattle to the abattoirs by millers/abattoirs. Transport costs are deducted from the final price paid to the farmer.


---
**Page 247**
---

As women and men who reside in rural areas generally do not have regular sources of income, they also do not have bank accounts or utilize mobile money services such as Orange Money, My Zaka or Piso Money. 62 out of 86 respondents said they do not have bank accounts as they do not have a steady source of income. The majority of the savings groups in Botswana are these highly populated by women compared to men. Savings generated through these groups will go to household expenses, Women and men consulted mentioned that these groups were primarily started for financial security and to help with administration, and since they still benefit from their wives/partners participation.


### 3.4.3 Access to Land

There are three types of land tenure in Botswana: tribal, freehold and state land — and any person over the age of 18 years can apply for land. The Deeds Registry Act, Married Persons Property Act, makes it possible for women to register deeds in their own name, whether married or unmarried. If married, women are required to disclose whether they are married in or out of community property. Women who are married in community property have no right over their husband in executing any deed, and that immovable property bequeathed or donated to a woman married in community of property shall not form part of the joint estate if it is specified as a condition on the will.


A gender study conducted by FAO found that one of the major problems in tenure rights and inheritance laws in Botswana was that women appeared to be unequally treated under the law. Under customary law, the homestead and land around it could be inherited only by the son. The majority of women who are married out of community property have no right over their husband (either the husband or older son). Other constraints factors to women owning land in Botswana are that they are too old; those under 18 years have no rights at all, and as a result of all property transactions, women may not always know unless they explicitly marry out of community, and most women lack the assets, capital or labour to use the land productively. Women married in community of property are permitted to own immovable property in their own names; however, their husbands still retain considerable control over jointly held assets of the marriage.


A marriage solemnised under the Marriage Act is governed by civil law only so far as the marriage is concerned and not by customary law. The Married Persons Property Act provides for a choice between customary law for persons who are subject to customary law, unless they express exemption from such. The Property Bill of Married Persons provides that the property of spouses who are married under the Marriage Act, but are subject to customary law, will be held, disposed of, or devolve according to customary law unless the spouse has exempted the property from this application[6].

This chief has assisted some widows in his village as he would have been familiar with the widow's life situation.**

## 3.4.3.1 Arable Land

A study conducted by the Heinrich Böll Foundation in Cape Town found that climate variability has had an impact on arable farming and this has a negative impact on women as this is predominantly a female activity. In contrast, climate variability is not likely to affect the hunter gatherer life style of the San people. The study concludes that with reduced natural resource-based livelihood options, more people will be looking for alternative livelihoods (e.g., wage employment or informal work/wealth). Many of the women participants in this gender assessment stated that they are unable to produce as many crops as often as they used to due to dwindling and unreliable rainfall.


---
**Page 249**
---

In Botswana, many women in rural areas depend on subsistence agriculture for the survival of their families. The 2010 Ministry of Agriculture (MoA) assessment of the Integrated Support Programme for Arable Agriculture Development (ISPAD) showed that more females (57.6%) were involved in farming than males (42.4%). However, women are less likely to own land or machinery ownership among women and men farmers. Women said they owned the ploughing field while some men owned productive resources such as cattle post (morokwa) or borehole.

Some women in the project districts say though they have ploughing fields they cannot afford, for instance, a tractor to till their fields. In Eha 13 (Ngamiland), women said that they own land at their disposal yet are still harvesting less than men as they are hindered by lack of inputs to maximise utilisation of all the land that they have*. In Eha 13 (Ngamiland), especially in female headed households, women say they cannot afford to buy fertiliser and seeds. The costs for the diesel for the tractor and weeding. In some cases, single women or those from poor households say they cannot afford the required contribution to release government agriculture programmes such as the National Livestock Development Programme (NLDP) which provides subsistence farmers in the form of free seeds and fertiliser and payment to a tractor owner after germination.

### 3.4.3.2 Community Grazing Land

Local authorities in Eha Tsietsetsebo (Bobirwa), said that there is not enough grazing as the villages and settlements around are slowly converging, reducing the communal grazing areas even further. In some cases, women say they have no access to water points for their livestock.

Tsetsewele farmers are that masimo (ploughing fields) are better managed as they are properly allocated by the Land Board. On the other hand, merika (cattle posts) are mafumelo (self-allocated) and women say they have no access to them. In some cases, women say that grazing land community members oppose as there is no law that prevents him to do so.

In Ngamiland in Nkazara, buffalo and buffer zone fences more or less surround the village. Sentiments from the kgotla meeting discussions especially from men were that there is not enough water for livestock and crops. The women say that they have no access to water points as they are losing in another way as well; wildlife, for example, buffalo, hippo, elephant, crocodile, etc which were a source of bounty in the area, are now dying in the dry soil river bed. They no longer plough at night anymore because elephants eat their crops and destroy them. The women say that 100 (USD) per ha for pigs or crops or fencing damaged by the elephants. There is no repeat compensation as the elephant population has increased. In some cases, the elephants will break through their farm fences and eat their crops. The project will address the latter challenge through the introduction of managing livestock away from croplands, thereby reducing this vulnerability contributor.

Members of communities in the three districts feel powerless, squeezed by numerous boreholes, buffer zones and buffalo-fence lines. Ngamiland is a region which experiences outbreaks of Foot and Mouth Disease (FMD), and the two fences serve a purpose to control this disease. One


---
**Page 250**
---

male member feels the fence has encroached their area and should be moved towards the wildlife area. The men also believe that recent plans to drill boreholes in the area will encourage wildlife to drink where in the communities, which will lead to more injuries and death. In Kgalagadi District, members of the communities say it is difficult to form effective syndicates for boreholes as available land has greatly reduced by the Ghanzi border and game reserves.

### 3.4.1 Livestock Ownership

In the case of livestock, the Livestock Management and Infrastructure Development (LIMID) is one of the MA’s women’s economic empowerment programmes that assist farmers to purchase livestock inputs such as feed, vaccines and grazing land products. In 2016, LIMID accounted for 78% of beneficiaries of LIMID. Men who applied at the time for LIMID had chosen boreholes and related equipment as their priority. The majority of women applicants were many women cannot afford especially in case of boreholes and related equipment. In Botswana men are likely to be in a better financial position than women hence they can afford the 50% required by the programme. Women who applied for LIMID had chosen livestock inputs and feed.

Cattle ownership is dominated by men. The majority of the western cattle do so through marriage[26]. In addition to owning more goats than men, women are also more likely to have participated in LIMD[2] in larger[3]. The larger project will ensure that female headed households fully participate in the project. Women who own livestock face many challenges such as drought and disease. They also have lost goats due to drought and disease. The goats get sick and eat dirt and waste because of lack of vegetation. Men worry about their livestock and want to put in place measures to ensure that they are not affected by drought or disease. The results from the survey show that community who own livestock show that both women and men have lost animals as a result of drought or disease caused by the aftermath of heavy rains in 2017. From both the women and men's comments, it is clear that women face more challenges than men.

The Annual Agricultural Survey was conducted in 2017 and the above livestock numbers may have been greatly reduced, as can be deduced from the gender assessment survey which shows that many farmers have lost cattle since 2016 due to drought. Farmers in Eshas 13 alluded to the fact that they had lost their animals because of lack of water and vegetation. It is clear that if this project is implemented, it must be noted that all but one (79 out of 80 respondents[4]) of the respondents in the project districts who said they own cattle and other livestock said they have lost some or most of them due to drought. The animals are dying because there is no water for them to drink as well as lack of vegetation. Some goats have been seen to die in the drying rivers as they get stuck in mud. Much of the borehole water has become saline leading to some death of cattle.

In Bobonwe families, women who own livestock were more likely to have lost livestock during severe droughts. One drought was so bad that goats also died — notably, goats are considered very hardy animals that can weather a drought much easier than cattle[5]. In Bobonwe women say their cattle resort to eating the dry leaves and 30kma minu (they eat soil) once all grazing in the area has


---
**Page 252**
---

In Kigagdadi, when a woman gets married, she brings cattle her father would have allocated to her before he was married. The cattle are placed under the control of the husband who adds them to his existing herd. The husband is told by the wife's parents that he must not allow his wife to manage the cattle but must discuss and agree with his wife before any of the cattle is sold. This practice is done to ensure that the wife does not sell her share of the cattle without her name. Their resulting calves are also branded with the wife's brand[1]. If the wife pre-deceases the husband, she is allowed to keep a portion of the herd and her children inherit it by name. If they both die at the same time, the husband's relatives collaborate with the wife's parents/relatives to divide the cattle among the remaining children. For women from poor families, it is difficult to acquire livestock.

In Botswana when a husband marries into a marriage, he is his alone. Those accumulated during the marriage are shared with the wife and bear their collective brand[2]. This is done for all livestock. The practice is done to share out cattle amicably during divorce. If the man is guilty of adultery during the marriage, he leaves everything behind and must start afresh with the new family.

In the majority of cultures, a male child is the natural heir of the estate of his deceased father. Customary laws and traditional court appeals often result in the division of inheritance and seriously disadvantages women. As a result societal discrimination against women persists in many societies. This has serious implications for women's access to property rights and economic opportunities[3]. It must also be noted that cultural practices that apply to livestock appear to affect older women and men more as they are married under Customary Law. In Botswana, the cattle owner is usually the husband of a woman who is married whom the project must ensure that they discuss and agree with their husbands on how the wife can participate and benefit from the project activities, especially if only the livestock is to be sold. This is because women have traditionally been denied the prerogative over small stock and hence have decision making powers over this type of livestock – so this is unlikely to become an issue during project implementation.

There are concerns with women and men regarding collecting and these include: similar branding, lack of access to water for cattle for drinking, brading and even just check on the cattle. The project will address these issues by giving access to the farmers to undertake the activities that they did in the past such as milking and branding. Men and Matriarchs are able to collect water during drought as they can mobilise resources for fodder and transport water to the livestock.

Preliminary observations can be made from the gender assessment and stakeholder discussions that many women own more goats than cattle. For the women that own cattle their numbers may be small. The project will address this by providing training on how to manage these animals should establish a gender disaggregated baseline of cattle ownership to ascertain existing numbers of cattle in the project areas as stocks are likely to have been depleted particularly in the last three years. It could be the case that many women do not own cattle but goats and


---
**Page 253**
---

enterprise development opportunities for optimising value for climate adaptation from goats or alternative livelihood options should be a priority project.

In farming communities, farming associations are mostly dominated by men. Women do not make any decisions on or even respond to agricultural surveys in the absence of their male counterparts, who are the decision-makers. The expectation of agricultural extension is also that a male should answer and make decisions. The married women interviewed during the qualitative research were not allowed to participate in farming activities shared with the wife and husband. The husbands said the same. Customer messages are very difficult in communities where women do not have access to water. In the case of the Cudahy project, the property including livestock. The married respondents said that the decision to sell livestock, including cattle, was done by both of them. It is not clear whether this is a default answer, as it is known that women are more involved in livestock keeping than men. Women have access although having more control over small stock still has to inform the husband of a decision to sell. Decisions on share farming and the right to command grazing will be a key consideration in the project. The culturally-appropriate mechanisms need to be implemented to ensure equitable participation of married women in Swaziland Agreements.

### Access to Water

Land use and allocation processes have a profound impact on water resources and demand, and the way land is used to tend generally include access to surface water resources subject to existing rights. Individuals and syndicates requesting a borehole generally contribute towards the cost of sinking the borehole, and operate and maintain the borehole in exchange for free use of the water. The problem with this arrangement is that women are not given any opportunity after paying for the sinking of the borehole because they lack access to financial resources and other agricultural inputs. There is a huge disparity in ownership with women owning 27% and men owning 73%. This is due largely by findings from Swaziland Agreements projects sites that revealed that women relied on government standpipes, buying water from those who own boreholes or water sellers.

There is currently high water scarcity particularly in some parts of Swaziland such as Colombojo and surrounding areas. Boreholes and perennial rivers are drying up much faster than the respondents have seen in the past. In areas like the project sites that are water stress regions, all women use their stand pipe coupons to collect water for the household and their goats (goats drink at the compound). They have to use 20 litre containers, about five per day to collect water and take it back home. Some women collect water from boreholes on a daily basis while others request from borehole owners for goats. Furthermore, both women and men farmers in the project districts where there is water stress have dug wells that some call 'dry' or metaseidio. These are hand operated as can be seen in the picture below.

Hand-dug and operated wells in Bobwes and Kigalagadi (photograph by Nezirupa Wright)

Cattle are said to be dying in large numbers particularly in Ngamiland where farmers relied heavily on the rivers, which in their own words, are drying up much faster recently. In Seretsha, we are told that the situation was different when the river flowed annually and most farmers applied for water permits from the Department of Water Affairs. However, this year, the goats were then disbursed because the farmers said they had access to water from the river for the goats. There were no water issues or stress then. Now the lake has dried. In Etsha 13, cattle died almost immediately after being released into the area. Farmers have to spend a lot of time travel back, then travel to the west where grazing is available. Some of the cattle are said to die on the way to the grazing areas due to hunger and fatigue.

The Nxozgra chief mentioned that this is the first time in his life that he has seen such livestock as depicted above. In Kigalagadi, in Etsha 13, a gentleman said "there will be no cattle in the community to save by that time", meaning at the time of project implementation. This shows the severity of situation. In Kigalagadi there are no rivers or dams and most of the water for livestock is from wells. The government has provided boreholes but some farmers have drilled boreholes to provide water for the livestock. Some women farmers find some of the boreholes to be situated far from when they keep their livestock which means longer times are required to collect water. This situation has led to a reduction in the amount of time available for these women and grazing areas, and correspondingly, women's time availability for other activities.

Women in water stressed communities spend time and effort on water collection (for household use and for goats, for those who own goats) from the public standpipe using coupons/tags. This is common practice in many parts of Botswana where there are no boreholes or private wells. The water at the public standpipe which happened almost daily. Women have to get up early to start collecting water before it runs out and they may be up until late into the night. They collect around midday. Sometimes the coupon system malfunction, hence the queuing. FHRIs in which the women own livestock have added responsibilities of taking care of livestock. These households are more likely to be hard hit by the negative impact of climate change on subsistence farming and livestock farming. Goat production has become an income generating


---
**Page 255**
---

activity that comes with a burden for the women as they have to draw additional water for the goals. However, there has been an announcement in the past few people to remove goats from the village as watering goats from potable water sources is deemed illegal.

The public stand pipe water system is a project that was implemented by the government countrywide to cater for consumers who cannot afford standalone installation in their homes. In some villages including in the present districts some households pay for installation of standpipes in the home. The standpipe systems are installed at a distance from the village and they pump water which is loaded into the tag/coupons. Those living where there is no water reticulation are encouraged to use these standpipes as an alternative source of drinking water. The system provides water for those who cannot afford to load the tags. The water is affordable and possibly highly subsidised, for example, one woman in Ngcimland can buy her tag by BWP 1 (US$ 1), which she pays monthly. However, there are some challenges associated with this project. These include:

- Leaking of water from the pipes leading to a reduction in supply.
- The system is not reliable as it is dependent on the availability of water at the source.
- There have been reports that the water is contaminated and causes illness.

The standpipe systems are also used by livestock farmers who use them for watering their animals. However, there has been some congestion with the pipes leading to a reduction in supply. The government has closed down these pipes as they are not reliable sources of drinking water.
3.4.6 Access to Information and Technology

As of March 2016, mobile phone access stood at 3.5 million subscribers, meaning that there were 3.5 million active mobile simcards in use in Botswana[2]. There were also 1.4 million mobile phones in circulation with a penetration rate of 78%. The country has the highest number of users of mobile broadband internet as evidenced by the country ranking among the highest in Africa in terms of usage of social media, particularly Facebook[3]. The majority of women and men who have access to mobile phones are able to connect them to mobile phones (94 out of 186 respondents), which shows high access to this technology. Women have the same high rate of access as men to mobile phones.
