Some women and men in the project districts also have access to the radio and can read the Government newspaper (Daily News) which is delivered at the local administration (kgotla)where members of the community can collect the newspaper from Monday to Friday. The Daily News has some summary news in the local language, Setswana. Many women and men also receive news through mobile phones. In addition, the District Administration organises meetings where Officials address community meetings to present the programmes, the Chief sends his staff to notify the community through a loud speaker to tell them about the officials' visit. Nonetheless, those residing in remote areas are not always informed of these events as they are announced as the van circulates mostly in the main villages. These are important practices the project communications strategy will take into account as outreach methods are developed.

Both women and men in Nkata have requested an important service: radio. For example for this project to be successful, it is advised that the radio be done for some of the Government programmes. The announcements for the kgotla meetings do not reach everyone, which means women who cannot attend these meetings will not know about them. This implies they do not have equal access to information nor participation during the meetings. We were told that generally more women than men who attend kgotla meetings. This was proven by the fact that all the kgotla meetings in Nkata were attended only by women and not a single man was present at any of these meetings. The women share what they learnt at the kgotla with members of their family and friends. Women are therefore likely to be easy to reach through the kgotla system, in terms of delivering messages about the project, adopting new technologies and participating in planning and project implementation.

The level of education among women and men farmers is similar, and in some areas both have been trained by Veterinary Officers on how to vaccinate their livestock. In the case of abattoirs LMDG officers are providing an urgent training for radio communication and animal management organised by the Department of Animal Production for those applying for small stock and chickens[2]. Both women and men can therefore be trained on different skills in the project, which will increase their capacity to participate fully in the project. This is also true for the Botswana project. For example, one tailor in Tshane would like to be given the opportunity to produce the Escorings uniforms.

The equal access to mobile phones by both women and men, and access and use of internet by both genders within each community group means that they can be trained during the Escorings courses to utilise mobile phones to transmit data.


### Decision Making

In 2012, women accounted for just under a third (30.6%) of all decision-making positions within the four political parties. Of the 23 cabinet posts, (4.17 %) were held by women while there were also 4 women members of parliament out of 61 (6.5%). In 2019, only a fifth of members of the Council of Ministers are women and in 2018, only one woman was appointed as an authority such as district councils and traditional leadership have some of the lowest ratios of females to males, showing serious underrepresentation of women in these bodies. It is likely that


---
**Page 257**
---

the cultural stereotype that perceives women as incapable of holding leadership positions is a likely contributory factor to the status quo[1]. The unequal representation between women and men in the highest decision-making bodies continues. The bias against women, whether deliberate or not, is evident in Botswana's political landscape.


3.5.1 The Local Government System

Botswana has a two-tier system of government namely; the national government in the first tier, headed by the president with councils forming the second tier. Councils are headed by a mayor in urban areas and a chief in rural areas. There is also a third tier of local government known as the village administration which consists of traditional village leadership that has both a traditional and a development component. Tribal administration supports rural development initiatives, particularly at the community level. It comprises a council of elders who are responsible for the day-to-day running of their area under tribal secretary tradition. The traditional system of village leadership works cooperatively with the councils. Each village is headed by a chief or a mayor who is elected from among the members of his/her tribe in their area of jurisdiction. The tribal administration system is supported by a traditional arbitration forum now known as the Customary Court of Appeal, and by the Nito ya Dikgosi, which advises government on social matters. The main legislation is the Local Government Act 2012.


The Local Government Act establishes village development committees (VDCs) at ward and village level to assist in the implementation of the local development plan. It also provides for a principle of bottom-up planning where developments are to be discussed with communities before implementation. The bottom-up planning approach requires that communities are consulted on all government policies and programmes, including those related to the Local Government Act used to reach the community. Dikgosi (traditional leaders) play an crucial role in mobilising communities for consultation on all government policies and programmes at the local level. They also act as mediators between the village administration and the national government as national and local-level forums involving kgotla meetings and high-level consultative meetings. Each district administration is headed by a district commissioner appointed by the permanent secretary of the Ministry of Local Government and Rural Development. The District development, implementation and monitoring of their local district/urban development plan. Local authorities, headed by council secretaries/town clerks, and heads of departments for national government provide effective communication links between communities and national government[2].


The District Administration is headed by the District Commissioner, whose primary responsibility is to serve as a coordinator of district level planning and development. The Land Board is a

[2] http://www.civil.org.uk/default/assets/Five Country profiles/Botswana.pdf


---
**Page 258**
---

statutory body consisting of political members, responsible for land and housing with the assistance of the District Commissioner, Land Board Secretary and the Chief. Land boards administer communal land through customary law and hold the tribal land in trust and allocate it for residential, agricultural, industrial, commercial or general development purposes. The three inclusionary projects are located within the Kgalagadi District. The Village Development Committees which are responsible for the development of the District Development Plans that the local authorities are responsible for implementing. It is important to note that the project sites have been identified in the district councils such as the Department of Land Affairs, Botswana and District Commissioner in Kgalagadi land who are both supporting this project.

Several kgotla meetings were held in the three project districts to inform members of the community about the project as well to identify gender disaggregated challenges and climate change adaptation strategies. The meetings were chaired by the village headman and followed collective grazing. Protocol dictates that community members be informed of development projects through these meetings, but this is not always the case. In some kgotla meetings women had to be encouraged to speak during the kgotla meetings. Very few women spoke, nonetheless. This could be due to the entrenched patriarchal nature of the rural setting which has made women to be more reserved and less confident in speaking up. The lack of confidence that arises from this fact that poor women, and indeed men, feel self-conscious are and sometimes shouted down (one woman from Ngamiland mentioned this) when they try to make their opinions known during kgotla meetings. This is a reflection of the inequality between women and men's confidence over the years and it would take time for this inequality between the poor and the rich to be bridged.

Botswana is a patriarchal society and this is more evident in rural societies, for instance all the chief and subchiefs interviewed during this gender assessment are male. This means decision making at the village level is led by the chief (male) which in turn means decisions made at the kgotla are likely to favour men/men more than women. In one community engaged in Ngamiland, the village headman was a man and he was not willing to allow women to speak sometimes be taken to task if they say the wrong things, so it is views of men that are heard during kgotla meetings. The women were later engaged via the questionnaire.

Although women are allowed to speak, they are self-conscious in doing so as per the class division and gender inequality. This was evident from the meeting and may one woman spoke of discrimination against poor women and men. She believes they are discriminated against even when Government policies are supposed to be gender sensitive. She further stated that married women benefit more than unmarried ones. She further stated that there is no equality between women and men, men have more power and women have to ask permission to do certain things. This was also evident in the meeting held with the community on some parts of the country, to a normal, less heavy dress. Some men agree, others refuse.

### 3.5.2 Village Development Committees (VDCs)

A more positive picture emerged during the discussions that if anything, women outnumber men in the VDCs and they are very active members. Members of the (VDCs) are voted into the committee by the community. The maximum number of members of the committee is ten. This does not seem to be bias towards any gender and both women and men participate in the VDCs


---
**Page 259**
---

more or less in equal numbers. For example, Lepokule has 4 men and 6 women (the treasurer and secretary are both women), while Hurhurutere VDC has 3 men and 7 women. Tukohave has 6 women and 4 men; Tavhae VDC has 7 men and 3 men (the chair is female and while the deputy is male and the secretary and treasurer are both women). Chairs are ex-officio members of all the VDCs, and they are also the chairs of the local government. The VDCs are a new structure set up. VDCs with women chairs are an indication that women have more decision-making power in particular community/VDC. Also, as observed in some project areas, VDCs headed by retired men and women are more likely to be involved in projects related to the implementation of climate change impacts and the direction development projects should take.

The Chair of a VDC usually chairs kgota meetings, regardless of their gender. The VDC chairs, chaired all the kgota meetings addressed by the project during the gender assessment, while the Chief was not present at any of these meetings. At the end of each meeting, a project will be presented, and the VDC chair would moderate the question and answer session while the Chief wraps up by giving consent for the project to be implemented in his village.

VDCs are highly regarded in the villages and come second to the chief in terms of decision making. Women who are more educated, matriculated or have attended kgota, and mostly by men, are likely to take precedence over those agreed in VDCs.


As can be seen in sections above, at a household level, men generally have more decision-making powers when it comes to cattle, while women manage mainly the small stock. Because women of childbearing age are less represented on the Board of Directors, decisions in many cases will take precedence over small stock. The project must therefore ensure that women's voices are heard during the development of the planned Stewardship Agreements.


3.5.3 Community-Based Natural Resource Management

Most community-based organisations (CBOs) which manage and coordinates the CBNRM processes have Boards that meet with an average board size of 9. The gender balance of the Boards is also uneven, with 60% (SSB) of the Board members being male. The age distribution of board members has increased. The age distribution of board members is fairly even. However, youth (below 30 years old) are increasingly underrepresented. This trend that was observed in earlier reviews, may be due to the fact that young people are more interested in implementing projects or long-term sustainability CBOs need to attract more youth[26].

A good example of involving women in decision making with regard to management of communal natural resources is a community water agreement prepared and used by a syndicate in Tiahane. The syndicate was managed by a woman. The agreement is a constitution on how livestock belonging to members of the syndicate is watered.


3.5.4 Other CBOs

In all the three districts it was quite noticeable how very few men are involved in associations or groups of any type. More women said they were part of PTAs and other organisations such as

those working with the Red Cross in Tshane, and some said they were part of saving groups. The project must utilise the VDC as a conduit for equal participation of women and men in the project.

The drawn-out implementation of the decentralisation from central government to local government (see the ESIA Report) means that a top-bottom approach is still to be conceived to implement these poverty reduction development projects, which has an impact on the ability of the few women within the local government system to influence meaningful participation for women in rural and poor women's lives.

It was found, during consultations, that there are no women-focused organisations in the target areas, with the exception of existing savings groups (savings or mitehle) which were predominantly women members. The Botswana Village Development Committee has women representatives and occasionally address 'women issues'. Women and self-organised units are not represented on any of the local government structures at the village level (albeit short staffed), as well as from extension officers, social workers and veterinary officers.

### 3.6 GENDER BASED VIOLENCE

Gender based violence (GBV) is frequent in Botswana, with 67% of women having experienced some form of GBV in their lifetime[65]. Most gender violence that occurs in Botswana is domestic violence and the majority of victims are young women who have experienced IPV in their lives. There has also been a stark rise in the number of "passion killings" where men kill their female partners after an argument or disagreement to leave the relationship. On average, younger women, aged 18-44, are more likely to experience IPV compared to older women aged 45 years and older[66]. In total, less than 1% of the GBV (including sexual violence) cases reported in Botswana are prosecuted. When women and men consulted, many attributed the GBV to also be a perverse outcome of women's increased empowerment and autonomy in recent years disrupting patriarchal systems, with men feeling left behind or embittered by the progress towards equality despite women still

[65] Available at: https://newsroom.un.org/docs/default-source/1943-magazine/report-230-bolivia-pg-faithcenter-may-2019.pdf?sfvrsn=8c7f2ebe_2
[66] Available at: http://www.bw-uohp.org/content/botswana/en/home/library/democratic_governance/gender-based-violence-indicators-study/research.html


---
**Page 261**
---

being formally and customarily discriminated against in many ways). Income from ilegenging,
despite being a low amount and often (almost always) going to household spending, was cited in consultations as a "trigger" for GBV.

This baseline of prevalent GBV is further exacerbated by climate change[68], as the social and
financial stresses due to climate change and weather-related disasters heighten and intensify gender inequalities and gender-based violence. As environmental degradation and ecosystem
stress increase, women's access to livelihoods and resources can be reduced, leading to increased
anger, social isolation alcoholism and substance abuse, which are known triggers for increased GBV[69]. Women who are unable to cope with these stresses are said to be very low or they are hidden. However, of those reported, women note being abused by their partners who claim that their ilegenging earnings and use it for alcohol.

Consultations revealed that sexual exploitation increases following climate-related disasters,
when households are forced to sell the insecurity of the insects to vulnerable groups. Boys and
girls may resort to extreme coping mechanisms, including transactional sex, to support families.
Climate change-driven physical migration or displacement exposes women and girls to increased GBV risk[70]. Women who have been displaced should be encouraged to stay with men and girls to travel farther from home to collect resources, putting them at greater risk of GBV.

Climate-driven impacts can have many knock-on health impacts that disproportionately affect
women, such as missed HIV treatment medications and experiencing reduced access to maternal health. Additionally, common coping mechanisms to climate-driven stressors include increased workload for women in the household, which may lead to less time available to generate income. In nearby Malawi, an alarming increase in child brides was recorded following Cyclone Idai[71]. In Zimbabwe, girls are increasingly being forced out of school or dropping out
of school to help families obtain food, care for siblings, or earn money. In neighbouring Zambia,
for example, school drop-out rates are so high in drought-affected districts that dozens of primary schools have closed down due to lack of students[72]. The UNICEF report also notes a significant increase of worldwide GBV related to the COVID-19 pandemic[73]. While cases and deaths are still relatively small in Botswana[74], it is projected that COVID-19 will intensify GBV in the country.

Given the already high rates of GBV in Botswana, combined with high climate change
vulnerability and COVID-19 impacts, women are among the most vulnerable members in the
proposed climate adaptation measures. Thus, the necessity to prioritize gender-responsive action throughout project design.

In moving forward, projects can ensure that policies, national documents, and awareness-raising materials include sections that provide information about the nexus between climate change and


---
**Page 262**
---

gender, including GBV. These sections should include, for example, information of societal gender roles, gendered divisions of labour, and data on the prevalence of gender inequality and GBV**. Although comprehensively addressing GBV is outside of the project outcomes, the project does intend to empower women through the continued pplegaming program and the many activities that are part of it. In order to ensure that the empowerment of women is not undermined in such, the project aims take proactive steps to ensure that GBV is not adversely exacerbated as a result of interventions. This will include partnered empowerment, working with husbands and male community members to address gender norms and attitudes.

Mechanism will also specifically include considerations for capturing and responding to reports of GBV. The mechanism will be designed in such a way that it is accessible to all women and men both to ensure that women have full access to the GRM, and to clearly show project-related GBV as something the GRM is meant to capture and is accessible to them. During project monitoring, the project will also collect data on the number of GBV incidents reported through the mechanism questions like “have you heard of any GBV incidents resulting from the project” during community visits.

Parentral attitudes and societal norms are a primary driver of the incidence of GBV in Bosnia. While women and men affirm gender equality in the public domain this has not translated to their private lives particularly in their intimate relationships**. According to the Tisane chief, incidences of GBV are quite low in his village. i.e. low numbers of rape, and beating of women. However, he says that there have been cases where young men have raped women in the past, and the problem was that the male and female misinterpreted the laws that govern the charge of rape. The Tisane chief said that in such cases it is always a young man reporting to the police and the police allows the man to take away a baby that they have conceived. However, the chief has in the past mediated in these cases by explaining the laws to the couple and ensuring the young man returns the baby to the mother.

In addition to providing information and challenging social norms that harm both women and men, projects must further do so by knowledge building via adding gender-disgregated data collection outcomes that link GBV and climate change exposure and vulnerability. This will make visible women’s disproportionate vulnerability to GBV and climate change impacts, further highlighting the need for action.

Projects should focus on engaging men and women in discussions around GBV – the root causes and the routes to eradicating it—in order to achieve a peaceful, sustainable social setting in which people are able to adapt to upcoming climate impacts. At a national level, there are currently no laws that protect women from GBV. However, there is an initiative called “Cooking in the Kitchen run by Men and Boys for Gender Equality (MIGE), which engages boys in traditionally female-assigned labour roles and assigns older males as mentors. It was found during consultations that older men in the proposed project areas are currently not stable


---
**Page 263**
---

mentors, given their substance abuse or general overexertion and stress – often from crop and livestock losses due to drought and other climate-related impacts.

Similarly, Men and Boys for Gender Equality (MBUGE) has conducted Kigita meetings in Hwahawale, Mabvuto, and Chikwanda Districts which served as a forum of community engagement where the members were encouraged to take action to challenge gender based violence, promote gender equality, educate and promote children’s rights and preventing HIV. These initiatives have been successful in creating awareness on GBV and promoting positive change.

Finally, fatherhood programmes, such as Mabvuto, have had an impact on reducing child and carer-giving responsibilities when noting that HIV-positive most prevalent form of GBV is Botswana – linked to intergenerational violence, in which 26% of men have witnessed their mothers being abused75. In Tshokwe, some young mothers complained of being abandoned by the father of her child, and in Mabvuto, many women cited lack of money as the reason for not helping to support their children. Through engaging men in traditionally female tasks, projects may ease women’s double burden of caring and income-generating activities.

In line with this, focusing project efforts on climate-resilient practices that reduce women's and girl's time spent collecting water and food resources. This, in turn, will reduce their exposure to GBV. Such measures in an agricultural setting, for example, may include a focus on sustainable land management, improved livestock care, and income-generating activities, as well as empowering women’s initiatives that are already engaging in climate-resilient practices such as savings groups or cooperatives where they are not present or are lacking.

These aforementioned measures align with government-led NDC and SDG focal areas of: promoting gender equality and the empowerment of women in all areas of governance; developing policies and conducting effective campaigns for eradicating gender violence, HIV and AIDS; and building the capacity of women and men to engage critically in democratic processes that advance gender equality.

### 3.7 INTERGENERATIONAL VULNERABILITY

The context sections above described the varying opportunities and discriminations subject to married, widowed or divorced women under legal and customary authority systems in Botswana at large. In addition, women who are single (widowed or divorced) and/or childless (who specifically lack the social safeguards of male partners or dual incomes), this does create better baseline resilience and independence by necessity, albeit in a way that is highly strained and insecure. For example, while married women may be more likely to have access to land for men, single women would have more control and autonomy in household decision-making and spending than married women and may own their house/land/cornfield or livestock or smallstock. However, they do tend to be poorer and also more likely to engage in sex work than married


---
**Page 264**
---

women, although such cases do occur, especially if the husband is living/working remotely from the woman. These single women are also less exposed to instances of IPV in the event of their empowerment, and may offer a strategic entry point for empowering vulnerable beneficiaries without such perverse outcomes, at least in early stages when the success and opportunities of the project are demonstrated. Once the benefits of the project are visible, willingness and participation in the project can be demonstrated, married women may feel more confident in taking part, or their husbands/partners may see the household benefits to be had.

On the other hand, older (middle aged) women are more likely to be widows, and have many dependents. Even with employment and social grants, which some of these older women receive pensions, this amount is not necessarily sufficient to sustain a large household of dependents. During consultations, older women were often quieter or slower to speak/contribute, showing more deference to men and critiquing the young women who dominated discussion in the focus groups.

# WOMEN AND MEN'S DIFFERENTIAL EXPERIENCES OF CLIMATE CHANGE

Agricultural-based livelihoods and forms of diversification are associated with particular gender roles. Climate change in drylands is shifting the nature of responsibility sharing and cooperation in the household, which can have negative consequences for women's well-being. Changing rainfall patterns are contributing to ecosystem degradation, poor animal condition, disease outbreaks, and market exclusion. These factors create a vicious cycle of degradation, increased livestock vulnerability, reduced agricultural security, and food insecurity, all of which are livelihoods for the most vulnerable populations in Botswana living in last-mile communities. As in other contexts, these environments highlight inter- and intra-gender differences in how men and women are affected, and how they adapt.

Under increased ecosystem degradation, poor animal conditions and disease outbreaks, women (and poor, single women in particular) in the target districts tend to carry a higher burden of impact, as they are more likely to be responsible for livestock management. Women have limited options to pursue alternative livelihoods through migration, due to care responsibilities and other gender constraints on mobility, personal security/safety, and autonomy; iii) a patriarchal society that limits women's access to land, credit, education, and employment in secondary industries or care work, that leads to diversions of household resources (financial, or in-kind like water) towards supplementing losses in male-dominated activities/livelihoods; iv) supply chain disruptions due to climate change, which can lead to increased costs for women who create additional time burdens and may expose women and girls to safety risks (in the event of traveling long distances); v) the provision of land protection for secondary industry and/or care responsibilities (as a help in the case of preterm birth).

The ASSAR research highlighted gender as one of many social factors influencing responses to climate change. For instance, gender, age and marital status interact to shape how households manage risks, as described above. As a result of frequent droughts, as well as the human and wildlife competition for water resources, women are more likely than men to adopt the following ways to adapt and survive the negative impact of climate change on their livelihoods.

## HOW WOMEN ARE IMPACTED BY, AND ADAPT TO DROUGHT

### Livelihoods

Consultations revealed that, regardless of status (married, single, FHH, MHH), women were largely responsible for the finances and management of the household and dependents if present. Women's responsibilities in the household are often increased by climate change, which reduced women's income or poor performance of crops/stocksmalls, women described self-sacrifice or taking on additional burdens to continue to provide for the household's needs. Meanwhile, men were more likely than women to migrate away from their homes and dispersed across the household, with women carrying additional burdens in that case as well.

Some women depend on Government programmes such as belegging and the food Basket, both of which are government poverty alleviation programmes, while others receive remittances from


---
**Page 266**
---

relatives for food, clothing and school fees. Consultations showed that when men are more mobile or socially enabled to transfer to new livelihoods, women tended to have fewer opportunities as such, and more often ended up relying on social protection schemes. Some (often poorer/FHR) women use donkeys for draught power as they cannot afford diesel for tractors. The government has introduced a ploughing assistance scheme where farmers can hire tractor owner (after the crops have germinated) as part of the ploughing assistance scheme. Those that use donkeys cannot benefit from the government ploughing assistance programme, as the requirement is to own a tractor and meet established standards. This tends to impact poorer men and women, as well as single parents or widows.

Women say they are more likely to dispose of their assets, for instance sell all livestock, and borhole (using the children's inheritance). This is done with the intention to restock when conditions improve. The animals sold are usually donkeys, goats, sheep and cattle that have recovered to restock. Goats generally do not have a market, except within Poverty Eradication and LMD projects where they can be sold for cash or in-kind. The demand for livestock is high as the supply outstrips the demand. Additionally, goat meat is said to be expensive, and cannot be eaten everyday due to its strong taste, unlike beef, hence the slow sales. However, many people consume milk. Some farmers have large numbers of goats and cannot offload due lack of market access.

Some women are said to migrate in search of employment, while some report to sex work as a means survival, which exposes them to various risks. Women in semi-arid regions of Africa are not lacking in skills or capacity to earn income; however, the type of support they receive from an agency is directed towards short-term survival and coping[8]. The challenges presented by frequent droughts in the project districts have made many able bodied women to turn to the main source of their livelihoods, which is livestock. This has led to a shift away from agriculture being intensive, and is seen as demeaning by others. Some women are said to turn to sex work to earn income. Since women need to support their families, they tend to overlook some of the negative issues associated with this.

Respondents mentioned that the hardships brought about by loss of livelihoods from livestock has led to some men deserting their families in search of work and sometimes never coming back. Some women also migrate looking for work while others turn to sex work to make ends meet. The additional burden of providing food, clothing and school fees for children, as well as care work, including looking after children, means that women, more than men turn to belegging as their main source of income. The additional burden of providing food and other necessities for the family is also a major challenge. Some women have reported that they are no longer able say ploughing fields where they used to regularly produce crops for the households are now lying fallow.

If household income is reduced, such that mothers can no longer afford school or creche childcare, the mothers will either stay home with the children (missing work, or duties outside of the home) or transfer the burden of care to elderly relatives. The project team observed young children being brought with to Kigali meetings, by older family members, for instance.

If household income is reduced, such that mothers can no longer afford school or creche childcare, the mothers will either stay home with the children (missing work, or duties outside of the home) or transfer the burden of care to elderly relatives. The project team observed young children being brought with to Kigali meetings, by older family members, for instance.

A project such as the GCF Botswana project will support safe and remunerative livelihood diversification, thus making the women agents of longer-term adaptation in the three districts.

### 4.1.2 Livestock

Women in Bobirwa said they utilise their indigenous knowledge to estimate when goats are about to give birth, and keep and feed them when the kids until they do so. The same is done with new born calves. In some cases a number of mothers are fed by other women who supplement feed from their own resources i.e. those who can afford to do so. Sometimes there is no market for livestock due to the Foot and Mouth Disease outbreak.

Women in Sehlatwa have migrated some goats to the nearby forests and purchase water from boreholes which is unaffordable. Women in the Kgasapadi are participating in the Department of Agriculture research project that produces livestock feed by grinding acacia pods.

### 4.1.3 Water

Livestock is watered from boreholes. Women, as well as men farmers, have dug wells on river beds for the livestock. Lesoko (a hand dug well) is made by both women and men on the members of the community. The women are responsible for digging the well and request from neighbours when the hand-dug borehole runs dry. Many goats are watered from possible water from household or public standpipes in all the three districts. Some pay monthly rent for access to a standpipe. In some cases, they have dug their own wells. If they had resources they would drill their own boreholes, fence, fodder and keep livestock in the collective farm. The women are responsible for the maintenance of the wells and men syndicate have received assistance from the Government to reticulate water for the benefit of their livestock.

### 4.2 How Men Are Impacted by, and Adapt to Drought

#### 4.2.1 Livestock

Men do the following to ensure that their livestock survives until the next the drought has passed:

- A few men particularly in Bobirwa say they have relocated their livestock to better rangelands.
- One farmer in Bobirwa, out of desperation, mobilised his livestock to an area within a reserve, but for security reasons he had to move them back. The same happened with Zimbabwean farmers/herders who are said to steal cattle.
- Understandably, men have reduced their herd and fodder production such as fabab. Some use crop residue and natural vegetation to supplement feed.
- Reduce stocking capacity through sale of non-productive animals (destocking). This is not the preferred option.
- Resort to small stock production.
- Those with vegetable gardens patrol all day and night to keep elephants away, since the elephants come into the village.
- Grow drought resistant crops with early maturity.

#### 4.2.2 Water

To adapt to water scarcity for the livestock the men:


---
**Page 268**
---

* Use boreholes which are used by both households and livestock which can create risk of disease. The minerals from the groundwater (from the boreholes) are said to cause diarrhea in some cases.
* Others dig temporary wells in rivers for households and livestock — these are sometimes washed away after heavy rains.
* They also collect rainwater in JoJo tanks that were received from a government programme for backyard gardens. When water is low, they fill up the JoJo tanks and take them to the cottages where there is no borehole or river nearby. The use of a pump connected to the borehole increases the water pressure for the whole village.
* In Botswana during drought some migrate livestock or take water out of the river using pumps.
* Some buy water from borewell owners.
* Those with financial resources buy water from bowser owners.

# CONCLUSIONS

This gender analysis was conducted to assess implications of the changing climate, particularly with regard to frequent drought on women and men’s current roles in households, access to, and control over resources as well as their ability to make decisions in the households and communities within the project sites. The patriarchal nature of Botswanaan society has led to restrictions on women's ability to make decisions regarding land ownership, use of land and assistance to access to improved livelihood opportunities. It has also resulted in widespread gender based violence (GBV).

Discussions during the gender assessment indicate that women are more vulnerable to climate change as a result of their gender roles - which is highly focussed on looking after children and other family members while they depend on subsistence farming for meeting household's needs. Frequent droughts which have led to poor crop harvests have in turn led to food insecurity and frequent hunger spells among the households. The average number of days spent in fetching water in many of the villages, pays - USS 50 per month and at times, with payment delays. As crops fail due to drought, children can suffer from malnutrition. For instance, women in Botswana have indicated that their children currently do not have enough to eat and rely on school meals during term.

Yield products (wild fruit and herbs) that some women and men used to harvest and sell have also become scarcer. Similarly, the mopane caterpillar, which is a source of protein and income for many households in Botswana has been greatly affected by climate change. The caterpillar is affected by poor rains, which have led to the tree species (mopane trees) that carry the caterpillar becoming unproductive. Additional stresses for women can also be seen in increased workloads for those who own goats, as they have to collect additional water for the goats from public standpipes.

As men rely a great deal on cattle production as a means of generating income, they are exposed to loss of large numbers of cattle, particularly in the last three years due to reduced communal grazing land. The number of cattle owned by women has also decreased over time while cattle are greatly affected by the reduced grazing land while women who mainly manage small stock such as goats may not be as negatively impacted as goats are browsers. Men's incomes from cattle production have been greatly affected and this has led to increased dependency on cattle in cattle production in the form of supplementary feed. Spending on supplementary feed and water for the cattle at the expense of the family is said to lead to potential conflict (including GBV) in the household, particularly when there are limited resources available. For instance, to support the family with groceries. The impact of climate change, resulting from loss of cattle and reduced income has also led to increased alcohol consumption which are also considered to be broad winners in their families to become vulnerable, leading to mental health issues such as stress and anxiety. This can cause alcohol-related issues as well as GBV.

Data and information collected during the gender assessment shows that both gender groups are struggling to adapt to the frequent droughts. They have lost some or all of their cattle due to lack of grazing land and water. In addition, over the last five years elephants have invaded communities in Botswana (Botwana district) and Ngamiland as they look for water, eating and destroying crops and sometimes causing injuries and even death during the invasion. Livestock predators have also encroached communities and can mar or

## Women are more likely to be vulnerable to loss of livestock

Women are more likely than men to be affected by drought and disease because they are more likely to be employed in livestock keeping, from morning until noon or 1pm, and are also likely to be vulnerable to loss of livestock as they are unable to take care of the rest of their families. Women are also more vulnerable to HIV/AIDS and other diseases that affect women more than men.

### Discussion

Discussions revealed that the increase in heat due to increases in temperatures and heat waves have led to more mosquitoes and an increase in malaria incidences, even out of malaria season. The high temperatures have also led to some skin-related medical issues and death from heat stroke. Those working in ppleging sites say they are affected the most by the heat as they walk home after feeding cattle or to their cattle pens at kraals in the afternoon. Some say the year 2019 has been the worst.

### Women's Vulnerability

Women are more likely than men to be employed in livestock keeping, from morning until noon or 1pm, and are also likely to be vulnerable to loss of livestock as they are unable to take care of the rest of their families. Women are also more vulnerable to HIV/AIDS and other diseases that affect women more than men.

### Impact on Livelihoods

Many women in rural areas do not own productive land and rely on ploughing fields (when the rains are good) to produce crops. Some women in the project districts have suggested that they should be given land by Government to grow crops, as this would help them earn a living and provide food for their families.

### Women's Resilience

Women tend to be poorer, but were also notably more independent by necessity and so more resilient to baseline challenges but not climate change. Women are also more vulnerable to droughts and other climate change impacts such as drought as they have added responsibilities for feeding their families. Culturally women are responsible for taking care of the cattle and other livestock while men go out to work in the fields, and because of this they are more likely to be affected by drought.

### Government's Role

The proposed project provides a lifeline for many of the poor women in the project sites who will have the opportunity to earn an income from the sale of milk, eggs, and meat from their livestock. # GENDER ACTION PLAN: ECOSYSTEM AND LIVELIHOODS RESILIENCE / CLIMATE CHANGE RISK REDUCTION THROUGH ECOSYSTEM-BASED ADAPTATION IN BOTSWANA'S COMMUNAL GRAZING LANDS

The project will restore at least 4.6 million ha of degraded land, directly benefit 160,000 people (85% of the total population below the poverty line) and will decrease emissions from the country's livestock sector by three-quarters by at least 2030. Transformed livelihoods in rural youth and women will be created through the integration of training with financial literacy and professional qualifications. Additionally, women’s livelihoods will benefit from a continued supply of quality seeds, improved access to markets for their produce (e.g., fuelwood and natural products [medicines, mopane worms and other local foods]). Men will benefit from support as the project removes some of the responsibility and burden of livestock production. Women will also receive training to reduce stress related to losing their main form of income/livelihoods.

The project will be implemented across the communal lands of three areas: Ngamiland, Kgalagadi, and Bobirwa and over three overlapping but distinct phases. In the foundational phase, the focus is on land restoration and rehabilitation (land preparation and seedling-based on their 'readiness'). Simultaneously, community level facilitation engagement for replication will begin for priority sites to prepare for rangeland rehabilitation roll-out in priority communities, and a third project phase will be implemented.

The main aim of the gender action plan is to present the challenges, constraints and opportunities for women and men identified during the gender analysis into operational action. Adaptation actions reduce vulnerability and increase resilience to current and projected climate risks at the national level. However, lack of access to resources, information, education, and power constrains many people's ability to take action on climate change, with different constraints and impacts on women and men*. The G4P gender-responsive actions/activities that will be implemented in this project are designed to ensure they benefit from the project. The gender performance indicators are linked to the Project logframe.

## 6.1 GENDER ISSUES

The following are key gender issues and considerations identified in project areas that should inform activity selection and implementation.

### Collective Grazing
- a) Customary marriage laws which have negative consequences on women's right to inheritance and unequal decision-making power between husband and wife with regard to cattle.
- b) Low levels of education and high levels of poverty for women and men, and reliance on pledging, for women in particular.
- c) Lack of or very low cash incomes.

* Project Funding Proposed to GCF
* European Capacity Building Initiative. 2017. "Pocket Guide to Gender Equality". Updated January 2018

52


---
**Page 273**
---

d) Poor or lack of markets for livestock (cattle and small stock), including goats
e) Increased burden on women as a result of additional water collection for goats and work in telepigweeding while taking care of livestock and households.
f) Human-wildlife conflict impacting on women's and men's livelihoods.

Eco-rangers and Restoration Workers Training
a) Young, single mothers' ability to travel and stay away from home and/or children. In some communities, the mother is not allowed to leave after the children when the mother is not available, including taking the child to the clinic for scheduled visits.
b) Potential loss of income for women who are employed during implementation – these could include culinary training. Some women in the project areas have received tenders from government to supply telepigweeding workers with meals while they are on duty.
c) Lack of collateral for women to benefit from potential opportunities that could be created by the project
d) Low levels of income to invest in businesses for FFRs

Gender/social norms
a) The gender dynamics at the community planning level may be the reason that only a small number of women speak during consultations at kgotla meetings. It is suggested that women who are not involved in decision-making processes and who do not have access to information on women's issues to table during project implementation. This means that instead of asking for gender equality, it is more likely that women will be asked to be more active as women leaders and mobilise and build their capacity to speak up forward women's issues (e.g., a woman who has been involved in the project and is now a leader). The project may be implemented which will integrate gender equitable and/or female targeted leadership.
b) Women spoke freely during the focus group discussions that were held afterwards. Most of the challenges that were raised by women, including those who are not involved with the kgotla meetings, came from the men. The kgotla systems although meant to be fair, where mafikgo a tsebele is supposed to be used to resolve disputes, it was noted that in most cases, almost 90% of them will stand up to speak about their challenges and needs and suggest what should be done about a certain issue. In contrast, very few women speak in this set up, which means that the kgotla system is not gender equitable. The project may be implemented ideas all of the women in that community and its associated settlements/livelihoods.
c) Frequent droughts which have led to loss of livelihoods has led some women to turn to sex work, and others to migrate for seasonal work. This has also led to increased risks, including GBV
d) Mental health issues, sometimes leading to OIVB and alcohol consumption, by men as a result of loss of livelihood, i.e. cattle.
e) The intersections of gender with income/wealth, age and marital status continue to inform women's vulnerability (and resilience) exposure to, and adaptation to, climate change. The project needs to consider these different groups, and how to target these segments.
f) Relative empowerment of women, leading to increased instances of GBV from partners and husbands, due to perceptions of relative deprivation, 'emasculation' and challenging gender norms.

It is proposed that all gender training include awareness raising/training (specific to agriculture and livelihoods) on existing gender policies such as the National Gender Development Policy

53


---
**Page 274**
---

and the Abolition of the Marital Power Act 2004, as well as the Ministry of Agriculture and Food Security’s draft gender strategy. Trainers should also be made aware of the inclusion on women, widows and youth in the Botswana Land Policy of 2019. Furthermore, the gender training should comprise a module on the importance of addressing gender equality issues in relation to other social issues such as women and men’s livestock decision making and control, in relation to cultural and religious practices, and how these can be addressed through the FTT. The material intended for the communities will be translated into the local languages of the three districts.

The project also aims to take proactive steps to ensure that GBV is not adversely exacerbated as a result of interventions. This will include partner empowerment, working with husbands and male community members on gender equality issues, and ensuring that the FTT Mechanism (see Annex 8) will also specifically include considerations for effectively capturing information about GBV incidents. The Gender Officer will be responsible for ensuring that data is shared widely in places where women get information, clearly showing that project-related GBV as something that can happen to any woman, not just those who are involved with the FTT within each cluster and working with the FTT will be encouraged to maintain vigilance and report any concerns. The Gender Officer will also be responsible for ensuring regular monitoring regarding GBV – for example informally asking a few questions like “have you heard of any GBV incidents resulting from the project” during community visits.

The project will employ a full-time Gender Officer who will be based within the Project Management Unit. This role will be supervised by the Project Planning and Adaptive Management Director to ensure gender targets and considerations related to gender are integrated into all aspects of the project, including planning, implementation, monitoring and evaluation. The Gender Officer will also work closely with district level officers and cluster level graduate monitors through training, data collection and GAF monitoring. The proposed salary of the Gender Officer will be with the Government D-scale. Responsibilities include:

* Incorporation of gender aspects in stakeholder surveys/consultations in collaboration with the staff/contrastants responsible for surveys and consultations.
* Validation of project design documents to ensure gender considerations are included.
* Regular collection of sex-disaggregated data (both quantitative and qualitative) for monitoring of gender-related indicators.
* Advising and training for project staff, gender focused graduate monitors, and key stakeholder groups on gender aspects across all project activities.
* Implementation of a GBV risk assessment in collaboration with the Project Management Unit and cluster-level gender issue-focused interns.

Disrupting the traditionally and predominantly male-dominated livestock sector to build gender-inclusive climate resiliency must be strategically done, so as to empower support and buy-in from both men and women. The project will need to address the potential for increased levels of relative deprivation in the event of women’s empowerment. The gender action plan describes several pragmatic strategies to do: i) specifically target young, unemployed single/single parent women; ii) work with existing community groups (e.g., youth groups, women’s groups and couples); together, iii) support community ‘role models’ for men and women; iv) encourage participation by reducing women’s already high care burdens; and v) work to address drivers and constraints on women’s empowerment. The Gender Officer will be responsible for leading strategic communications about the distribution of project benefits (which do, by the nature of the sector, still favour men)

* Adapted from the Lao GCF SAP

### Component 3: Promoting climate-sensitive enterprise development and value-chain innovations to sustain transformational change (AT)
**Impact Statement:** Increased livelihoods of vulnerable women and poor farmers to mitigate impacts of climate change as well as improved livelihoods of PWDs, youth and in communities facing the effects of climate change.
**Outcome Statement:** Improved livelihoods for poor females and male farmers and broader population in the Project Areas

| Activities | Indicators and Targets | Timeline | Responsibilities | Budget |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Activity 3.1: Market readiness training, enterprise development support** | - Identify women and/or enterprises or small businesses that are interested in participating in the program. - Develop a business plan for each participant to be part of the Farmer Learning Network (FLN). - Connect women or particular with market leaders and the Cocoa Board to ensure access to markets. - Support poor women to apply for Government poverty alleviation schemes. - Provide training on financial management, record keeping and bookkeeping. - Prepare a training workshop for non-farmer private sector players that demonstrate the importance of working with women | - supply chain facilitators, and local level funds build the enabling conditions for new emission-intense livelihood options. 7 APPENDICES

7.1 DISCUSSIONS WITH STAKEHOLDERS

The first FGDs were conducted during the scoping mission in August and September 2019 where a combination of government officials, farmers and representatives from Village Development Committees were invited to workshops. A total of 54 women and 86 men participated in the collective workshops conducted during the scoping mission in Bobone (Bobonaro and Viqueque Phikoe), Ngatamid (Mauin and Gumein), and Kigalaiqi (Kong and Hukuntali).

Women and men attended at the kgofla and other stakeholders consultations held by the project were as follows:
a) Ngatamid: Narangai (40 women and 15 men); Sebitiswa (60 women and 25 men), Etsaha (13 [14 women and 8 men]);
b) Bobone: Lekpokole (101 women and just over 20 men), Lekpokole (37 women and 9 men); Tshokwe (36 women and 18 men);
c) Kigalaiqi: Huchuluwe (59 women and 28 men), Tshandu (58 women and 16 men), Zutswaha (31 women).
